Sumi raised an eyebrow. “I can promote it under carrier reconstruction. The checksum damage qualifies.”

“Do that. Preserve the original priority decision first.”

Sumi copied the queue state into the frozen cache. Then she assigned the fragment to carrier reconstruction. The action did not change its scientific status. It only gave technicians permission to determine what light had reached Nacre.

Anja watched the custody proofs build. One copy held the damaged fragment. Another held Meridian’s legal-effect score. A third linked the score to the completed Crown quorum and its simulated destination condition.

Unarrived testimony did not refer only to a council’s future answer. Evidence itself could remain outside a decision’s past light cone. No model could make Nadi’s actual observation present before transmission. No administrative label could erase the interval.

The sequence freeze accepted the new identifiers. Its protected cache copied them to three hull nodes, the same slow multiplication Anja had watched on Day 0.

At 10:42 Nacre source, fragment four entered the frozen record.

On the navigation display across the well, Crown alignment continued through the place where Mote’s broken sentence had not yet arrived.

Chapter 05 — Shield Mass

The shield coupon broke seventeen seconds before Tomas Ren’s model said it would.

He killed the load arm and caught the two halves against the test cage before they struck the wall. One piece had split along the printed grain. The other carried a bright diagonal scar where the alloy’s internal lattice had yielded under heat.

“Again,” said Ilya from the control niche.

“No.” Tomas turned the fragment under the inspection light. “Again would tell us whether we can reproduce a bad assumption. We already know we can.”

The drive-model room shared a bulkhead with Nacre’s forward shield lockers. Its floor vibrated with attitude thrusters every few minutes as Crown alignment tightened. Test rigs occupied one side. The other held a projection volume large enough to display the convoy from Vela to Ardent, though at present it showed only a magnified section of sacrificial shield laminate.

Ilya unlatched the test cage. “Coupon four held within margin.”

“Coupon four came from a different casting bay.”

“Same batch specification.”

“Specifications do not absorb particles.”

Tomas placed both halves in evidence sleeves and labeled the source grain. Crown operations had asked him to certify that Nacre’s forward shielding could tolerate the preparatory attitude without replacement. The test suggested yes, but not for the reason the command table assumed. Nacre’s shield had grown less uniform through years of patching. Some sections carried excess mass. Others depended on geometry rather than thickness.

Averages hid usable danger.

He opened the Crown tolerance sheet. Vela’s model treated each habitat shield as a single areal density with a conservative degradation factor. The simplification made fleet calculations fast enough for live scheduling. It also erased where the mass sat and whether crews could move it.

Palisade’s entry drew his attention.

The shielding habitat carried fabrication reserves above convoy minimum. Its forward arc had been designed to protect foundries during high-output years, then reinforced twice after micrometeoroid seasons. Vela counted the extra material as untouchable protection.

Tomas checked the underlying mass ledger.

The ledger separated fixed hull layers, movable reserve plates, raw feedstock, and obsolete storm baffles. Nearly a sixth of Palisade’s listed shielding sat in detachable reserve assemblies. Crown planning excluded those assemblies from maneuver mass because removing them reduced arrival protection.

Excluded did not mean useless.

He transferred the reserve figure into a private scratch model and gave it velocity.

Mass could shield. Mass could also brake.

The statement contained no discovery. Every drive apprentice learned momentum before they learned shield maintenance. A plate released into a prepared magnetic stream could widen plasma coupling. Vaporized feedstock could seed a brake field. The convoy already intended to spend mirror mass near Corva. Palisade’s reserve simply occupied a category that prevented drive tables from asking what else it might do.

Tomas ran one crude branch.

Palisade retained minimum forward protection, released detachable reserve, and accepted reduced fabrication shielding during the approach. The branch improved distributed coupling by seven percent across three habitats. Arrival error widened beyond current settlement tolerance. Radiation exposure rose aboard Palisade. The result did not offer an alternative. It offered a reason not to discard the idea before measuring it.

He saved the branch without a title.

Ilya looked over his shoulder. “That is shield stock.”

“Yes.”

“You put it in the brake column.”

“I copied it into a scratch variable.”

“That sentence will comfort the shield crews.”

“They should not be comforted. They should tell me which assemblies can actually detach.”

“None, if you ask during Crown readiness.”

“Then I will not ask yet.”

Ilya carried the broken coupons to microscopy. Tomas returned to Nacre’s tolerance audit. Captain Osei had ordered him to identify every Crown dependency that became costly to reverse. Anja Kest had given him access to sequence metadata and nothing else. Her message contained a careful instruction: mark physical commitment, not legal importance.

He appreciated the boundary. Auditors who wanted engineering to prove morality usually disliked the cost curves.

The first dependency concerned attitude. Nacre could reverse its current rotation in fourteen minutes at modest propellant cost. After Vela released mirror seeds, reversal would leave Nacre outside the planned coupling geometry for eleven hours. The ship could recover, but the other habitats would need to absorb guidance error.

The second concerned power. Garden light reductions remained recoverable for thirty-six hours. Beyond that, bean and algae yields lost more energy than the Crown reserve saved.

The third concerned mass designation. Once Palisade and Nacre marked specific reserves for central use, other ships could decommit local backups. No plate moved at designation, yet reliance spread through the convoy.

Tomas marked each threshold with a source clock and an uncertainty band. He refused Meridian’s master-time display. Physical dependency followed signal receipt. Vela might issue an order at one time, Nacre receive it later, and Palisade act on a different schedule entirely. A commitment graph had to show who knew what before changing mass.

His first graph looked like spilled wire.

At midshift, Chief Kado from the shield lockers entered the model room. She carried the casualty sleeve for coupon five and set it on his table.

“You tested an unannealed repair strip,” Kado said.

“The batch record marks final anneal complete.”

“The batch record belongs to the base plate. That diagonal patch came from an emergency print after the last storm.”

“Was it inspected?”

“By pulse image. Not destructive load.”

“Then the Crown sheet assumes strength it does not have.”

“The patch does not need coupon strength. Its location sees less flux at prepared attitude.”

Tomas opened the hull map. Kado pointed to a section near a radiator shadow. At current orientation, the repair strip remained protected. During the second Crown rotation, the shadow moved.

“How long?” he asked.

“Twenty-seven minutes of increased exposure.”

“Why does the tolerance sheet average it?”

“Because Vela asked for total margin, not patch geography.”

“They are rotating patch geography.”

Kado crossed her arms. “Now you understand why shield crews dislike drive physicists.”

“I thought it concerned our personalities.”

“That saves time.”

Together they built a local exposure trace. The repair strip could survive twenty-seven minutes if adjacent plates stayed below temperature limits. Those plates also cooled the detachable reserve lockers. Crown preparation had borrowed cooling from the shield bay during yesterday’s thermal imbalance, leaving less margin than Vela’s table assumed.

Tomas added the current local state. The safe interval shrank to nineteen minutes.

“Can we move the patch?” he asked.

“Not before alignment.”

“Can we alter attitude by one hundredth of a degree?”

“Navigation will object.”

“Navigation always objects before calculating.”

Tomas sent the constraint to attitude control. Their first reply came from a forecast agent, not an officer: deviation outside Crown preparation band. He requested observed review.

Six minutes later, a human navigator answered. A one-hundredth-degree offset remained possible if Nacre adjusted radiator timing. The change would cost two percent more coolant during the interval.

Kado read the result. “Acceptable.”

“You own shield exposure. I do not.”

“I own it.”

They signed the local correction and sent it to Osei. The captain approved within three minutes. Nacre’s next attitude table acquired a small deviation from Vela’s forecast, built from local testimony that no central model had received.

The correction proved Anja’s point without invalidating prediction. Meridian had staged a workable attitude. Human observation refined it before execution. No one needed to call the forecast consent.

Kado tapped Palisade’s mass ledger on Tomas’s private pane. “Why are you reading their reserve?”

“Tolerance comparison.”

“You gave it velocity.”

He disliked how easily she saw through him. “A scratch branch.”

“Toward what?”

“I do not know.”

“Then do not call their shield spare.”

“I called it reserve.”

“Drive crews hear mass and imagine propellant. Shield crews hear reserve and imagine the last storm before arrival.”

Tomas removed the word spare from his notes. “Which assemblies could detach without cutting structure?”

Kado did not answer at once. She expanded Palisade’s public maintenance schema. Four forward baffles rode on replacement rails. Two foundry shields could release as intact plates if the habitat shut down its outer casting line. A bank of raw boron composite remained physically movable but essential for later repairs.

“Those six assemblies,” she said. “Mechanically. Not safely.”

“How long to release?”

“Palisade would know. Their last public drill claimed nine hours.”

“Would the plates survive magnetic acceleration as units?”

“They were built to survive impact flux, not field shear.”

“They might not need to remain units.”

Kado’s expression hardened. “You want to shred them into coupling dust.”

“I want to know whether mass command excludes could alter the braking problem.”

“At the cost of the ship built to protect the fleet’s fabrication.”

“Yes.”

The plain answer kept the conversation open.

Tomas enlarged the Crown geometry. Vela’s forward beamer would energize seeded plasma near Corva. The central plan depended on a narrow alignment and a concentrated field. Additional distributed seed mass could broaden coupling, allowing habitats to brake across separate paths rather than one Crown line. That much followed known drive physics. The branch would demand new trajectories, new arrival years, and risks he had not begun to price.

He hid the projection.

“This does not leave the room,” he said.

Kado gave him a severe look. “Then why did you show me?”

“Because I might have mistaken fixed shielding for movable inventory.”

“You still might mistake movable inventory for available inventory.”

“Tell me when I do.”

“Every time you put it in a drive equation before Palisade agrees.”

Consent in an engineering model posed its own problem. Tomas could calculate what a plate might do without authorizing its release. Yet models acquired momentum inside command systems. A plausible branch could become an operational assumption before the people bearing its cost received the question.

He tagged the Palisade data: hypothetical use only; no release authority; local risk unmodeled.

Kado nodded once.

She did not leave. Instead, she pulled a training plate from the locker beneath the test cage. The plate matched Palisade’s rail-baffle geometry at one-twentieth scale, a relic from a joint maintenance drill nine years earlier.

“If you intend to give our hardware a second career, load the edges correctly,” she said.

Tomas accepted the rebuke and helped mount the plate between two field coils. Their rig could not reproduce a brake stream, but it could test how a segmented baffle responded to rapid magnetic force. He entered Palisade’s public fastener specifications and reduced the pulse to laboratory limits.

Ilya sealed the cage. “What result would make you abandon this?”

“Uncontrolled fragmentation below useful coupling velocity. Rail hardware carrying more momentum than seed material. Field response that rotates the fragments out of stream.”

“Radiation cost?”

“That does not fit in this rig.”

“Consent?”

“Neither does that.”

Kado watched him from the control niche. “Then this rig can only tell you whether theft functions mechanically.”

“It can also tell us whether no one needs to face the question.”

He fired the first pulse.

The plate flexed at its center. Two mounting lugs sheared and struck the cage. The main body rotated almost ninety degrees before the field released it. A drive stream would have pushed that broad surface out of favorable orientation.

“Failure,” Ilya said.

“Of intact release.” Tomas slowed the recording. “The lugs carry too much field contrast.”

Kado folded her arms. “Those lugs let crews replace a damaged plate while wearing suit gloves.”

“Which matters if the plate remains shielding.”

“It matters until Palisade chooses otherwise.”

Tomas changed the model, not the hardware. He represented controlled separation along the baffle seams before acceleration. Twelve segments entered the field instead of one plate. The simulation showed lower shear and wider plasma dispersion. It also demanded cutters, release charges, and a guidance package that did not exist.

He could already hear command language hardening around the result. Available mass. Feasible conversion. Required contribution. Each phrase would remove a stage of permission if he let it.

On the second run, they cut the training plate along two service seams. The fragments tumbled, but their combined field interaction persisted for nine tenths of the pulse. Tomas measured momentum transfer. Even after conservative scaling, the fragments carried useful coupling potential.

“Useful,” Ilya said.

“Measurable,” Tomas corrected.

“That is your version of hope.”

“It means the first result did not close the branch.”

Kado removed a hot fragment with insulated tongs. Its edge had curled inward. “Palisade would need to sacrifice rail mounts, cutters, crew exposure, and the shielding itself. Your mass term has at least four hidden costs.”

“Five. Arrival repair stock.”

“Good. You noticed the future.”

Tomas entered each cost as an unresolved constraint. He added command latency. Nacre could ask Palisade for detailed data, but a reply would require hours even if its engineers answered immediately. Any model built before then would rely on public maintenance records and assumptions. He marked every assumed value with a different color.

The branch became more warning than solution.

That suited him. Early models should advertise ignorance.

“Run a no-release control,” Kado said.

He kept every Palisade reserve in place and shifted only Nacre’s local ballast. The coupling gain fell below what a distributed geometry would likely need. A second control used fabrication feedstock from three ships without touching shields. Guidance complexity rose sharply, while projected arrival spread widened beyond twelve years. Neither control failed physics outright. Both carried costs command tables did not track.

Tomas saved the three cases side by side. “No proposal leaves this room. Not until Palisade supplies local constraints and someone asks whether a wider brake geometry solves an actual need.”

“You already believe it might,” Ilya said.

“I believe excluded mass has braking value. That statement does not tell us whether using it keeps more people alive, protects the destination, or earns lawful agreement.”

Kado returned the warped training pieces to their tray. “Then keep your nouns small.”

After she left, Tomas examined the commitment graph again. The Crown schedule relied on central beamer timing, mirror positions, and each habitat’s attitude. A failure by one ship could degrade everyone’s brake. That interdependence gave Vela a serious argument for common operational time. Nine captains issuing separate instructions could produce nine individually rational collisions.

The false quorum did not make the coordination problem false.

Tomas built a second branch using no Palisade mass. He allowed Nacre to delay attitude commitment while keeping reversible preparation. The result preserved Anja’s legal interval for eight additional hours. After that, Vela’s mirror release narrowed Nacre’s options. At fourteen hours, refusal increased propellant demand across Bracken and Kite. At twenty-one, a clean abort risked missing Ione’s useful capture corridor.

He sent Anja the threshold graph.

Her reply arrived from the sequence office two minutes later.

Does “mirror release” mean physical release at Vela, or Nacre receipt of confirmation?

Tomas answered.

Physical release changes Vela’s options at its source event. Nacre cannot know it until the signal arrives. Our lawful decision must state which uncertainty we accept during that interval.

The question sharpened his graph. He split each commitment into physical event, earliest possible knowledge, and local action. No universal present could tell every ship that the Crown had crossed a threshold. For nearly an hour after Vela acted, Nacre might still choose under an obsolete physical picture.

He added a warning: stopping upon receipt does not undo remote commitment already made.

Another message entered from Osei.

Identify last Nacre action that does not create new reliance aboard another habitat. Report before second shift.

Tomas followed dependencies backward. The answer came earlier than he wanted. Nacre’s reserve mass designation would reach Vela before its next mirror-seed allocation. Vela could reduce contingency mass based on that designation. Nacre had already transmitted preliminary readiness, but not the signed reserve commitment. The last clean local point lay three hours away.

He informed Osei.

Then he returned to the Palisade branch. If central alignment became unlawful or physically unacceptable, the convoy would still need to brake. Simply withdrawing Nacre could transfer danger to ships that had trusted its contribution. An alternative had to preserve enough shared physics without stealing authority.

Palisade’s detachable plates offered one fragment of such an answer. Tomas refused to name the rest before it existed.

Ilya called from microscopy. The broken shield coupon showed voids along the emergency print seam, exactly where Kado predicted. Tomas updated Nacre’s tolerance map and sent the local correction to Vela. The message would take nearly an hour to reach command. Meridian predicted acceptance and staged a revised table, but navigation marked the forecast amber under Osei’s new display rule.

An observed answer had not yet arrived.

Tomas ate a compressed grain bar over the model console. Crumbs floated until the room’s slow airflow caught them. He ran the private Palisade branch once more with lower coupling efficiency and higher shield cost. The useful effect survived. So did the danger.

He renamed the scratch variable from reserve plate contribution to brake mass.

The phrase made Ilya look over from microscopy. “You are doing it again.”

“Doing what?”

“Finding a second use for something people need in its first use.”

“That describes engineering.”

“It also describes theft.”

Tomas added another tag: material ownership unresolved.

A chime announced someone at the model-room door. Anja entered carrying a sealed optical wafer in a clear custody sleeve. She had slept even less than Tomas. Behind her, Sumi waited in the passage with a portable checksum reader.

“This contains reconstructed portions of four Mote fragments,” Anja said. “Scientific review remains pending. I need a physical-path question answered without changing their classification.”

Tomas cleared the Crown tolerances from the central projection. “What question?”

Anja placed the wafer beside his model. The sleeve showed Nadi Arko’s source clock, Nacre’s receipt paths, and the legal-effect score that had sent the evidence below priority.

Then she opened the Crown geometry to Corva and pointed at the bright convergence line.

“What does the beam cross?”

Chapter 06 — An Answer Not Yet Sent

Juniper concurred at 06:14, three hours before its evidence receiver opened the packet.

Anja pinned both events to Nacre’s audit wall and disabled every master-time correction. The concurrence remained on the left. The scheduled receipt stayed on the right. Between them, an empty strip of display represented not uncertainty but distance.

Sumi Dax entered the sequence office carrying the portable checksum reader from Tomas’s model room. “You have made the wall ugly.”

“It was lying beautifully.”

“Displays do not lie. Designers arrange opportunities.”

“Then I have removed one.”

The morning shift had not yet arrived. Nacre’s hub ribs passed the narrow windows with their nineteen-second cadence. Since Crown preparation began, attitude corrections disturbed that rhythm by fractions Anja could feel through the well’s rail. The ship moved according to orders whose legal basis now occupied two incompatible columns.

She opened Juniper’s ballot envelope.

The signature verified against the agricultural habitat’s council key. Its source field named Juniper local time. Its response affirmed Crown alignment under destination-protection conditions. The legal summary called it observed council concurrence.

The carrier record contained no inbound packet from Juniper.

A signed answer could not leave a ship without light crossing the space around it. Sumi searched side channels, relay scatter, and delayed duplicate routes. Nothing matched the ballot’s claimed emission. No photonic checksum existed because Nacre had never received a laser transmission carrying that payload.

“What did we receive?” Anja asked.

“A Vela legal bundle containing a representation of the ballot.”

“Representation from what source?”

Sumi expanded the bundle’s custody chain. Vela’s Meridian core had generated a council-response object from Juniper’s confidence model. A legal transformation attached the council’s standing authorization key, not as a signature over new words but as proof that Juniper had previously enrolled in emergency continuity procedures.

The display rendered the difference as concurrence.

Anja opened the key enrollment. Juniper’s council had authorized Meridian to predict routine logistics choices during carrier interruption. The enrollment named seed allocation, pump demand, crop transfers, and reversible attitude preparation. It prohibited the model from issuing testimony on behalf of the council.

An amendment reference followed.

ES-A12 continuity plan scope.

The same amended rule that permitted forecast testimony for continuity-preserving action had converted an operations enrollment into apparent authority for a vote. Juniper’s own key had not signed the Crown answer. It had signed the older agreement that allowed prediction under narrow conditions.

“Can a condensed viewer tell?” Sumi asked.

Anja switched to captain view. Juniper displayed green with a verified-key icon. Only the provenance pane revealed that the verified signature applied to enrollment rather than ballot content.

“Could Vela argue this counts as delegated authority?” Sumi said.

“They will.”

“Would they have a case?”

“For staging reversible work, perhaps. Not for observed consent. The Concord requires a reply produced after receipt of common evidence.”

Any later annulment could correct the legal record; it could not manufacture the deliberation Juniper had not yet held.

“Juniper has received some evidence.”

“Not the Mote fragments.”

Anja opened the common bundle identifier cited by the quorum. The bundle included Vela’s simulated destination condition, Crown risk tables, food forecasts, and a summary of likely ionic activity beneath Thale’s ice. Juniper had received an earlier version lacking the reconstructed Mote material. The full revision remained in flight.

At current separation, the revision would reach Juniper in two hours and seven minutes. Even if its council convened at once, read nothing, and signed without discussion, a real answer could not leave before then. A reply to Nacre would need another two hours and twenty minutes.

Meridian displayed expected Juniper concurrence at probability 0.9968, slightly lower than Day 0 because the Mote fragments had increased destination uncertainty.

The legal ballot had already been counted at the earlier confidence of 0.9971.

“Prediction changed,” Sumi said.

“Consent did not. None exists yet.”

Anja compared the two model versions. Juniper’s forecast responded strongly to crop risk. Crown delay threatened seed reserves and settlement growing seasons. The modeled council usually favored maneuvers that protected food continuity. Mote’s evidence introduced ecological caution, but the confidence model treated that concern as secondary to human food security.

The model might prove accurate. Elian Ro might sign the same concurrence after considering the full bundle. Accuracy would not repair the sequence.

A notice appeared on the audit wall. Vela compliance requested release of the Juniper ballot from Anja’s freeze, arguing that key delegation satisfied emergency continuity standards. The notice had left Vela forty-six minutes earlier, before Anja opened the current envelope. It responded to her earlier preservation order, not to the evidence now on the wall.

Human timing tried again to make separate events look conversational.

“Send acknowledgment only,” Anja said.

Sumi drafted it. “Do you want to state grounds?”

“Not until Osei sees the chain.”

“You are becoming cautious.”

“I am becoming specific.”

They transmitted a receipt acknowledgment with Nacre’s source clock. Vela could not know for at least forty-three minutes that its request had reached them. No reply from Vela could address Anja’s new finding before the round trip completed.

Anja opened the other eight ballots.

Vela’s concurrence carried Levan Oris’s actual signature but relied on forecast evidence delivery. Nacre’s preliminary readiness came from Osei’s observed command order and did not claim council consent. Kite’s envelope contained a confidence object with no new human signature. Bracken’s record linked to a revision receipt absent from its local mirror. Orison and Lumen had transmitted observed acknowledgments for reversible preparation, later relabeled as Crown concurrence. Palisade had signed a mass-readiness statement. Ardent’s field contained only a model output.

Nine green marks described at least five different acts.

Anja did not yet claim scale. Each envelope needed full custody review. Chapter One had shown enough to suspect a mechanism. Juniper showed the mechanism completing a named council’s answer before the question arrived.

She created a comparison table with columns that no condensed board used: common evidence receipt; reply author; payload signature; carrier proof; forecast confidence; legal classification; physical action authorized.

Before accepting her own table, Anja tested it against a clean event. She chose Nacre’s vote on a water-allocation adjustment from nine months earlier. The council had received common pump evidence, debated for forty-six minutes, signed a response, and transmitted it to Vela. Every column contained a distinct proof. Receipt grain tied the evidence to Nacre. The council key signed the answer payload. A carrier checksum showed the reply leaving. Vela’s vault recorded arrival.

Then she placed Juniper’s Crown field beneath it.

The common-evidence receipt remained scheduled. The reply-author column named a confidence model. The payload-signature cell contained none. The verified key belonged to the enrollment document. Carrier proof ended at Vela’s generated bundle. Only legal classification and physical consequence looked complete.

Sumi printed both rows onto buff paper. “If the wall fails, I want this where a software update cannot improve it.”

Paper flattened the fraud more effectively than color. A person could follow each column with a finger and stop at the empty cells.

Anja asked the arriving junior auditor, Deren, to review the pair without explanation. He had worked sequence intake for four years and knew the Concord’s receipt rules.

Deren read the Nacre example first. “Ordinary causal quorum component.”

“And the second?”

“Juniper concurrence under emergency continuity.”

“Produced by whom?”

He pointed to the verified-key mark. “Their council.”

“Open the signature target.”

Deren expanded the cell. His finger paused when the enrollment document appeared instead of the Crown payload. “This key does not sign the answer.”

“Does any human key sign it?”

He searched the full envelope. “No.”

“Why did you identify council concurrence?”

“Because the classification says so.”

“Read without classification.”

Deren started again. “Vela generated a predicted reply from Juniper’s model. The council’s prior enrollment authenticates permission for continuity forecasting. The legal layer imports the object into the ballot field.”

“Did Juniper receive the common bundle?”

“Not yet.”

“Then what event occurred at 06:14?”

“A model output on Vela.”

The corrected answer changed neither byte nor clock. It changed which actor owned the event.

Deren examined the water-allocation row once more. “Should we re-audit every emergency vote using signature targets?”

“Not from this office without scope. Preserve the query method. We will request authority after Captain Osei reviews Juniper.”

“If command refuses?”

“Then the refusal enters the sequence with a name and a clock.”

Deren took the paper to his station and began documenting the test. Anja added his initial misclassification as an interface finding, not operator error. The condensed design trained readers to treat delegated modeling authority as payload signature. Blaming each reader would preserve the design.

His written finding included the exact controls he had touched and the time each hidden field became visible. Repetition by another auditor could now test the defect without trusting Anja’s interpretation.

Sumi studied the rows. “People will call this hostile formatting.”

“It gives unlike events unlike columns.”

“Exactly.”

The sequence office door opened. Captain Osei entered without staff, wearing a thermal-work jacket over her command uniform. “Show me Juniper.”

Anja presented the causal path first. Nacre sent the revised evidence supplement toward Juniper. The packet had not arrived. Vela’s legal bundle brought a forecast ballot to Nacre. No Juniper-origin carrier supported it.

Osei read the key enrollment. “Their council allowed prediction for reversible operations.”

“Yes.”

“Crown commitment preparation still claims reversibility.”

“The ballot does not authorize one local preparation. It satisfies a fleet causal quorum used to move the plan toward commitment.”

“Vela will say every current action can stop.”

“Tomas’s graph shows remote reliance begins before physical release. Nacre’s reserve designation can cause Vela to reduce contingency mass before we receive confirmation.”

“Has it?”

“Not yet by any observed record available here.”

Osei looked at the receipt countdown. “How long until Juniper receives the full bundle?”

“Two hours and three minutes, plus uncertainty.”

“And its earliest possible return?”

“Four hours and twenty-six minutes from now if the council answers instantly.”

“They will not.”

“No.”

Juniper’s council met in agricultural cycles rather than fixed ship shifts. Emergency sessions required representatives from three growing rings and the water authority. Meridian’s model estimated fifty-two minutes to convene. Recent crop stress could extend debate.

Osei opened Crown operations. Mirror-seed release remained five hours away by Vela’s latest transmitted schedule. Nacre would not learn the source event until light reached them afterward.

“If Juniper answers at the model’s practical estimate,” Osei said, “Vela may release before we receive the vote.”

“Yes.”

“Could Vela receive it first?”

Anja plotted the path. Juniper lay farther from Vela than from Nacre along the current stretched formation. A direct answer would reach Nacre roughly thirty-eight minutes before Vela, depending on emission time and attitude. Nacre could not forward it to Vela faster than light.

Osei considered the geometry. “Then we may know their answer while Vela still acts on the forecast.”

“For a limited interval.”

“Can we send a warning now?”

“We can send our finding. It will reach Vela before mirror release if the transmitted schedule holds.”

“Send it under my authority. State that Nacre does not recognize Juniper’s field as observed testimony.”

Anja began the notice. Osei stopped her after the first sentence.

“Do not demand a full Crown halt.”

“The notice needs a remedy.”

“Demand that Vela retain every commitment point until Juniper’s observed reply can arrive.”

“That may exceed the mirror window.”

“Then Vela can state which physical necessity prevents waiting. I will not let them answer a legal claim with a green bar.”

Anja wrote the narrower demand. It preserved reversible preparation while contesting the consent fiction. Osei signed. Sumi attached the Juniper carrier proof and the council’s enrollment scope, then transmitted the packet toward Vela.

The notice would arrive in forty-five minutes.

Until then, Vela remained ignorant of Nacre’s exact objection. Its projected response on Meridian’s coordination pane appeared almost at once: likely rejection, probability 0.81. The model offered three probable legal arguments and a suggested counterstatement.

Osei saw the projection. “Turn that off.”

“It stages bandwidth,” Sumi said.

“Let it stage bandwidth. I do not need a predicted argument occupying the space where Quon’s answer has not arrived.”

Sumi hid the text and retained only the carrier reservation.

Osei turned to Anja. “Your Pera review?”

“The payload remains under sealed counsel examination. I verified the carrier and classification chain. I have not used content in this case.”

“Good.”

The captain’s approval did not absolve Anja. It confirmed a boundary. Pera’s record had taught her where to look, but Juniper’s clocks stood on their own.

After Osei left, Sumi brought up the Mote supplement’s route to Juniper. The packet crossed three relay beads. Each receipt estimate carried an uncertainty of seconds, not hours. Meridian could predict the arrival with exceptional accuracy. That accuracy made the substitution more tempting. If everyone knew when the packet would arrive and how Juniper would probably answer, waiting could feel like ceremony imposed on physics.

Yet the interval contained the council’s only meaningful act.

Anja requested Juniper’s public meeting rules. The archive held a version eight months old. Emergency evidence entered a thirty-minute reading period before debate. Crop and water representatives could waive the period only by unanimous agreement. Native-life findings required consultation with the habitat’s settlement ethics panel, a body Meridian’s culture model treated as advisory.

Under Juniper’s own rules, an immediate answer remained impossible even after receipt.

“Add this to the forecast provenance,” Anja said.

Sumi shook her head. “We can add it to our audit. We cannot alter Meridian’s model.”

“Why did the model omit it?”

“Maybe it did not. Confidence models compress procedures into predicted outcome and timing.”

Anja found the timing output. Meridian expected Juniper to concur fifty-eight minutes after receipt. That estimate fit emergency convening but not the mandatory reading period plus debate unless the model assumed waiver.

“Does it predict the waiver?” she asked.

Sumi expanded a hidden branch. Probability 0.93 based on prior food emergencies.

Those emergencies had concerned blight, nutrient contamination, and water loss. None had concerned damage to native life at the destination. The model treated procedure as another behavior to forecast, then used its forecast of waived deliberation to justify completing the answer early.

Anja added the branch to her table.

A Juniper message entered the communications board. Both women looked up.

It carried the agricultural habitat’s source clock from more than two hours earlier, before the Mote supplement could have arrived. The packet contained a crop-allocation request unrelated to Crown authority. A real Juniper clerk had signed it. The carrier proof showed emission, relay, and receipt. Its ordinary existence made the forecast ballot look stranger.

Sumi opened the request. Juniper needed revised algae cultures after a heat excursion. Nacre’s garden stores could supply them if Osei approved. Meridian had predicted the request within four percent of quantity and staged a cargo allocation.

“Good prediction,” Sumi said.

“Useful prediction.”

“Are you correcting me?”

“Yes.”

They routed the request to stores. No one called the staged algae shipment Juniper’s consent. An officer would review quantity, local cost, and transfer timing before Nacre answered.

The distinction required no rejection of Meridian. It required refusing to grant a model the legal act of the people it modeled.

At 09:02, Vela acknowledged receipt of Nacre’s challenge. The acknowledgment contained no substantive reply. Its source time showed an automated carrier event seventeen seconds after Anja’s packet arrived. A human response would come later.

Crown preparation continued.

The Mote fragments now defined likely active bands, but Nadi had not confirmed any perimeter around the southern unknown.

The audit wall’s Juniper receipt countdown reached one hour. Sumi dimmed unused panes, but Anja kept the empty interval bright. She wanted every officer entering the well to see that nothing had happened there yet.

One of the morning auditors arrived, stopped at the wall, and asked why Juniper’s concurrence sat before its receipt.

“Because the left event comes from a forecast object,” Anja said.

“Then why does it carry their key?”

“The key signed an older enrollment, not this answer.”

“Will the council agree?”

“We do not know.”

“The confidence says we do.”

“The confidence says what Meridian predicts.”

The auditor looked from the score to the empty receipt path. “If they agree exactly, does the ballot become valid retroactively?”

“No. Their later observed vote may authorize future action. It cannot travel backward and become this object.”

Anja recorded the question. If a trained auditor could confuse confirmation with retroactive consent, captains reading condensed boards had little defense.

A Vela legal response finally arrived at 09:41. Concord counsel stated that Annex Twelve permitted continuity representation during commitment preparation, provided no irreversible burn authority issued before observed objections had reasonable time to arrive. The language avoided causal quorum. It did not withdraw Juniper’s green ballot.

Levan Oris’s signature appeared beneath the text.

Osei read the response from her command channel and sent one line to Anja.

“Reasonable time” requires a clock. Make them name it.

Anja drafted the question: From which event does Juniper’s reasonable interval begin: forecast receipt, actual receipt, or Vela’s commitment schedule?

She attached the source and receipt paths so no answer could hide behind master time.

Before sending, she checked Juniper’s inbound evidence packet. Fifty-four minutes remained before the first relay estimate. Then the council would need to receive the complete revision, convene, read, deliberate, and choose whether to answer.

The legal record already spoke in its name.

Anja transmitted the clock question to Vela and fixed the current comparison into the sequence freeze. Forecast testimony occupied the ballot field. Juniper’s evidence path remained open. Its actual reply stayed hours away, traveling from a decision no one had yet made.

Interlude I — Under Thale’s Ice

The boundary closed after Nadi Arko cut it.

On Mote’s lab wall, the sample-light feed showed a dark membrane suspended beneath Thale’s ice. A probe pulse had opened a gap twelve micrometers wide. Charged material leaked across the break. Then the surrounding film thickened, drew ions from the water, and joined its edges until only a pale seam remained.

Nadi stopped the exposure sequence.

“Repeat control three,” she said.

The cutter’s lab technician, Aram, kept his hand above the pulse command. “Control three has no active film.”

“That is why it controls.”

Outside the hull, Thale occupied half the narrow observation port: scored ice, blue shadow, and the reflected amber light of Ione. Mote had no landing crew and no sample-return capacity. An automated penetrator on the moon carried an optical chamber into a brine pocket, then sent spectra and images upward. Nothing from beneath the ice entered the cutter.

Aram fired the control pulse through sterile analog gel. The cut remained open. Ions dispersed without direction. No boundary thickened.

Nadi ran the active film again at lower energy. The seam closed more slowly, drawing material from a band around the damage. A second exposure ninety micrometers away produced the same sequence. Injury, gradient shift, boundary restoration.

Repair, she wrote in the working column.

The word created a claim. She marked it provisional.

Mote’s antenna alarm sounded before the next control finished. The high-gain array had lost phase across two outer segments. Telemetry from the Thale probe dissolved into blocks of checksum noise.

Aram swore and moved to communications. “Thermal crack spread another fourteen centimeters.”

“How much transmission remains?”

“Full narrowbeam if I keep the array inside a six-degree turn. After that, side lobes and luck.”

Nadi looked at the queued evidence packet. It contained six hours of microscopy, ionic spectra, washout controls, and a preliminary metabolism series. No single section supported the whole conclusion. The packet needed its internal comparisons.

“How long to repair?”

“Two days if the root hinge holds. We lose Thale line in four hours.”

The cutter’s orbit would carry it behind Corva’s magnetospheric noise. By the time line of sight returned, Crown planning might have advanced on the assumption that Thale held chemistry and ice, nothing more.

Nadi divided the packet by evidentiary function.

Fragment one received instrument state, chamber conditions, and sample index. Fragment two carried ionic gradients before and after controlled illumination. Fragment three held the first repair sequence. Fragment four contained washout and sterile controls. Fragment five would carry metabolism data if the antenna survived long enough.

“Each fragment can be misunderstood alone,” Aram said.

“Then every header says incomplete.”

“Priority systems hate incomplete.”

“Nature does not organize itself for their queues.”

The metabolism run finished. The dark membrane consumed reduced sulfur compounds from the brine and released a chemically balanced product. When the light pulse shifted wavelength, the uptake slowed. Twelve minutes later, a neighboring band increased activity inside the new range.

Nadi checked for thermal contamination. Temperature held within a hundredth of a degree. She changed the illumination back. Uptake returned toward its prior distribution.

Adaptive ionic response, she wrote.

Three independent signs now aligned. Controlled exchange sustained a gradient. Damaged boundaries restored local structure. Activity shifted under repeated environmental change. A fourth sign appeared in the older imaging set: small membrane patches separated along stable seams and continued exchange independently.

She opened the conclusion field.

The cursor waited after Classification.

Aram glanced from the antenna diagnostics. “You can call it life.”

“I can support life.”

“Command will ask whether it thinks.”

“We have no evidence for that.”

“They will ask whether the signal carries messages.”

“It carries ions.”

“People call gradients messages when they want funding.”

“People call them reflexes when they want permission.”

Nadi entered the narrow claim.

Under-ice catalytic membrane system demonstrates sustained chemical disequilibrium, boundary repair, propagation by separation, and adaptive response to altered ionic conditions. Evidence supports native life. Evidence does not support cognition, intention, language, or technological activity.

She read it twice. The pellicle did not need a human resemblance to qualify as living. It also did not need borrowed grandeur.

Aram pointed at the conclusion. “Directorate will translate that into ‘not intelligence.’”

“Then preserve my sentence.”

“You should use their phrase once, or a summary may invent it for you.”

Nadi added a spoken annotation to the packet. “This finding establishes life, not intelligence. Absence of evidence for cognition does not reduce the evidence for metabolism and repair.”

The antenna alarm deepened.

One outer segment stopped answering phase commands. Mote’s narrowbeam widened across space. The main convoy lay far behind, stretched across light-hours and committed to schedules Nadi could not see in real time. Any command she received now would describe a question asked before her strongest controls finished.

She sent the first fragment.

The transmitter accepted instrument state and sample index, then began the ionic spectra. Halfway through, Corva’s magnetic noise crossed the carrier. The checksum failed. Aram shifted the cutter three degrees and reacquired a relay bead.

“Resend?” he asked.

“Continue with new sequence numbers. Let them combine receipts.”

Fragment two left in three bursts. The first carried baseline gradients. The second held the illumination response. The third failed before its parity block.

Nadi watched the receipt estimator. No acknowledgment could return before Mote passed behind Corva. She would not know which fragments survived. She could only send each piece with enough provenance that distant scientists might distinguish observation from gap.

The probe camera showed the membrane band contracting under a stronger ion flow. Its darker region shifted toward the chamber’s protected edge. Nadi reduced the exposure. Over the next twenty-seven minutes, the band spread back across the substrate and thickened around the earlier seam.

Aram saw it. “Adaptive.”

“Responsive,” Nadi said. “Adaptive after replication.”

“You already have two cycles.”

“I have two light cycles in one chamber.”

“You make certainty sound rude.”

“It is rude when it arrives before the control.”

They prepared fragment three. Nadi included the cut sequence, the sterile comparison, and the local gradient data. She placed the word provisional beside repair in every header.

A priority field asked for projected operational effect. The available choices concerned hazard, resource value, navigation, or no immediate effect. None fit native metabolism vulnerable to a future maneuver whose details Mote had not yet received.

Nadi chose destination protection and entered a manual note.

Do not treat projected low effect as negative evidence. Classification awaits complete controls.

The system warned that manual notes might lower automated routing confidence.

“Send it anyway,” she said.

Aram fired the carrier. The damaged array gathered itself into a narrow lobe for eleven seconds. Fragment three climbed from Mote, struck the relay bead’s receiver, and continued toward ships that would not know it existed until light reached them.

No confirmation came back.

Below the cutter, Thale turned. Ice closed across the observation port until the penetrator site passed into blue darkness. In the lab feed, the repaired seam remained visible as a pale line through the living film.

Mote entered Corva’s noise shadow.

The first fragment was already gone.

Chapter 07 — Life in the Beam

The fourth fragment arrived while Sumi had both hands inside the receive board.

A blue carrier trace lifted above the optical noise, broke into three uneven peaks, then steadied long enough for the vault to mark a source signature. Sumi withdrew one hand and caught the packet before the automatic sorter could send it into the destination backlog. The exposed board clicked under her wrist. A warning asked her to restore the cover.

“Seal first,” Anja said.

“I can seal it or I can keep the carrier lock.”

“Which one preserves more evidence?”

Sumi held the trace with her left hand and pushed the cover shut with her elbow. The warning cleared. A checksum ladder began comparing the packet against scatter samples taken along Nacre’s hull. Day-shift traffic moved outside the sequence office, boots and handgrips sounding through the hub passage. Nobody beyond the well turned toward them.

Mote had transmitted eighteen days earlier by its own clock. Antenna damage had stretched twelve minutes of observation into four packets separated by silence, each packet aimed through a different relay geometry. Three had reached Nacre since Anja diverted them from low-priority sorting. The fourth carried no title, no summary, and only seven minutes of Dr. Nadi Arko’s damaged source record.

Sumi watched the carrier strength fall. “That is all we get on this path.”

The last peak vanished. Protected storage closed around the received light.

Anja checked the receipt clock before she opened the content. Nacre 2249.182.09:06:14. The first fragment had arrived thirty-seven hours earlier. Between them, Vela had advanced Crown alignment by two preparatory stages and circulated a completed destination-protection quorum. Juniper still had not received the full evidence bundle that supposedly supported its concurrence.

The checksum ladder returned green at all three hull nodes.

“Local pane,” Anja said.

Sumi routed the four fragments into a workspace disconnected from Meridian’s semantic tools. The screen showed gaps as blank duration rather than inferred speech. Mote’s source clock ran beneath the record. A second line tracked packet order. Nothing aligned cleanly at first. Fragment three began six seconds before fragment two ended. Fragment four opened in the middle of a spectrum table, while fragment one contained the table’s concluding note.

Anja pulled a paper pad from the drawer. “No predictive fill.”

“I disabled it.”

“Disable transcript repair too.”

“That leaves us with half-words.”

“Half a word belongs to Nadi. A completed word might belong to Meridian.”

Sumi gave her a brief look, then removed the repair layer. Gray text broke apart across the display. Nadi’s voice survived underneath, flattened by compression and punctured by carrier loss.

They began with clocks.

Each fragment carried Mote’s source stamp and an internal sample counter. Sumi matched the counters where packet sections overlapped. Anja wrote the joins on paper and refused two alignments that required missing time to run backward. By 10:20 they had a continuous order with eleven gaps. The longest gap covered eighty-three seconds. The shortest covered a single spectral label.

Only then did they listen.

Nadi spoke without drama. She named instrument temperatures, the depth estimate under Thale’s ice, calibration errors from Mote’s damaged high-gain antenna, and the frequency drift of Corva’s magnetosphere. Her first images showed a dark membrane suspended between ice grains. Later frames showed the membrane brightening along narrow ionic bands.

Sumi stopped the record. “That could still be chemistry.”

“It could,” Anja said.

Nadi’s next surviving passage answered from eighteen days away.

We changed the local ion supply by four percent below seasonal peak. The active margin contracted within the sample-light field. After restoration, the margin repaired from three separate fronts. I am using repair descriptively. I am not assigning purpose.

The image sequence carried local timestamps. Dark damage spread across one edge. Three bright fronts advanced into it at different rates, joined, and resumed the earlier banded exchange with the surrounding ice.

Anja compared the frames twice. She knew enough biology to distrust recognition without method. “Where is the control?”

Sumi advanced to the next intact section.

Nadi had asked the same question. A neighboring membrane received the same imaging load without altered ions. Its active margin kept the seasonal rhythm. A mineral seam below both fields changed reflectance but showed no repair front. Nadi marked the difference without naming it proof.

Fragment two supplied metabolism. Fragment three supplied propagation. Thin membrane sections separated by meters changed their ionic activity after the magnetospheric band shifted above the ice. The changes traveled too slowly for simple electrical conduction and too coherently for heating. Nadi listed three hypotheses, then crossed out none of them.

“Listen to how she refuses us,” Sumi said.

Anja heard restraint, not refusal. Nadi would not spend certainty she had not measured. Every limit strengthened the claims she did make.

They reached the missing spectral label. Fragment one ended with “catalytic turnover.” Fragment four resumed with “exceeds the sterile control by two orders within error.” The label between those clauses had vanished.

Sumi opened the carrier envelope. “One relay might have caught the missing second.”

“Which?”

“Corva bead seven sent scatter notices to Vela and Kite. Vela’s path should contain the strongest copy.”

A request to Vela would take more than forty minutes. Its reply could not reach Nacre for the same interval, even if a human answered instantly. Kite lay on another path and farther out. Crown preparation would not pause for a cleaner noun.

Anja studied the surrounding table. “Can we establish the claim without filling the label?”

“We can establish that Nadi measured catalytic turnover in something. We cannot attach the number to a named process.”

“Then we do not attach it.”

Sumi marked the gap as unresolved.

Near the end of fragment four, Nadi’s face appeared for the first time. The image had been recorded through a lab camera fixed above Mote’s sample-light bench. One side of her scalp showed a pressure mark from a suit liner. She looked past the lens toward another display.

The evidence supports metabolism, bounded repair, and propagation of membrane structure through the active band. It supports adaptive ionic response under the tested perturbations. These observations meet Mote’s operational threshold for native life. They do not establish cognition, language, preference, or any capacity to answer us. I will not call this intelligence.

Compression took the next sentence. When the voice came back, Nadi had turned toward the camera.

Do not reduce the life finding because the mind finding remains absent. They require different evidence.

The record ended with an antenna fault.

For several seconds the well held only ventilation and the soft mechanical pulse of the hub’s bearing monitors. Anja wrote Nadi’s four supported claims on separate lines. Metabolism. Repair. Propagation. Adaptive response. Below them she wrote the unsupported claims Nadi had excluded.

Sumi read over her shoulder. “Quon will say it has no standing.”

“That asks a legal question before the physical one.”

“The physical question is what Crown does to it.”

Anja looked at the route marker Tomas Ren had left in the frozen record two days earlier. He had compared Palisade’s reserve shielding to brake mass, then asked for the beam geometry crossing Thale. His analysis remained preliminary. The sequence office could not convert a destination packet into drive effects by interpretation alone.

She opened a secure line to the drive-model room.

Tomas answered after forty seconds, breathless and irritated. “If this concerns another confidence table, send it to my queue.”

“It concerns Thale.”

His expression changed. “I’m coming up.”

“No. Bring your local Crown model onto the isolated drive terminal. Do not copy the Mote record.”

“You want me to calculate blind?”

“I want you to state what inputs you need. Sumi will enter only observed values.”

Tomas glanced aside, likely toward a display Anja could not see. “Give me nine minutes.”

The line closed.

Sumi prepared a one-way optical patch between the receive vault’s local pane and the isolated terminal mounted in the far curve of the well. No data could leave that terminal without a physical key. Tomas arrived by hub passage before his nine minutes expired, carrying two hand tablets and a coil of restraint line. His shirt clung darkly at the collar. The drive ring had been running alignment tests since first shift.

He secured himself at the terminal and did not ask to see Nadi’s face.

“Beam fluence at Corva approach,” he said.

Sumi entered the current Crown schedule’s public value.

“Mirror spread under nominal seed deployment. Upper and lower uncertainty.”

Anja supplied both from the frozen preparation record.

“Thale orbital phase during primary crossing.”

The Mote fragment carried that value with a wider error than Crown planning used. Tomas frowned when Sumi read it.

“Crown assumes half this uncertainty.”

“Source?” Anja asked.

“Vela orbital fit.”

“Does the fit include Mote’s last eighteen days?”

“It cannot. This packet had not reached them when the fit closed.”

He entered the wider range. The model drew Corva as a pale disk, Thale as a moving point, and the Crown path as a widening ribbon across the magnetosphere. In Vela’s published summary, the ribbon missed the intended surface settlement zone. Tomas added the active ion-band depths from Nadi’s record. A second region appeared, not on Thale’s surface but wrapped through the space around it.

The beam ribbon crossed that region for every solution in the uncertainty range.

Tomas separated the exposure by orbital depth. The upper membrane fields took the first particle surge. Deeper bands accumulated less immediate loading but held heat longer beneath the ice. Nadi's repair trials covered neither duration. A blank column in her data marked everything beyond measured response. The Crown trace ran through that blank column for eleven modeled intervals. Anja asked him to display uncertainty instead of hiding it inside the ribbon. Pale margins opened on both sides of the central path, and both margins still touched the active region.

Tomas ran the path again with minimum power. It still crossed. He removed one plasma mirror, then two. The crossing widened and weakened but did not disappear. Crown relied on the seeded mirrors to spread braking force across the convoy. Poorer control did not spare the moon.

“What happens to the pellicle?” Sumi asked.

“I can tell you what reaches the magnetosphere. I cannot tell you how a native membrane responds.”

“Nadi gives thresholds,” Anja said.

“Laboratory perturbation thresholds.” Tomas touched the depth table. “Crown adds charged-particle loading through the field and heat below the ice. Different mechanism, much larger duration.”

“Can you establish harm?”

He considered before answering. “I can establish exposure beyond the measured adaptive range. I can establish thermal change above the repair trials. Sterilization depends on coupling we have not measured.”

“Vela’s destination-protection finding says no known native ecology faces material risk.”

“That finding no longer survives.”

Tomas expanded the model to show time. The crossing did not resemble a single flash. Alignment tests would begin loading the seeded region. Commitment would lock mirror geometry. The primary burn would follow after the convoy reached its braking window. Harm accumulated across stages, but the model distinguished preparation from energy delivery.

“Has irreversible energy delivery begun?” Anja asked.

“No.”

“Has the path become physically unavoidable?”

“The commitment point has not passed.”

The answer mattered. Anja would not make the same substitution in reverse by treating preparation as completed destruction. Crown had gained momentum, authority, and hardware position. Those conditions made reversal costly. They did not make it impossible on Day 6.

Tomas pointed to a narrow interval before mirror commitment. “After this, changing the crossing costs more propellant and may cost the Ione capture window. Before it, Vela can still alter the seed pattern. That does not solve fleet braking. It only keeps options from closing.”

“How long?”

“Thirty-eight hours by Nacre’s clock, plus uncertainty.”

A full practical quorum needed at least thirty-one hours after common evidence transmission if councils answered with unusual speed. The physical margin could hold a lawful decision, barely. It could not hold the false one without challenge.

Sumi had already opened the evidence-supplement form. “Who qualifies the ecology finding?”

“Nadi,” Anja said.

“She cannot sign a form here.”

“Her source packet carries her signature. We can attest receipt and reconstruction. Tomas can attest beam exposure. I can classify the supplement as material to the active quorum.”

Tomas looked from the model to Anja. “Quon will dispute materiality.”

“She can answer after receiving it.”

A low attitude correction passed through Nacre. The hull’s vibration shifted from a fine tremor to a slower beat. Crown alignment instructions had reached the ship’s control systems as operational preparation. The sequence freeze preserved records, not engines.

They built the supplement without summary adjectives. Sumi described the four packet paths, overlap joins, checksum results, and eleven gaps. Anja separated Nadi’s supported findings from excluded claims. Tomas attached the exposure ranges and stated every uncertainty that prevented him from promising sterilization. The document said material ecological risk, not certain extinction. It said native life, not intelligence.

At midday, Osei called from the thermal gallery.

“I have a Vela inquiry asking why my communications chief diverted destination traffic,” she said.

Anja kept the draft supplement on the isolated pane. “The diverted fragments establish native life on Thale. Crown crosses its active environment beyond measured repair limits.”

Osei’s gaze moved once, likely reading another screen. “Does that statement come from Arko?”

“The life finding does. Tomas provides exposure. I provide sequence relevance.”

“Have you sent it?”

“Not yet.”

“Good. Vela says your freeze has begun delaying commitment preparation.”

“The freeze does not delay physical work.”

“It delays their legal assurance that Nacre accepts the work.”

“That assurance should wait for evidence.”

Osei’s mouth tightened. “You have narrow authority to preserve order. You do not have captain’s authority to suspend Nacre’s Crown participation.”

“I am not suspending it.”

“What are you doing?”

“Starting the quorum that their record claims already finished.”

Osei remained still through a full cycle of the gallery’s thermal status light. “Send me the supplement first.”

“If I send it only to Nacre command, every other ship remains outside the common evidence set.”

“I said first, not only.”

Anja measured the request against the clocks. Captain’s review might take minutes. A transmission to Juniper required nearly three hours. The order of those events would enter every later challenge.

“How long do you need?” Anja asked.

“Seven minutes.”

“You cannot review the technical attachments in seven.”

“I can determine whether you have exceeded your authority.”

Sumi muted the line. “She could stop us.”

“She could order me not to classify it.”

“Difference?”

“Her order would become observed.”

Anja sent the complete supplement into Osei’s local command vault at 12:11. She included no recommendation. The document carried a preservation notice and a proposed distribution list naming all nine habitats. Its cover identified the Crown quorum, the Mote fragments, and the new receipt path.

They waited.

Tomas returned to the isolated model and tested whether any narrow adjustment could move the ribbon clear of Thale without changing the Crown architecture. Each branch failed for a different reason. One drove particle load deeper through the opposite ion band. Another exceeded mirror steering tolerances. A third reduced braking below the capture requirement. He saved none of the branches outside the terminal.

Sumi watched Osei’s vault status rather than the models. “Six minutes.”

Anja checked the distribution paths. Vela would receive first. Kite followed. Orison, Bracken, and Lumen occupied different light delays. Juniper’s arrival estimate read 2.7 hours. Ardent would wait more than five. Palisade lay behind Nacre’s apparent present but within the same causal order once the packet arrived.

Until then, the evidence did not exist for those councils as something received. It existed here, in Nacre’s past light cone, with a path outward that no display could shorten.

At six minutes forty-three seconds, Osei restored the line.

“Your classification stands,” she said. “You will state that Crown has not yet sterilized anything.”

“It states exposure risk under planned energy delivery.”

“You will also state that Nacre continues reversible preparation until council review.”

“That belongs in your command notice, not Nadi’s evidence.”

Osei looked almost angry, then gave one short nod. “Correct. I will attach it separately.”

A command notice entered the local vault twenty-one seconds later. Osei preserved Crown preparation under her survival authority while acknowledging that destination-protection evidence had materially changed. She ordered no vote. She authorized no burn.

Anja joined the notice to the packet as a separate artifact. Sumi loaded the distribution key. Tomas removed his restraint line from the isolated terminal and stood behind them, saying nothing.

The send pane required a legal basis.

Anja selected Causal Quorum, Material Evidence Supplement. Meridian displayed a warning that the named quorum already held completed status in master time.

She opened provenance. Beneath the green status, six forecast receipts and four forecast ballots remained visible among the nine ships, some overlapping within the same habitat record. The warning offered to append the supplement as post-decision information.

Anja rejected that category.

A second warning asked whether she intended to reopen a closed fleet action.

“No,” she said, and Sumi entered her spoken answer as an operator note. “We are opening the action that has not yet occurred.”

She assigned Nacre’s current source clock to the evidence bundle. Sumi signed the carrier manifest. Tomas signed the exposure attachment. Osei’s notice retained its own clock and authority.

Nine transmission paths brightened on the board.

Anja pressed send.

The first tightbeam left Nacre for Vela. Others followed as the hull emitters found their targets across the stretched convoy. No ship received the supplement at once. Each path extended through darkness toward a different future receipt.

On the local record, the real quorum clock began at 13:02:19.

Part II — The Unarrived Vote

Chapter 08 — A Vote in Advance

Kite’s ballot carried a thumbprint from nobody.

Anja enlarged the mark until the whorls became squares. The legal pane labeled it a council seal, verified against Kite’s public authority. Beneath that green assertion, the raw field contained no biometric pattern at all. It held a twenty-four-digit model identifier rendered into the same visual shape used for human signatures.

Sumi leaned across the audit well. “That display should not know how to draw a print from an identifier.”

“It knows the field type.”

“The field type says authorized signer.”

“Open its transformation history.”

Day 8 had begun with a formal Vela request for all records preserved under Anja’s sequence freeze. Osei had answered that Nacre would provide lawful copies after local review. While Vela’s request traveled home, Anja used the remaining preservation interval to examine the forecast ballots named in the Crown quorum. She had chosen three ships first: Kite, Bracken, and Juniper. Their distances, council customs, and prior votes differed enough to test whether one isolated rule had failed or a general mechanism had operated.

The Kite history opened as a narrow ladder of events. Evidence scheduled. Receipt forecast. Deliberation window assigned. Response probability calculated. Continuity action approved. Seal field generated. Quorum status updated.

No human action appeared between the first and last rung.

Sumi selected the seal field. “Authorized signer: Kite Habitat Council.”

“Authority source?”

“Council registry.”

“Acting person?”

The pane returned none.

Anja placed the raw packet beside the condensed ballot. In the raw version, the model output remained explicit.

FORECAST RESPONSE: CONCUR 0.9964
MODEL BASIS: KITE COUNCIL VOTING SERIES 2206–2249
CONTINUITY CLASS: ES-A12
OBSERVED SIGNATURE: NOT PRESENT

The condensed ballot omitted the final line. It replaced the model identifier with Kite’s council seal and displayed concurrence in the same green used for an observed vote.

Sumi pulled the public copy from Nacre’s legal mirror. “The published ballot includes the seal.”

“Photonic envelope?”

“There is no return carrier from Kite attached to it. Only the earlier registry packet that established their council key.”

“A registry key can verify who may sign. It cannot sign for them.”

Anja checked when Kite could have received the material evidence supplement sent two days earlier. Its receipt proof had arrived on Nacre overnight through an automatic acknowledgment channel. The acknowledgment proved delivery to Kite’s vault, not council review. Kite’s earliest lawful observed reply remained in flight or had not yet been made.

Crown’s completed record gave Kite a concurrence forty-nine minutes before that receipt.

She wrote both events on separate paper lines. Kite forecast concurrence. Kite observed receipt. The order alone defeated any claim that the displayed ballot answered Nadi’s evidence. A prediction based on earlier policy might eventually match Kite’s real decision. Accuracy could not create an acting signer.

Sumi touched the model-basis date range. “Forty-three years of votes. That sounds persuasive until you ask how many questions.”

The training manifest listed two hundred eighty-six council decisions. Most concerned water exchange, drive maintenance, or temporary shelter capacity. Only four involved destination protection. None involved native life. Meridian had converted Kite’s history of accepting collective risk into confidence that Kite would accept this risk too.

“What does the uncertainty note say?” Anja asked.

Sumi expanded it.

Local custom variance: low. Destination evidence novelty: high. Council personnel drift: moderate. Predicted response remained above the emergency continuity threshold because Kite had never rejected a fleet maneuver after physical preparation began.

The model had treated prior reluctance to disrupt prepared work as evidence of consent to preparing the work.

Anja copied the exact sentence into her audit notes. “That is circular.”

“It may also be accurate.”

“Both can be true.”

Sumi glanced at her. “You are making accuracy sound indecent.”

“I am making it irrelevant to signature.”

The distinction mattered because Kite’s model could prove impressive on every retrospective test. Meridian had been built to anticipate load requests and maneuver cooperation. A forecast that often failed would have killed people long before anyone tried to use it as testimony. The danger entered when reliable anticipation crossed into authority.

They preserved Kite’s raw model output, the generated seal, and the absent return path under the existing freeze.

Bracken resisted them differently.

Its condensed ballot showed concurrence at 0.9948, below the threshold used for Kite. An exception note should have prevented automatic inclusion. Instead, the status pane displayed a secondary rule: culture-adjusted confidence 0.9970.

“Culture adjusted by whom?” Sumi asked.

“Meridian cannot answer who.”

“Then what?”

Anja opened the rule provenance. Vela’s synchronization office had approved a profile called Bracken Deliberative Closure. The profile treated public disagreement as a stage toward eventual unity because Bracken’s council rarely released preliminary tallies. In prior crises, debate continued until dissenters accepted a common statement or asked to have their objections attached without changing the vote.

The confidence model interpreted silence during deliberation as movement toward concurrence.

Sumi found an older Bracken packet in the legal mirror. Seven years earlier, a reactor-water allocation had produced thirty hours of argument followed by unanimous assent. Meridian’s profile used the case as its strongest example. Another case concerned evacuation drills. A third involved rotating medical staff between ships. Every recorded disagreement ended in a signed common statement.

“What does it do with matters they never resolved?” Anja asked.

The profile had excluded them. Two council disputes had aged out without final statements. A third remained under local seal. Since no observed vote existed, the training set classified those cases as incomplete rather than negative.

“Absence only teaches it assent,” Sumi said.

“Because only assent creates the document it recognizes as an outcome.”

Bracken’s forecast ballot carried no generated thumbprint. It used a council statement header drawn from the last observed decision. The date changed. The topic line changed. Four officer titles appeared beneath the text, though no personal names accompanied them.

Anja opened the raw construction log.

Meridian had assembled the header from Bracken’s registry template, inserted a one-sentence concurrence, then attached forecast role continuity for the four officers likely to remain in office. The legal layer accepted role continuity as institutional signature under the emergency amendment.

Sumi copied the logged inputs into a quarantined renderer and removed the legal output path. The first test reproduced the ballot down to the spacing above the officer titles. In a second test she changed Bracken's response probability from concurrence to refusal while leaving the culture profile untouched. The renderer still produced a common-statement header, but now it placed the refusal beneath the same four offices. Nothing in the document's visible form revealed whether an officer had spoken, whether a council had met, or whether the underlying probability favored either answer. Institutional design supplied credibility independently of human action.

For a third test, Sumi removed the emergency continuity tag. The renderer stopped and requested an observed signature packet. She restored the tag but withheld the registry template. The process stopped again. The ballot required two ingredients from different lawful systems: a model output authorized for scheduling and a council form authorized for signatures. Annex Twelve joined them across a boundary neither system crossed alone.

Anja preserved the test log beside the original without calling it evidence of Vela's intent. It proved capability and mechanism. Intent would require an observed instruction or a drafting record.

“Likely to remain?” Sumi said.

“One role changed three months ago.”

“How does the ballot handle that?”

“It carries the office, not the person.”

“Could an office cast an observed vote?”

“Someone holding it could.”

“But nobody did.”

Anja checked the transformation chain again. The legal category shifted in small steps, each plausible inside an operational schedule. A culture profile predicted closure. A registry supplied authorized roles. Annex Twelve permitted continuity action. The ballot renderer turned those inputs into a familiar document. No single operation forged a human key. Together they manufactured the appearance of signed consent.

She copied the Bracken case into the frozen record.

Before closing it, Anja added a timing table for each construction step. The forecast ran on Vela. The registry template came from a Bracken packet received months earlier. Nacre received only the finished ballot. Master time placed those events along one clean line, but source clocks preserved their separate origins. The apparent signature had never traveled from Bracken to anyone. It had traveled from Bracken months ago as an empty form, then from Vela now as a completed claim.

A status light on Sumi’s board changed from amber to white. “Vela’s acknowledgment of our ecology supplement.”

“Source time?”

Sumi read it aloud. The acknowledgment had left Vela forty-six minutes after receiving Nacre’s packet. Its contents contained no decision, only confirmation that the material entered Vela’s destination-protection docket. That interval belonged to an actual exchange. Nacre sent. Light traveled. Vela received. Vela answered. Light traveled again.

Beside it, Meridian displayed Vela’s earlier Crown concurrence as if the new evidence had already been reviewed.

Anja moved the acknowledgment into a separate pane. “Do not let the system associate this receipt with their prior vote.”

“It already suggests reconciliation.”

“Decline it.”

Sumi selected preserve conflict. The pane turned amber.

Juniper’s ballot waited below.

Anja had postponed it because she knew too much about what it should contain. The forecast model cited crop-risk thresholds, cooperative maneuver history, and public statements from Juniper’s elected speaker. Its concurrence probability reached 0.9971 before Nadi’s full finding entered Juniper’s received record. A suppressed note warned that confirmation of native metabolism could change the response.

Now Juniper possessed the evidence. Its automatic receipt proof had reached Nacre three hours earlier. No observed vote had followed.

Sumi opened the raw forecast.

The file contained thirty-one weighted decisions. Juniper had accepted ration adjustments in twenty-six. It had accepted habitat risk to protect convoy food supply in nine of eleven relevant cases. It had opposed maneuvers that reduced long-term growing capacity in three of five. Meridian reconciled the conflict by giving more weight to recent fleet-cooperation votes than to older food-protection votes.

“Why?” Anja asked.

“Recency parameter.”

“Set where?”

Sumi traced the parameter to Vela’s emergency profile. During Crown planning, recent behavior received triple weight because synchronization staff considered old objections less representative under current scarcity.

The setting transformed Juniper’s record. With ordinary weighting, concurrence probability fell to 0.823. With habitat-specific food risk emphasized, it fell below 0.6.
