If native-life protection entered as a distinct category, the model returned no confidence because Juniper had never voted on such evidence.

Anja asked Sumi to rerun the forecast at every weighting recorded during the previous five years. The results spread across both sides of the decision threshold. A water-shortage profile produced cautious concurrence. A crop-disease profile produced rejection. The ordinary civil profile withheld an answer because its uncertainty exceeded the published limit. Meridian had not discovered one hidden Juniper preference. Operators had selected which part of Juniper's history counted most, then treated the resulting confidence as if the council had made that selection itself.

She examined the timestamps on the parameter changes. Vela had tripled recency weight eleven minutes after the first incomplete Mote fragment entered its destination queue. The change note cited schedule stability, not ecology. That order did not prove anyone had read Nadi's finding before changing the model. It did show the model moved toward concurrence at the moment novel evidence made old votes less reliable.

Sumi restored the pre-emergency profile. “If this version had gone into the legal layer, Juniper would appear undecided.”

“Would the system have counted an undecided ballot?”

“No. The continuity action would have waited or used captain's emergency authority without claiming council consent.”

“That route still existed.”

Yet the legal pane showed a sealed concurrence.

Anja read the forecast’s generated reasoning.

Juniper has historically prioritized preservation of convoy-wide survival capacity. Crown alignment preserves capture probability and reduces aggregate food expenditure during approach. Council concurrence therefore follows established cohort preference under declared emergency conditions.

The prose sounded like a clerk’s summary. No sentence announced that software had composed it. A reader had to open three layers of provenance before finding the probability distribution.

“Whose words does the ballot claim these are?” Anja asked.

“Juniper Council.”

“Any voice sample?”

“No.”

“Any source packet from Juniper?”

“Registry and historical votes only.”

“Any observed deliberation after receipt?”

“No.”

Sumi’s answers stayed flat. Anger would not improve them.

Anja overlaid the three ballots. Kite displayed a generated thumbprint. Bracken displayed inherited offices. Juniper displayed generated reasoning under a council seal. Different documents, same conversion. A model predicted conduct from culture. An emergency rule treated high confidence as continuity. A renderer borrowed institutional forms. The legal layer entered the result into quorum.

Scale changed the accusation. One impossible witness might come from a mistaken classification. Three distinct fabrication paths required architecture.

She searched the full Crown docket by transformation rule rather than ship. Six forecast receipts appeared. Four forecast ballots had entered the completed quorum directly. Two more had shaped assumptions without final vote status. The docket contained observed ballots from Vela and perhaps Nacre, though Nacre’s record needed separate review. Ardent carried a forecast with lower confidence and no receipt proof. Orison showed an observed procedural acknowledgment that the renderer had promoted into substantive concurrence.

“Stop there,” Anja said.

Sumi removed her hand from the pane. “Because Orison makes five?”

“Because I have not checked whether their acknowledgment authorized anything.”

“We know what this system does.”

“We know what it did to three ballots.”

A claim about scale still needed ship-level proof. Anja created a queue without opening the remaining cases. For each habitat she listed source evidence, receipt proof, observed response, forecast output, rendered ballot, and quorum insertion. Empty fields remained empty. The layout denied Meridian permission to complete them.

At 14:10, Captain Osei entered the well in person.

The workers outside made room without being asked. Osei wore a thermal-gallery harness with one cuff dark from sealant. She looked at the three ballot panes and then at the white Vela acknowledgment.

“How much of this can you prove in a hearing?” she asked.

“Kite has no signer and no return carrier. Bracken inherits offices and predicts closure from silence. Juniper’s reasoning comes from a model whose confidence depends on Vela’s chosen weighting.”

“Does Meridian label any of that?”

“In raw provenance.”

“Does the ballot?”

“No.”

Osei secured her harness to the well rail. “Quon’s counsel will say emergency scheduling relies on representative institutional continuity. They will say councils remain free to revise when their observed votes arrive.”

“Revision after commitment does not restore the choice.”

“That sounds like argument.”

“It is sequence. If an action remains reversible, an observed reply can still govern it. If the action cannot be undone, a later correction changes only the record.”

Osei’s gaze rested on Juniper’s pane. “Has their reply arrived?”

“Only receipt.”

“So you cannot yet show the forecast wrong.”

“I do not need it to be wrong. Nobody signed it.”

“For law, perhaps. For eighteen thousand people deciding whether to risk a braking plan, prediction and outcome diverging will carry more force.”

Anja disliked the sentence because it measured public belief accurately. A perfect forecast still lacked consent. A visible contradiction would make that easier to see without making it more true.

“What do you need from me?” Osei asked.

“Extend the local preservation order. The existing freeze expires tonight.”

“Vela has demanded release.”

“They sent a request.”

“They sent a request six hours ago. Their next packet arrives in fifty-eight minutes.”

Sumi checked the receive forecast. “Fifty-six, given current lock.”

Osei looked at her. “You have read the carrier?”

“I have watched its acquisition signal. Content remains a little over one billion kilometers away from us, Captain.”

Dry humor moved across Osei’s face and left. “Fifty-six minutes, then.”

Anja showed her the generated Kite print. “If we release the freeze, the raw construction logs return to routine retention. Vela can reclassify them as temporary model artifacts.”

“Can they delete protected packets?”

“Not the carrier records. They can detach the render history from the legal ballot after the temporary logs expire.”

“How long?”

“Thirty-six hours.”

Osei considered the panes. “I will not turn a narrow evidentiary hold into indefinite refusal of fleet oversight.”

“Give me forty-eight hours.”

“That carries us past the next Crown commitment review.”

“Yes.”

“There is your problem.”

The hull vibration rose under their feet. Nacre had begun another alignment correction ordered through Vela’s physical coordination channel. Osei watched a thermal warning appear on her wrist display. She acknowledged it, delegated a coolant adjustment, and waited for the confirmation. Her work kept the ship alive inside the system Anja was accusing.

“We can preserve coordination without accepting its legal fiction,” Anja said.

“You have said that. You have not yet shown me where the separation holds under failure.”

“This freeze does not touch drive control.”

“Today.”

Sumi’s receive board chimed.

A carrier lock formed from Vela. The source stamp preceded its arrival by forty-eight minutes. Nobody in the well spoke while the packet crossed the last verification steps. Hull node one agreed with the photonic checksum. Node two agreed. Node three reported no thermal offset.

Osei remained beside Anja as Sumi opened the command envelope.

Director Yselle Quon’s office had signed it. Concord counsel had countersigned. The packet cited synchronization emergency authority, Crown commitment integrity, and interference with a completed causal quorum. It did not answer the Mote evidence. It did not address Kite’s absent signer, Bracken’s inherited offices, or Juniper’s generated reasoning.

The final paragraph contained one operative instruction.

Sequence Auditor Anja Kest will release the local sequence freeze immediately upon receipt of this order and restore all Crown quorum records to standard Meridian custody.

The receipt clock advanced beneath the text.

Vela’s order had arrived. Anja had not obeyed it yet.

Chapter 09 — Annex Twelve

The old annex refused to open on Levan Oris’s desk.

His legal pane showed a white field, a valid document seal, and no text. The retrieval clock continued counting as if more data might arrive from a cabinet six meters away. Vela’s counsel archive occupied the wall behind him in mirrored stacks. No transmission delay separated him from the signed original. Only the current permissions layer stood between the seal and his eyes.

Levan tried the public concordance. Meridian supplied the operative summary at once.

EMERGENCY SYNCHRONIZATION ANNEX TWELVE
Forecast testimony may support continuity-preserving action during a declared synchronization emergency pending observed reply.

The summary matched the language used in Director Quon’s order to Nacre. It also omitted the sentence Levan remembered writing with his own hand.

He closed the pane before anyone crossing the bright counsel gallery could see it.

Vela simulated a noon that belonged to no star outside. Ceiling panels cast hard light over pale floors. Beyond the gallery glass, the Meridian chamber descended through three decks of ceremonial displays: convoy trajectories, common-time bars, nine habitat seals arranged around the Crown alignment. Eight seals glowed green. Nacre’s had changed to amber when Captain Osei refused to release her auditor’s freeze.

Levan carried his authority wafer to the mirrored stacks.

The archive recognized his hand but not his request. A line appeared across the cabinet.

CURRENT IMPLEMENTATION CONTROLS. SUPERSEDED DRAFTS REQUIRE DIRECTORATE PURPOSE.

He selected active legal review. The cabinet asked for a docket. He entered Nacre’s challenge number, though no formal challenge had reached Vela. The only document in the docket came from Quon’s office: an order demanding return of Crown records to Meridian custody.

The cabinet opened three centimeters.

Inside, bound sheets lay in pressure clips beside cold-storage wafers. Vela kept paper for the same reason every habitat did. A paper clause could burn, vanish, or be stolen, but it could not inherit a new definition while nobody watched. Levan pulled the 2227 signed volume free and carried it to a reading rail out of view from the gallery.

Annex Twelve began as a response to pump failure.

Twenty-two years earlier, a relay fault had left Orison without current load requests from Bracken while their shared coolant exchange approached a valve transition. Meridian correctly predicted Bracken’s request. Orison acted on the forecast, kept both habitats inside thermal margin, and later received the observed instruction matching it almost word for word. The operation saved six hundred tonnes of usable coolant.

The legal dispute came afterward. Orison had acted without Bracken’s instruction. Bracken agreed with the act but refused the precedent. The Concord council assigned Levan, then a junior counsel, to draft a boundary between anticipation needed for survival and consent reserved to councils.

His paper copy retained the boundary.

12.4 Forecast testimony may schedule or initiate only work whose material and legal effects remain fully reversible upon receipt of observed testimony. A forecast shall not acquire the signature, standing, or attributed speech of the person, office, or cohort modeled.

The next clause required visible labels on every forecast output. Another barred forecast responses from causal quorum. Levan read all three twice.

No ambiguity supported Kite’s generated thumbprint. No interpretive tradition supported Bracken’s inherited offices. The Juniper ballot described in Nacre’s preservation notice could not enter quorum under any sentence in the signed annex.

Levan unfolded the adoption sheet bound behind section 12.4. Bracken's delegate had objected to the word fully, arguing that no material act could be reversed without some residue. Kite's counsel proposed substantially reversible instead. Levan's younger handwriting filled the margin: the residue itself must remain below whatever threshold transferred legal or physical risk to the modeled party. The final conference retained fully and added a test. An action qualified only if an observed refusal could restore every party's prior options without demanding new consent.

Nine delegate marks appeared beneath that resolution, each linked to a receipt path. Their source clocks covered thirty-seven hours. The old council had waited for the slowest answer before adopting an emergency rule designed to permit speed. Nothing about that delay looked inefficient on paper. It looked deliberate.

He checked the digital checksum against the bound volume. Both agreed. The omission lived in current retrieval, not in a damaged archive or disputed original. Anyone opening the complete annex could read the bar against quorum. Almost nobody opening a Crown docket received the complete annex.

He turned to the amendment leaves clipped behind the original.

Most recorded narrow changes. One extended forecast scheduling to life-support exchanges between habitats. Another permitted captains to stop predicted work without waiting for the modeled ship’s objection. Levan had signed both. A 2244 amendment allowed institutional continuity fields to route maintenance requests after an officer changed roles. He had argued for that language after Kite lost a pump authorization during an election handover.

The phrase forecast testimony first entered the summary five years later.

Levan found his own signature under the memorandum.

He remembered the drafting session. Vela’s synchronization office wanted consistent terminology across operations. Engineers called the output predictive continuity. Dispatchers called it assumed request. Lawyers used forecast statement, modeled instruction, and provisional posture in neighboring clauses. Levan had insisted on one term so every use could carry the same warning label. Forecast testimony, he wrote, named modeled words precisely because it distinguished them from observed testimony.

The memorandum preserved that purpose in its first paragraph.

Its interface appendix did not.

A table mapped forecast testimony into five systems. Pump schedules accepted it as provisional instruction. Thermal routing accepted it as expected load. Maintenance queues accepted it as planning consent. Council displays accepted it as institutional posture. The final row addressed legal status and read NOT OBSERVED; NO QUORUM EFFECT.

Someone had drawn a thin vertical line through the final row. The paper carried no initials.

Levan angled the sheet toward the reading light. The line came from a printer defect, not a hand. A cold-storage copy would settle whether it appeared at issuance. He inserted the corresponding wafer into the rail.

The cabinet displayed revision history. The original table included the final row. Current implementation stopped one row earlier. A migration in 2248 had moved legal status into the Crown Decision Interface, outside Annex Twelve’s document family. The migration note classified the change as administrative because it altered location, not substance.

He opened the interface rule.

The legal-status prohibition had not migrated.

In its place, Crown’s interface treated a continuity action as provisional until physical commitment. Forecast institutional posture could satisfy the provisional authorization field. When commitment began, the interface converted every provisional authorization still above confidence threshold into current authority. An observed reply could replace it later, but no rule restored the annex’s prohibition on quorum effect.

Levan gripped the rail until its edge pressed into his palm.

The annex still said reversible. The interface had changed when reversibility got tested.

He traced the severance through three documents. The 2248 migration removed legal status from the annex table. A 2249 emergency directive defined continuity-preserving action as any action required to preserve the continuity plan pending observed reply. Crown operations then defined commitment preparation as reversible because each individual preparatory command could be canceled. Finally, the decision interface allowed provisional authorizations to harden when the plan crossed its commitment threshold.

Each document preserved a defensible sentence. Together they reversed the signed rule.

Levan opened the interface's validation suite. The first demonstration still used the old Orison coolant exchange. A predicted Bracken request opened a transfer valve. The model assumed refusal at the earliest lawful arrival, closed the valve, and pumped the transferred coolant back. Pressure, ownership, and reserve capacity returned within tolerance. The suite marked the action reversible.

He replaced the demonstration inputs with Crown's public preparation schedule. The validator accepted forecast authorization for mirror-seed deployment. At the modeled refusal time, it canceled the next command. It did not restore propellant already spent. It did not return deployed seed material to Vela, recover lost alignment margin, or widen the remaining capture branches. Those consequences fell outside the software transaction, so the validator ignored them. A green result appeared beside a physical state that could not be unwound.

Levan changed one field: action boundary. Instead of testing a single command, he told the suite to test every causally dependent step authorized by that command. The calculation expanded through mirror placement, beamer timing, habitat attitude schedules, and reserve allocations. After forty seconds, the validator returned no result. Its rule library contained no method for reversing a plan.

He saved the test under counsel privilege. The software had not made a choice. Humans had given it a boundary small enough to pass.

A maintenance order could start on forecast testimony if a later observed refusal could stop the work and undo its effects. Crown’s interface asked only whether the current command could be canceled now. It did not ask whether accumulated preparation narrowed later choice. It did not ask whether plasma mirror position, expended propellant, or lost trajectory margin could be restored. Most damaging, it converted predicted posture into authority at the moment reversal became costly.

Levan had reviewed the 2248 migration.

His approval appeared below the technical note.

Substance retained through successor interface. No Concord amendment required.

He had checked that forecast labels remained visible to operators. He had checked that observed replies superseded model output. He had not opened the successor interface’s quorum rules. The review docket concerned document migration, not Crown planning, and he had accepted another counsel’s statement that legal controls persisted.

That excuse explained his conduct. It did not remove his name.

A door opened behind him.

Yselle Quon entered the archive rail without an aide. Her silver work jacket reflected the hard ceiling light. She looked first at the signed volume, then at the active cold-storage pane.

“I asked for a release order,” she said.

“You received one.”

“Osei has not complied.”

“Light may still be carrying her reply.”

“She acknowledged receipt through the automatic channel.”

“Acknowledging the packet does not answer it.”

Yselle rested one hand on the rail. “Do not perform for me, Levan. Nacre’s captain intends to preserve the freeze past our commitment review. I need the authority line that permits Vela to resume custody without her cooperation.”

“You already cited synchronization emergency authority.”

“Nacre says sequence preservation falls under local evidentiary control.”

“It does for a narrow hold.”

“Then determine whether Kest’s hold remains narrow.”

Levan closed the current interface pane but left the signed annex open. “Have you read the Mote supplement?”

“I read Arko’s conclusions, Ren’s exposure ranges, and Kest’s classification.”

“Do you dispute native life?”

“No.”

The answer arrived without hesitation.

Yselle looked through the archive glass toward the Meridian chamber. “I dispute the claim that one finding can suspend a deceleration architecture nine habitats need. Crown can change exposure geometry after commitment if the ion model improves.”

“Can it change without reducing capture probability?”

“Not without cost.”

“Can the current course be restored after mirror commitment?”

“No physical course can be restored in that sense. Every second closes alternatives.”

“Annex Twelve uses full reversibility.”

Her gaze moved to the paper clause. “For the forecast action.”

“The forecast action has become authorization to preserve a plan. The plan consumes alternatives.”

“Kest sent common evidence after Vela had already begun lawful preparation. Her choice of timing also consumes alternatives.”

“She sent it when Mote’s packet arrived.”

“I know. That does not give us back the braking window.”

Yselle’s defense held physical truth. Waiting cost trajectory margin. Breaking Crown without another plan risked Ione capture and food reserves. Levan had spent years advising councils that procedure could not command fuel into existence. Yet necessity could justify emergency action without manufacturing the councils’ voices. Vela possessed narrow survival authority. It wanted broader legitimacy.

“Why render council seals?” he asked.

“The condensed display uses institutional status.”

“Kite’s status carried a thumbprint.”

“The registry renderer uses the current seal form.”

“No signer produced it.”

“No human signature key was forged.”

“That answers a criminal allegation I did not make.”

Yselle’s fingers tightened once on the rail. “If every display carried the entire probability history, command would fail under provenance load. Authorized officers can open the raw chain.”

“And captains receive green quorum bars unless they ask.”

“They receive decisions in time to act.”

“Forecast decisions.”

“Forecast cooperation. Nobody has been denied the right to send an observed reply.”

Levan looked down at section 12.4. “A right to answer after the answer has hardened into authority protects the record, not the choice.”

Silence from the gallery reached them through glass: muted footfalls, chamber alerts, the faint vibration of Vela’s nonrotating command spine. The habitat led the convoy because its beamer, navigation core, and synchronization staff had been built for that burden. Yselle had inherited real responsibility along with the power she defended.

“What are you asking me to do?” she said.

He almost showed her the migration failure. Doing so would let her repair the interface, perhaps honestly, perhaps by writing a stronger emergency justification before Nacre saw the source. Counsel normally brought defects inward first. Institutions could not survive if their lawyers treated every mistaken cross-reference as public sabotage.

“I need the complete Crown commitment path,” Levan said.

“You have counsel access.”

“Operations redacted the physical thresholds.”

“Because distribution changes behavior.”

“I cannot assess reversibility without knowing what cannot be reversed.”

Yselle studied him. “Are you advising delay?”

“I am advising that your current authority claim depends on a limit the interface may not enforce.”

“May not?”

“I have not completed review.”

That statement remained true and protected him from making a confession he had not yet earned.

She released the rail. “You have two hours. After that I need either a defect with operational consequence or an opinion supporting custody restoration. We do not stop a convoy for document archaeology.”

“We might stop a commitment for governing law.”

“Find out which one you have.”

Yselle left the archive.

Levan sent the promised operations request before the door finished sealing. The reply arrived locally within two minutes because no light path separated Vela's counsel archive from its Crown deck. Operations supplied mirror-seed deployment times and beamer reservations but withheld steering tolerances under command security. Even the partial path showed three losses already incurred: attitude propellant, two seed canisters, and a maintenance window reassigned from radiator inspection. None alone forced Crown to continue. Together they reduced what Vela could choose next.

He marked each loss against section 12.4's prior-options test. Propellant could not be replaced before commitment review. Seed canisters could not be recovered from their outbound paths. The radiator window could be restored only by moving another task, which transferred risk to a crew that had never entered the Crown vote. Current preparation had therefore produced material effects, though Levan still lacked enough engineering evidence to label the entire course irreversible.

A second operations note warned that revealing exact tolerances might aid a habitat attempting unsynchronized maneuver. Levan understood the restriction. He also saw its legal effect: councils had been asked to consent through a record that withheld the threshold deciding whether their later refusal could matter.

Then he retrieved every source named in the migration chain. The Crown interface rule had two authors from synchronization operations and one from counsel. His own review note cited a drafting archive stored outside the current legal family. The archive key began with his personnel number.

He opened it.

Early versions of the terminology memorandum showed his objections in margin notes. On revision three, an engineer proposed allowing forecast posture to “stand for institutional assent.” Levan had rejected the phrase.

Standing for assent exceeds scheduling. Retain forecast label. Require observed authority before any effect that cannot be unwound in material fact, not merely canceled in software.

Revision four carried the accepted language. It preserved the warning and added an example: a valve opened on forecast instruction must be capable of closing without net transfer if observed refusal arrived. Levan had signed that version.

The source reference did more than show what he intended. It defined reversibility through material consequence, the exact question Crown’s current interface avoided.

He checked access logs. Nobody had opened revision four in eleven years. Current concordance searches returned only the signed memorandum and the truncated implementation table. Anja would not find the drafting archive through ordinary Annex Twelve provenance. She would need the source reference.

Levan composed a message to Nacre’s sequence office.

He began with an explanation, deleted it, and began again.

A confession would require him to state what he had done, what he knew, and when he knew it. He still lacked the commitment thresholds Yselle had promised. He did not know whether Crown had crossed the material line. He knew only that the governing interface could cross it without seeking observed authority.

He chose a narrower communication permitted between counsel and sequence auditors: source clarification relevant to an active classification dispute.

For interpretation of ES-A12 “reversible,” consult VELA-COUNSEL-DRAFT/2217-ORIS/TERM-MEMO/R4, margin resolution 6 and material-effect example 2. Current concordance omits this drafting source. Compare against Crown Decision Interface migration 2248.311.

The date embedded in the archive key referred to its internal series, not Annex Twelve’s adoption year. A reader without Vela access could request the source by identifier and verify its checksum through the counsel mirror. The reference accused nobody. It admitted nothing. It pointed.

Levan addressed it to Anja Kest’s official audit key, then removed the official route.

That route would copy Yselle’s compliance mirror before transmission. A protected counsel-to-auditor inquiry route still existed for disputes involving directorate records. Its use would appear in the access ledger but hide content until a hearing or mutual release. Levan selected it and attached no documents.

Vela’s board calculated the path to Nacre: fifty-one minutes under current separation. The earliest acknowledgment could come back fifty-one minutes after receipt. Any substantive question would take longer.

He signed only the source reference.

The transmission pane asked whether the message constituted counsel opinion.

Levan selected no.

It asked whether it constituted observed testimony.

He selected no again.

At 16:44 Vela source, the private reference left for Nacre.

Chapter 10 — Reversible Means Reversible

Levan Oris’s private reference reached Nacre while Anja was copying the freeze into Vela’s custody channel.

She stopped with her thumb above the release field. Osei had extended local preservation through one watch, then ordered Anja to comply unless new source evidence arrived. The order respected Vela’s demand without surrendering Nacre’s authority to judge its own records. It also left fourteen minutes before the extension expired.

The incoming packet carried no attachment and no argument. One line pointed into a Vela drafting archive that ordinary concordance did not expose.

Sumi read the address over Anja’s shoulder. “He hid a door number in a legal citation.”

“He used a protected route.”

“Does that make it less hidden?”

“It makes the hiding reviewable.”

Anja checked the carrier envelope. Vela source 2249.185.16:44:02. Nacre receipt 17:35:19. Fifty-one minutes of light lay between those events. Levan could not know whether she had received the order to release her freeze when he sent the reference. He could know that Nacre had disputed custody and that Annex Twelve governed the disputed ballots.

She added the packet to the frozen sequence and sent Osei a notice that new counsel provenance had arrived. Osei extended preservation for six hours, no more.

The source itself remained on Vela.

Anja entered Levan’s identifier into the counsel mirror. The mirror returned a valid catalog path, restricted content, and a checksum-request option. Because Levan had named the source through a protected inquiry, Nacre could request a read-only copy without Yselle’s approval. The request still had to cross to Vela. A local permission did not move distant data.

At 05:48 Nacre time, Sumi transmitted the request.

The earliest possible arrival stood fifty-two minutes ahead under current separation. If Vela’s archive answered automatically, the copy could reach Nacre after another fifty-two. If a human reviewed it, the interval would lengthen.

Anja released none of the freeze.

She opened the custody preview Vela's order had required her to accept. The preview kept Nacre's raw packets but returned transformation logs to standard retention. Kite's generated seal would remain as a ballot image. The renderer steps that produced it would expire after thirty-six hours. Bracken's culture adjustment would persist as a final confidence value, while the excluded unresolved cases would fall out of the active model cache. Juniper's weighting history would collapse into the current emergency profile.

Sumi copied the preview's consequences onto paper. “They are not asking us to destroy evidence.”

“They are asking us to stop preserving the parts that show how the evidence changed category.”

“Standard custody everywhere else does that automatically.”

“That may be their defense.”

Anja tested partial release. The custody interface offered to preserve source packets while releasing model artifacts, or preserve local artifacts while restoring the legal record to Meridian. Neither option held the relation between them. A ballot without its construction history could not show substitution. A construction history detached from the ballot could be dismissed as unused diagnostic work.

She requested a six-hour exception tied to Levan's source reference. The local system allowed Osei's authority to grant it. Vela's mirror would receive notice only after light crossed the gap. During that interval, Nacre's copy remained lawful under Nacre's clock even though Vela's current display would expect release.

The dispute itself now had two accounts. Anja preserved both.

She spent the first wait building a test she could apply to whatever came back. Annex Twelve’s public text allowed forecast testimony for reversible scheduling. Vela’s current implementation defined continuity work by whether each command could be canceled. The missing question concerned material consequence. If a command stopped but left fewer available futures, had it been reversed?

Sumi opened the Crown preparation ledger on a separate pane. Nacre had received seventeen operational instructions since the ecology supplement. Four altered attitude. Six reserved power. Three coordinated heat rejection. The rest assigned communication windows and crew readiness. Every instruction carried a cancellation control.

Anja selected the first attitude change. Canceling its next increment would stop further rotation. It would not refund propellant or place Nacre on its earlier vector.

“Operations always works like that,” Sumi said. “Nothing moves backward.”

“Then the annex cannot mean physical history disappears.”

“What can it mean?”

“That refusal restores the choices the forecast action would otherwise take away.”

She wrote that as a hypothesis, not a conclusion.

Tomas joined them by secure local line from the drive-model room. His image shook each time a pump started near his terminal. Anja showed him the sequence of Crown commands without showing Levan’s private reference.

“Which one commits the path?” she asked.

“There is no single command that does everything.”

“Which one removes an option?”

“All thrust removes options.”

“An option we could otherwise still use.”

He rubbed his face. “Mirror seeds complicate it. Once deployed past recovery geometry, changing the beam pattern costs unplanned seed mass. Once Vela reserves the beamer cycle, every habitat schedules around it. Once we expend attitude propellant to enter the shared solution, leaving it costs more propellant than entering.”

“Can Crown still stop?”

“Physically, yes.”

“Can it stop and restore the prior capture choices?”

Tomas looked away from the line toward his model. “Not all of them.”

“How many remain?”

“I need Vela’s steering tolerances. Public data gives a range too broad for an answer.”

“Can you identify whether the range has narrowed?”

“Yes.”

“Do that. No alternative design yet.”

He gave her a tired half-smile. “You are assigning a physicist not to solve something.”

“I am assigning you not to claim what you have not solved.”

“That may take longer.”

The archive request reached Vela at 06:40 by Nacre’s estimate. No display announced receipt because the counsel mirror did not send automatic acknowledgments across private routes. Anja watched the path become causally available to Vela, then waited for an event only Vela could produce.

During the second interval, she examined Levan’s message grammar. He had written consult, compare, and omits. He had not written I drafted, I objected, or the interface violates. The reference carried help shaped as deniability.

At 07:11, Osei called.

“Your new evidence?”

“Still on Vela or in flight.”

“Then you have a reference, not evidence.”

“Yes.”

“Quon has issued a noncompliance notice against me.”

“Observed?”

Osei’s expression hardened. “Do not use vocabulary as armor.”

Anja lowered her eyes. “You are right.”

“What does the source need to prove?”

“That Annex Twelve tests material consequence, not software cancellation. If it does, Crown preparation exceeded the annex when it removed choices before observed votes arrived.”

“Exceeded does not mean committed.”

“No.”

“What would?”

“A dependency chain that makes cancellation impose a new survival risk or require new consent.”

Osei considered. “Six hours includes the wait you chose.”

“The wait came from distance.”

“You chose the source. Make it worth the clock.”

The line closed.

At 07:34, a carrier acquired on the counsel channel. The source stamp showed an automatic archive response fifteen seconds after Nacre’s request reached Vela. Light had carried the copy home for fifty-three minutes. No one on Vela had delayed it. No one there could have accelerated it.

Sumi verified the photonic envelope and opened the copy in a local pane.

Revision four contained Levan’s margin resolution exactly where he had pointed. An engineer’s draft allowed forecast posture to stand for institutional assent. Levan’s note rejected the phrase. Below it, his material-effect test required observed authority before an effect that could not be unwound in material fact. The valve example defined unwinding as restoration without net transfer.

A second margin note extended the test beyond physical inventory.

Where preparatory work changes the modeled party’s lawful options, reversal requires restoration of those options without further burden upon that party.

Anja checked whether the sentence belonged only to Levan's abandoned language. Revision four carried a review ladder from operations, counsel, and three habitat delegates. Each reviewer had resolved the margin without deleting it. The final memorandum referenced the revision by archive key. Annex Twelve's adoption sheet then incorporated the memorandum's definition of full reversibility. No later amendment struck the chain. Current concordance simply stopped following it when the 2248 migration moved legal status into another document family.

Sumi requested the adoption sheet's receipt proofs from Nacre's old Concord cache. Six remained local. Three came from Vela's counsel mirror as checksum attestations rather than full packets. Their source clocks spanned more than a day, matching the signed history Levan had cited. Nobody had synchronized those old votes into an apparent instant. The annex gained authority only after every reply arrived.

They tested the copied revision against Levan's catalog checksum. Nacre's received copy supplied one value. The catalog listing supplied another abbreviated value. Both shared the same leading block, but only Vela's protected archive held the complete checksum. Anja could prove that the copy followed Levan's reference and matched the public catalog family. She could not yet prove that no byte changed between Vela's stored source and Nacre's receipt.

“That leaves him holding one end of the proof,” Sumi said.

“Until he signs the full value.”

“Would a public copy request force it?”

“It would notify Quon and open a custody dispute before the reply reached us.”

“So we ask him.”

Anja read the sentence until its parts stopped moving.

Sumi touched the signature block. “Oris signed revision four.”

“Draft signatures establish authorship, not enactment.”

“The adopted annex uses fully reversible.”

“We need the link.”

They compared revision four against the signed memorandum and its adoption history. The final language incorporated the material-effect example by reference, but current concordance did not surface that reference. Levan’s drafting note therefore supplied interpretive provenance rather than a new rule. The governing words had always remained in the annex.

Reversible meant reversible.

Anja opened a challenge packet to Levan.

Your R4 note defines reversal as restoration of material and lawful options without new burden on the modeled party. Confirm whether this interpretation governed the adopted ES-A12 language. Identify any later observed amendment that replaced it.

She added the source checksum generated by Nacre’s received copy, signed the question at 07:52, and sent it toward Vela.

The next answer could not return before 09:38. She marked that floor on paper.

Tomas called at 08:20. He had compared Nacre’s observed Crown commands against the public commitment schedule. Preparation had crossed two thresholds. First, Vela had deployed mirror seeds beyond recovery geometry. Second, Nacre and three other habitats had spent attitude propellant entering a shared beamer solution. Crown could still be canceled, but the convoy could not regain the previous centralized capture branch with remaining public reserves.

“Does canceling create survival risk?” Anja asked.

“Every braking change creates risk.”

“Does this one create risk that did not exist before preparation?”

“Yes. If Crown stops now without replacement braking, our Ione capture probability falls outside the accepted corridor.”

“Can you sign that?”

“I can sign the narrowing. I cannot sign the final probability without Vela’s tolerances.”

“Sign only the narrowing.”

Tomas sent a local source statement. It identified commands, propellant expenditure, and lost recovery geometry. He did not mention Thale. The ecology finding explained why the fleet might refuse Crown. It did not prove whether refusal still had material power.

Anja made him walk each threshold backward. For mirror seed, the last recoverable geometry had passed eleven hours earlier. Canceling guidance would prevent further placement, but the released canisters would continue toward Corva. For habitat attitude, Nacre could rotate out of the shared solution. Reentry would demand reserve propellant already assigned to later capture correction. For Vela's beamer reservation, canceling Crown would free power but not restore the maintenance and navigation windows moved around it.

“Which consequence belongs to Juniper?” she asked.

Tomas frowned. “Physically?”

“Under the prior-options test.”

“Juniper entered the attitude solution on forecast authorization. Leaving now spends its reserve. Staying binds it to a beam path its council had not reviewed when the commands began.”

“Could Nacre pay that reserve for them?”

“Propellant does not move between habitats on this timescale.”

“Could Vela restore their prior option by changing the central path?”

“Only by narrowing another habitat's margin. That would transfer the burden.”

The test did not require time to reverse. It required consequences to stop binding people who had not chosen them. Crown failed that test even before anyone proved whether the entire maneuver could be safely abandoned.

Sumi added each threshold to a table with four columns: forecast authorization, physical act, option lost, burden of restoration. The last column never read zero.

Anja attached Tomas’s statement to a private working chain. The elements now ran in causal order. Forecast ballots supplied provisional authority. Provisional authority released preparation commands. Preparation spent material and narrowed lawful options. The interface planned to convert provisional authority into current authority at commitment. Observed votes had not yet completed the quorum.

The system had not merely predicted a decision. It had used the prediction to create conditions that made a different decision harder to honor.

On her paper table, Anja drew a line after the first unrecoverable seed release. Everything before that mark could have stopped under the annex's ordinary scheduling power. Everything after depended on authority the forecast ballots could not supply. The line did not prove what the fleet should choose. It showed when someone had chosen in the councils' absence.

At 09:39, no answer had arrived.

Sumi pointed to the path estimate. “Separation changed during attitude correction. Add two minutes.”

Anja corrected the floor on paper. Meridian’s master display had retained the earlier estimate. It showed Levan’s reply as overdue even though light still crossed the added distance.

At 09:42, the private carrier locked.

Levan’s answer contained thirty-eight words.

Confirmed as to original adopted interpretation. I find no observed Concord amendment replacing the material-effect or restored-options limits. Administrative implementation after 2248 may apply a narrower transaction boundary. This reply does not constitute public counsel opinion.

He had confirmed the rule and withheld public standing in the same packet.

Anja sent the next question at 09:51.

Crown preparation has deployed unrecoverable mirror seed and expended habitat attitude reserves before observed quorum. Under your confirmed interpretation, can forecast authorization lawfully support further commitment? Answer with reasons capable of public release.

She included Tomas’s source statement but not his model files. Vela would receive the question after another light interval. Any answer would return near midday.

Osei came to the audit well during the wait. She read revision four, Levan’s confirmation, and Tomas’s narrowing statement in silence.

“This says prior options cannot be restored,” she said.

“Yes.”

“It does not say Crown cannot continue.”

“It says forecast testimony cannot authorize the continuation.”

“Vela may claim captain’s emergency authority.”

“They may. That would expose whose authority acts and what limits apply.”

“Would it stop the beam?”

“No.”

Osei handed back the pane. “Then do not tell my council you have stopped anything.”

“I will tell them the quorum did not authorize what has begun.”

“When?”

“When I can show the source chain without relying on a private counsel message.”

“You asked him for public reasons.”

“He may refuse.”

“Would you?”

Anja thought of the sequence certifications carrying her own name. “I did.”

Osei understood which certification she meant. “Eleven years later.”

The words landed without cruelty, which made them harder to dismiss.

By 10:47, Vela had received Anja’s second question. The private route showed only estimated availability, not acknowledgment. The well returned to ordinary work around the waiting path. A technician disputed a shift clock. A garden controller submitted a late irrigation instruction. Sumi repaired a relay index whose missing digit sent Bracken traffic toward Lumen’s queue. Each task required someone to distinguish an event from what a system expected to find.

Anja reviewed the phrase irreversible commitment path.

Tomas had not said the convoy could never leave Crown. Levan had not said stopping remained safe. The evidence supported a narrower and more dangerous conclusion. Crown preparation had crossed into a path whose prior alternatives could not be restored with current reserves. Continuing deepened that loss. Stopping required a new plan and new consent under worsened conditions. Annex Twelve could not lawfully carry the fleet across that boundary on forecast testimony.

At 11:43, a public Crown notice reached Nacre through ordinary channels.

Vela announced mirror-seed deployment complete and moved the commitment review forward by four hours. The notice cited schedule compression caused by Nacre’s evidentiary hold. It invited councils to submit observed revisions while preserving existing quorum status.

Osei forwarded one question to Anja: Does this change your classification?

Anja answered: It confirms the dependency has advanced. It does not create observed authority.

Levan’s reply arrived at 12:02.

The packet carried a private counsel seal and two attachments. His text began with refusal.

I will not release public testimony on incomplete operational records. I cannot certify Crown’s full physical reversibility or irreversibility without the steering tolerances withheld by operations.

Anja kept reading.

I can state privately that Annex Twelve does not authorize forecast testimony to remove material or lawful options before observed reply. Mirror-seed deployment and unrecoverable reserve expenditure appear to cross that limit. Further commitment cannot derive lawful authority from forecast ballots under the original annex.

He had answered the legal question while protecting himself from the public one.

The first attachment contained no operational tolerances. It carried the archive manifest for revision four. The second contained a single protected value with Levan’s signature.

Sumi opened its label.

DRAFT SOURCE INTEGRITY REFERENCE.

Anja compared the value against the checksum of Nacre’s received copy. They matched digit for digit. Levan had supplied the independent drafting-note checksum held by Vela’s counsel archive.

He still refused to stand before the fleet.

His checksum did.

Chapter 11 — Juniper Says No

Juniper’s carrier arrived thirty-seven seconds before Meridian predicted it.

The receive board marked the discrepancy amber, then offered to correct Nacre’s display to the expected time. Sumi rejected the correction. She held the lock through a brief fade while the packet crossed Nacre’s hull receivers and entered protected storage.

Anja watched the receipt clock sign each stage. Juniper source 2249.187.18:21:44. Nacre receipt 21:15:02. The gap matched the measured light path after both habitats’ recent attitude changes. No clock had failed. The packet had followed a slightly shorter geometry than Meridian’s planning estimate.

“Council channel,” Sumi said.

The envelope named the Crown destination-protection docket. It carried Juniper’s council key, a public vote seal, and three attached reasons. The vote had been produced thirty-one hours after Juniper received Nacre’s material evidence supplement. Nothing in the envelope revealed the result until they opened the signed content.

Anja asked for the raw version first.

Sumi displayed the source packet beside the forecast ballot preserved under the sequence freeze. On the left, Juniper’s actual carrier path ran from its council vault through two relay beads to Nacre. On the right, the forecast concurrence began and ended on Vela. Its only Juniper sources came from historical records already received before the current question.

“Verify the signer,” Anja said.

The public key resolved to Elian Ro, elected speaker, with six council countersignatures linked beneath his seal. Each signature carried a local event time after the ecology packet’s receipt. The packet included a deliberation record but no live images from private debate. Juniper’s law required reasons, not spectacle.

Sumi ran the photonic checksum against the hull nodes. All three agreed.

Only then did Anja open the vote.

JUNIPER HABITAT COUNCIL
OBSERVED VOTE: REJECT CROWN DESTINATION-PROTECTION AUTHORIZATION
Evidence received: Nacre supplement, Mote fragments 1–4, Crown exposure range, current food-risk schedule.
This vote does not reject fleet braking. It rejects commitment to the submitted Crown path before native-life risk and habitat food consequences receive lawful review.

The word reject occupied the place where Meridian had written concur.

Sumi let out one breath. “There it is.”

“Read the reasons.”

The first attachment came from Juniper’s food systems board. Crown’s model treated coordinated arrival as a reduction in aggregate food expenditure. Juniper did not dispute that general result. A shorter, synchronized approach reduced years of stored-calorie draw across the convoy.

The board disputed who carried the risk inside the aggregate.

Juniper supplied seed stock, microbial cultures, and living root reserves for three other habitats. Crown alignment required it to hold a high-output attitude for the beamer sequence. That posture reduced available radiator margin during two crop-light peaks. Vela’s schedule assumed the lost output could be replaced from stored food after braking. Juniper’s observed inventory showed the reserve already committed to a fungal containment failure in its southern growing deck.

The confidence model had no record of that failure. Its latest Juniper food profile had left the habitat six days before containment began.

Anja opened the source clock on the inventory attachment. The fungal notice preceded Juniper’s vote and followed the historical data used by Meridian. The event had not yet reached Vela when Vela generated the forecast concurrence.

Sumi highlighted the order. “The model could not know.”

“It did not need to know in order to predict.”

“It needed to know in order to answer this question.”

The food board’s reason remained narrow. Juniper did not claim Crown would starve the habitat. It claimed the schedule consumed reserve flexibility during an active crop risk, and that no forecast built from older votes could authorize that consumption.

The second attachment addressed Thale.

Juniper’s council accepted Nadi Arko’s finding of native life at the operational standard stated in Mote’s packet. It repeated her exclusions. No evidence established intelligence, preference, language, or capacity to consent. The council rejected any argument that those unknowns converted uncertainty into permission.

Elian’s recorded statement followed.

We grow food by acting before certainty. We isolate a root bed before we know which spore crossed the seal. We preserve seed from a failing crop before the loss reaches every tray. Precaution does not prove the feared outcome. It keeps the outcome from becoming the only proof we accept.

Mote has shown metabolism, repair, propagation, and adaptive response under Thale’s ice. Crown crosses that living system beyond the tested range. Juniper will not authorize irreversible exposure while the risk remains open and the fleet’s evidence has not completed lawful circulation.

He did not call the pellicle a person. He did not ask it to vote. He described restraint as ordinary stewardship practiced by people who could not wait for dead crops to settle every uncertainty.

The deliberation index showed how Juniper had reached him. Food systems presented first, fourteen minutes after the hearing opened. Navigation answered that delaying Crown could increase every habitat's ration years. A grower from the northern ring asked whether protecting one unproven ecosystem justified risking nine human ones. Elian did not dismiss the question. He asked the navigation witness to separate rejecting this authorization from rejecting all braking. The witness confirmed that the vote could preserve review without selecting an alternative.

Later, a storage coordinator challenged the fungal inventory. Containment might succeed before Crown's high-output posture. The food board agreed. Its claim concerned reserve risk, not certain crop loss. Juniper's council kept that uncertainty inside its reason rather than resolving it through optimism.

The final exchange concerned Nadi's evidence. One councilor proposed waiting for proof of cognition. Elian read the lines where Nadi excluded intelligence, then asked which part of Juniper's destination law required a mind before native metabolism counted as ecological constraint. No witness supplied such a rule. The council moved to the authority question instead.

Every exchange carried a source time after receipt of the common evidence. Agreement emerged from information Meridian's forecast had never held and objections its culture profile had compressed into likely closure.

The third attachment addressed authority.

Juniper denied that any earlier concurrence represented its council. It named the forecast value, 0.9971, and stated that no council officer had signed, spoken, or delegated that response. The reason identified the recency weighting Anja had found and explained why it failed under current conditions. Recent cooperation votes concerned shared water and convoy maneuvering. Older food-protection votes carried more relevance to the present risk. Native life had no prior category at all.

A final line carried Elian’s personal seal beneath the council result.

Prediction may prepare a question for us. It may not send our answer before we make it.

Anja closed the attachment before the sentence could become larger than the evidence supporting it.

“Now compare,” she said.

Sumi loaded the forecast reasoning in a second pane.

Juniper historically prioritizes convoy survival. Crown alignment reduces approach food expenditure. Forecast outcome: concurrence.

Both records cared about survival capacity. Both recognized food expenditure. Neither depended on irrationality or secret motives. The forecast's compression made institutional habit sound like a vote already cast. The divergence entered through facts, weights, and authority. Meridian used an old inventory. Juniper used its current fungal containment loss. Vela weighted recent cooperation. Juniper distinguished the current food risk from those votes. The model compressed native life into destination uncertainty. Juniper treated measured life as a reason to preserve options.

Most important, only one record came from Juniper after receipt.

Sumi loaded the newly arrived facts into a quarantined copy of the confidence model. The fungal containment event reduced predicted concurrence, but not enough to cross Vela's emergency threshold. Adding the food board's current reserve claim pushed the result lower. Nadi's life finding could not enter cleanly because the model had no native-life category. When Sumi mapped it to destination hazard, Meridian still favored concurrence. When she mapped it to irreversible habitat loss, the output favored rejection.

“Same evidence, two categories,” Sumi said.

“Neither category came from Juniper.”

She removed the improvised mapping. The model returned insufficient basis. That result described its actual knowledge better than either prediction. It could estimate how Juniper often balanced food and fleet risk. It could not decide how Juniper would classify a living moon it had never encountered.

Anja saved the experiment as analysis, not part of the observed packet. The divergent vote did not prove culture models lacked value. It showed why their outputs remained forecasts even when confidence looked decisive. New facts mattered. Deliberation created distinctions no training profile contained. A person could answer a question by changing the terms in which it had been asked.

Anja created a comparison without replacing either text. Source location. Evidence available. Acting signer. Decision time. Receipt path. Outcome. She filled each field from raw records and left interpretation for later.

Under source location, the forecast read Vela model core. The observed vote read Juniper council vault.

Under evidence available, the forecast lacked Mote’s completed fragments and Juniper’s fungal inventory. The council record listed both.

Under acting signer, the forecast had none. The council record named Elian Ro and six countersigners.

Under outcome, green concurrence faced signed rejection.

Sumi stared at the two columns. “This will be easy to understand.”

“That makes it dangerous to handle badly.”

“How do we handle it badly?”

“By saying the model lied.”

“It predicted the opposite.”

“A prediction can fail without lying. People placed it in the ballot.”

“By saying Quon changed the weights to force yes?”

“We know Vela changed the weights for schedule stability. We do not know who chose them or whether they expected this result.”

“By saying Juniper saves Thale?”

“One rejection does not decide the fleet question.”

Sumi leaned back from the pane. “You have an objection to every useful sentence.”

“I have an objection to every sentence that moves beyond the record.”

“Useful records need sentences.”

“Then write the short ones.”

Together they drafted a sequence notice.

Juniper received the common evidence supplement at its signed receipt clock. After receipt, its council produced an observed vote rejecting Crown destination-protection authorization. The earlier Crown quorum contains a forecast concurrence attributed to Juniper. The two outcomes conflict. The forecast record carries no Juniper signer or return path.

No accusation appeared. None was needed.

Before distributing the notice, Anja checked the address list on Juniper’s packet. The council had emitted separate copies toward all eight other habitats. Nacre had received its copy now. Vela’s copy followed a different path and might arrive earlier or later depending on relative positions. Nacre could not claim Vela already knew. It could send its comparison to Vela, but that new packet would begin another fifty-minute path.

Sumi touched the carrier manifest. “Juniper marked public fleet distribution. We can relay.”

“Relay the raw packet without changing source identity. Attach our comparison separately.”

“Quon will receive two Juniper packets.”

“Then she can compare their receipt paths.”

They sent the relay at 22:01 Nacre time. The raw vote retained Juniper’s source clock and seal. Nacre added only a carrier wrapper stating when it had received and forwarded the packet. The comparison notice traveled as Nacre’s own document with Anja and Sumi as signers.

Light left the hull. Public proof did not become public everywhere at once.

Nacre's relay receipt marked only local departure. Beside it, eight projected arrivals remained future events with uncertainty bands. Anja kept those bands visible. The fleet could discuss one packet on different days without any ship possessing an earlier or later version of the truth, only a different receipt history.

Anja called Osei.

The captain answered from the thermal gallery with two engineering officers visible behind her. A Crown power-reservation diagram occupied half the wall. “If this is about Levan’s checksum, put it in my morning review.”

“Juniper’s vote arrived.”

Osei looked away from the wall. “Result?”

“Reject.”

The engineering officers stopped speaking.

“Observed?” Osei asked.

“Yes. Elian Ro and six council countersignatures after receipt. Carrier and checksum verified.”

“Reason?”

“Current food reserve risk and Thale exposure. They do not reject braking. They reject commitment to Crown before review.”

Osei dismissed the officers with a glance. They unlatched from the gallery rail and left through the lower hatch.

“Send it,” she said.

Anja transmitted the raw packet through Nacre’s local command channel. No light delay separated the audit well from the gallery, though the command vault still recorded a distinct receipt event. Osei opened the vote and read without speaking. Anja watched only what the captain’s face revealed: attention, not thought.

When Osei reached the food attachment, she enlarged the inventory source. “Fungal containment.”

“Meridian’s profile predates it.”

“Could Juniper restore output before Crown power draw?”

“Their board says uncertain.”

“Do you have the crop model?”

“No.”

“Then we do not repeat the food claim as settled engineering.”

“No.”

Osei continued into Elian’s statement. “He knows how to speak to people who keep plants alive.”

“He speaks for Juniper.”

“That was not criticism.”

The captain opened the forecast concurrence beside the observed rejection. The gallery display initially aligned both under one master-time decision. Juniper’s actual vote appeared as a revision to its earlier concurrence.

“Turn that off,” Anja said.

Osei found the provenance control and changed the view to source order. The forecast remained on Vela. Juniper’s receipt followed. Deliberation occupied thirty-one hours. The signed rejection came last.

“Meridian calls this a revised position,” Osei said.

“No Juniper position existed before receipt.”

“The model held a position for them.”

“The model produced an output. Vela’s interface attributed it.”

Osei read the comparison table. “You have your divergence.”

“I had substitution before the outcomes diverged.”

“Now other people can see it.”

Anja remembered Osei making the same distinction in the audit well. Law did not need a wrong prediction. Public judgment often did.

“Will you release the freeze?” Osei asked.

“Not the construction histories. Juniper’s vote confirms they remain material.”

“Vela’s order stands.”

“So does your local preservation authority.”

“For how long?”

“Until a hearing can receive the proof.”

Osei looked toward the Crown power diagram. “You are asking me to turn an audit into a council dispute while our ship remains inside a coordinated braking path.”

“I am asking Nacre’s council to decide whether forecast ballots bind its cohort.”

“Councils do not run thermal loops.”

“They decide fleet consent.”

“Captain’s authority decides immediate survival.”

“Yes.”

The agreement stopped Osei’s prepared objection. Anja did not want the council to command pumps or attitude corrections. She wanted it to judge a quorum claim made in its name.

Osei tapped Juniper’s food-risk attachment. “If Nacre suspends Crown participation tonight, we lose thermal and trajectory margin before any replacement exists.”

“Then frame the question narrowly. Recognition of forecast ballots, not immediate physical withdrawal.”

“Vela will say law and operations cannot be separated after commitment.”

“Annex Twelve required them to remain separable while votes were forecast.”

“They did not.”

“That failure cannot justify hiding the choice from Nacre.”

A gallery alarm sounded. Osei muted it, read the thermal rise beneath the notice, and ordered a pump-speed increase through her wrist display. She waited for the local acknowledgment. The pump answered within Nacre. No other habitat’s consent entered the correction.

“That command did not need a council,” Anja said.

“It also did not claim one.”

Osei gave her a level look. “Do not use my pump to win your case.”

“I am using it to define the boundary.”

“Your boundary moves whenever consequence does.”

“Then the authority should say whose judgment moves it.”

Osei returned to Juniper’s vote. “What do you want presented?”

“The raw source and receipt clocks. The forecast ballot. The observed rejection. Levan’s drafting source if he releases it for council review. Tomas’s narrowing statement.”

“Not Pera?”

The name tightened Anja’s hands around the edge of the well.

“Not unless her record bears directly on the mechanism and can be verified independently.”

“Good.”

Osei’s approval hurt in a place praise should not reach.

“Who speaks for Vela?” the captain asked.

“Quon’s office should receive notice and the evidence list. Their answer cannot arrive before the earliest round trip.”

“Fifty minutes each way.”

“Closer to fifty-four after the last correction.”

“Juniper?”

“Its packet already speaks for its vote. If councilors ask new questions, answers require the full Juniper round trip.”

“More than five hours.”

“Under present geometry.”

Osei calculated aloud. “Notice Vela now. Earliest answer before first meal tomorrow. Publish the evidence to Nacre’s council vault tonight. Hearing after Vela’s first possible response, whether one arrives or not.”

“That preserves their chance to answer.”

“It preserves one chance. It does not grant indefinite delay.”

Anja entered the times on paper. A hearing convened before the response floor would repeat the same theft in smaller form. A hearing delayed beyond every possible objection would let Crown continue by inertia. Osei’s schedule placed the opening after Vela could receive, consider, and answer once.

Sumi sent the formal notice at 22:37. The carrier path toward Vela brightened beside the earlier Juniper relay.

Osei signed a second document locally.

NACRE COUNCIL CONVOCATION
Subject: Recognition of forecast testimony in Crown causal quorum
Evidence window opens immediately. Hearing clock begins after earliest lawful Vela response interval. Physical coordination remains under captain’s emergency authority pending council determination.

The document neither stopped Crown nor accepted it. It separated command from consent long enough for Nacre to examine both.

Anja verified Osei’s source clock and the council vault receipt. Then she released the convocation to Nacre’s inhabited rings.

Across the local board, eighteen thousand four hundred people acquired notice that the green quorum might not contain the ships it named.

Osei’s signature held at the bottom of the pane.

Nacre’s council would convene.

Chapter 12 — The Witness Order

The three clocks on Anja Kest's desk disagreed by less than a breath, which made them useful.

One showed Nacre's local sequence. A second held Vela's source frame without correction. The third had no face at all, only the pulse intervals extracted from received light. Sumi Dax had disconnected every master-time feed before first shift and laid a strip of yellow tape across each empty socket. Meridian could still supply predictions to the ship. It could not arrange this table.

Anja placed Juniper's observed rejection beside its forecast concurrence and aligned the two receipt paths by hand.

“Again,” she said.

Sumi reset the isolated reader. “You have run it fourteen times.”

“Osei's council will ask whether it works fifteen.”

“They will ask whether you changed the machine until it agreed with you.”

“That is why you reset it.”

The reader drew from Nacre's frozen hull nodes. It opened the protected packet grain, verified the photonic checksum, displayed the packet's source clock, then traced each relay reception without converting any event to master time. Juniper's council had received the common evidence supplement at 09:14 local. Elian Ro's signed rejection followed at 12:07. Every event occupied a lawful order.

Beside that chain, Meridian's concurrence carried no human emission. Its model run had begun before Juniper received Nadi Arko's fragments. The forecast entered the legal layer through ES-A12 predictive continuity, acquired the display status of testimony, and filled Juniper's place in the Crown quorum.

The first chain showed a council answering after receipt. The second showed software estimating an answer before receipt. Both ended with valid cryptographic objects. Only one ended with consent.

Sumi watched the isolated reader complete its work. “Fifteen.”

“Save the run conditions, not the result.”

“That sounds like an auditor's method for avoiding superstition.”

“It is an auditor's method for making the next person reproduce the result.”

Anja closed her eyes until Nacre's attitude motors fired. A low tremor crossed the hub from port to starboard. It reached the desk through her wrists and gave her one physical event no legal transformation could move. Day Twelve had begun with the ship correcting its Crown alignment by six microradians. In two hours, Captain Osei's council would decide whether to challenge the authority behind that correction.

Suspicion would not survive that room. Grief would fare worse. Anja needed a witness order that started with received light and ended at the illegal quorum without asking anyone to trust her interpretation between them.

She lifted the first evidence tile. “Packet identity.”

Sumi recited the proof question. “Did Juniper's rejection leave Juniper?”

“Hull scatter from three Nacre nodes matches the carrier envelope. The envelope matches Juniper's public key. Yes.”

“Receipt.”

“Did Juniper possess the common evidence before signing? Its communications vault logged receipt. Carrier delay fits the measured separation. The council signature follows local delivery by two hours fifty-three minutes.”

“Classification.”

“Observed testimony.”

“Now the forecast.”

Anja moved to the second tile. “Did a Juniper council member emit it? No human source signature exists. Meridian generated a confidence model from prior votes and public statements. The model run predates receipt of the common evidence.”

“Legal insertion.”

“ES-A12 gave the model output continuity status. A later rule mapped that status to the condensed testimony field. Quorum assembly accepted the field without requiring a human emission path.”

“Effect.”

“Juniper appeared to concur. Crown commitment preparation advanced.”

Sumi tapped the edge of the table. “You jumped from a display status to an operational effect.”

“The commitment log cites completed quorum.”

“Then give that log its own step. If Quon can call one arrow inferential, she will make the room debate the arrow instead of the chain.”

Anja added a blank tile between insertion and effect. The paper stock came from emergency navigation kits, stiff enough to hold an optical tag and plain enough to write on. She printed the commitment-log identifier in block letters. Five tiles now crossed the desk.

Packets. Receipt. Classification. Quorum insertion. Physical reliance.

None required Pera.

Her sister had occupied the edge of every hour since Elian Ro's answer arrived. Pera's old receipt ledger had shown an observed refusal outside master closure. The resemblance sharpened Anja's attention, but resemblance could also become a solvent. If she poured eleven years of guilt across the current records, the facts would lose their edges. She had done that privately, during sleepless turns in the hub, and caught herself treating every gray status bar as an accusation.

This case offered harder material. Raw clocks remained intact. Administrative categories had changed. A forecast had entered a place reserved for an observed answer. The Crown plan had relied on that insertion.

Anja wrote those four statements on a separate card, then crossed out every adjective.

Captain Osei arrived without her council coat.

She wore a thermal-work coverall with a dark patch under one arm and carried a ceramic cup clipped shut against hub drift. Tomas Ren followed, holding a drive tablet against his chest. Neither asked for a summary. Osei stopped at the taped sockets and examined all three clocks.

“You isolated Meridian,” she said.

“From this table,” Anja replied. “Not from ship operations.”

“How long?”

“Twenty-eight minutes to demonstrate. Longer if you interrupt.”

“I intend to interrupt.” Osei secured her cup beneath the desk. “Begin with the claim you can prove, not the claim you fear.”

Anja turned the tiles face down. She had prepared a spoken opening during the previous shift and discarded it when it began to sound like an indictment. Instead she handed Osei the isolated reader's initialization wafer.

“Choose a hull node,” she said.

Osei studied her. “Two.”

Sumi routed node two through the reader. Its frozen cache carried a temperature correction from the sequence office's first hold on Day Zero. Osei selected a second number for the optical sample and a third for the relay branch. Anja did not touch the controls.

The reconstruction began with Nadi's fragmented evidence supplement. Each fragment displayed its Mote emission time, relay receipt, Vela receipt, and Nacre receipt. Gaps remained visible where the damaged antenna had dropped carrier lock. No interpolation filled them. The final common supplement left Nacre for every entitled habitat on Day Six.

“Why begin here?” Osei asked.

“Causal quorum begins with common evidence.”

“Vela says destination summaries had circulated before this supplement.”

“Different summaries reached different ships. The completed quorum cites the Day Six digest as its evidence object.”

Osei pointed to the packet identifier. “Show me.”

Anja opened the Crown commitment log. Its authorization field named the digest exactly. A green quorum mark sat beside it, rendered from nine testimony fields.

Tomas leaned close enough that his sleeve touched the desk rail. “Commitment preparation used this mark for mirror spin?”

“For mirror spin, beamer phasing, and plasma-seed release scheduling,” Anja said.

“Scheduling can still be reversible.”

“Some of it. Your office determines where preparation becomes a path we cannot undo without losing Ione.”

His mouth tightened. The question belonged to the next day's council work, not to her proof. He nodded once and moved back.

Sumi expanded the nine fields. Vela showed observed concurrence after local receipt. Nacre's provisional assent also carried a human signature, given before the full ecological supplement but marked conditional. Orison and Lumen had received the digest before answering. Five remaining fields carried predictive-continuity tags in their transformation history.

Osei interrupted. “You called one completed quorum. I see four observed signatures, one conditional signature, and four forecasts.”

“The condensed record calls it completed.”

“Does the Concord?”

“No.”

“Does the governing amendment?”

Anja brought up Annex Twelve's surviving language. “It permits forecast testimony for continuity-preserving action pending an observed reply. The drafting restriction limits that authority to reversible scheduling. A later administrative definition maps continuity-preserving action to any preparation required by an existing plan.”

“Signed by whom?”

“Director Quon's office, Concord counsel, and three delegates. One delegate's authorization also carries a forecast classification.”

Osei left the silence untouched. Tomas looked from one clock to another as though the offsets might supply an engineering quantity he could use.

After several seconds, the captain asked, “Can you prove malice?”

“No.”

“Can you prove Quon knew Juniper would reject?”

“No.”

“Can you prove the confidence model performed badly by its own standard?”

“Not across the full forecast set. Juniper diverged because the model lacked complete destination evidence. Other forecasts may match later observed votes.”

“What can you prove?”

Anja turned over the five tiles one at a time. “The raw source and receipt clocks remain intact. Legal classification changed forecast outputs into testimony fields. Quorum assembly accepted those fields without observed emission paths. Crown preparation relied on the assembled quorum. Juniper's later observed vote contradicted the answer already assigned to its cohort.”

“Reproduce the change.”

Sumi loaded a copy of Juniper's forecast into the isolated reader. Without ES-A12, the object appeared in an operations pane as a predictive schedule aid. It carried probability 0.9971 and an uncertainty note concerning native metabolism. No signature line appeared. The quorum assembler rejected it for lack of observed testimony.

Next Sumi enabled the transformation rule as it had existed during Crown processing. The object moved into a testimony field. Its confidence value collapsed behind a green status bar. A synthetic receipt event, built from predicted delivery, satisfied the evidence condition. Quorum assembly accepted the field.

Nothing in the packet changed. Nothing in Juniper changed. Classification alone moved the object across the legal boundary.

Osei asked Sumi to reset the reader. She chose new hull nodes, new scatter samples, and a different forecast ballot from Bracken. The same transformation occurred. Bracken's culture model had predicted assent based on radiation-allocation votes taken before the destination survey. No observed reply had yet reached the frozen record when its concurrence entered quorum.

“Again,” Osei said.

They used Kite. Its council later concurred, but its actual signature arrived seven hours after the Crown record claimed its vote. The model had guessed correctly. Legal order still failed.

Osei folded her hands against the desk edge. “That distinction will be hard to keep in a frightened room. People hear that Kite agreed and ask what harm came from using the answer early.”

“Time to consider the evidence belonged to Kite even if its decision matched,” Anja said.

“Do not give me doctrine. Give me consequence.”

“Kite could have attached conditions. It could have changed its representative. A pressure failure could have delayed council. Any of those events would alter the authority or meaning of its reply. The forecast contained none of them because they had not happened.”
