Tomas turned his tablet so Osei could see its drive trace. “Crown control treats a concurrence as more than a yes. It schedules how much each habitat contributes and when. A condition can change mass release, thermal reserve, or allowable spin. Guessing the vote also guesses the engineering terms.”

“Better,” Osei said. “Put that in the hearing.”

Anja watched the captain gather the tiles into a line. Osei did not arrange them by master time. She kept each event within its own frame and laid the receipt paths between them. Her engineer's hands understood dependencies before terminology.

“What answer does Vela have?” Osei asked.

“Operational necessity. Annex Twelve. Confidence high enough to preserve the plan. They may also argue that provisional acts remain reversible until ignition.”

“Are they right?”

“About necessity, perhaps. About confidence, irrelevant. About reversibility, Tomas has not finished.”

Osei glanced at him.

He rubbed one thumb along the tablet's cracked corner. “Alignment can be undone in the abstract. In the actual trajectory, every hour spent on Crown closes other braking geometries. An abort after the wrong threshold does not restore our previous options.”

“How long until you can show that?”

“Tomorrow, if the drive model stops pretending every habitat carries Vela's reserve.”

“Make it stop.”

The formal council convened in Nacre's thermal gallery because its ordinary chamber had lost cooling priority.

Condensate ticked inside the wall panels. Twenty-one council members sat among live heat-load displays, their faces lit by the same green and amber bars that managed Nacre's pumps. Osei took no elevated seat. She stood beside a manual valve wheel while Anja and Sumi rebuilt the witness order on a portable isolated reader.

Anja opened with the Juniper packet rather than the accusation.

She asked the agriculture delegate, Mara Venn, to select the hull node and optical sample. Venn chose without consulting anyone. The reader verified Elian Ro's signature, followed his receipt path, and placed his rejection after receipt of the common evidence. Anja then showed the earlier forecast concurrence and its missing human emission.

Questions came quickly.

Could clock drift account for the gap? No. Each causal link used local source and receipt clocks; measured light paths agreed within uncertainty.

Had Meridian forged Elian's signature? No. The observed rejection remained intact. The system had supplied a separate forecast object to an administrative layer.

Could Juniper have delegated in advance? No delegation accompanied the forecast. Juniper's charter barred binding destination votes before evidence receipt.

Did Annex Twelve authorize prediction? For reversible scheduling, yes. For an irreversible fleet commitment, no lawful text Anja had found.

Councilor Sen, who represented Nacre's outer residential ring, held up both hands. “You keep saying irreversible. We have not burned.”

Tomas answered from the second row. “Commitment can precede ignition. A ship that turns onto one braking corridor spends the others. I can show the loss, but not before tomorrow.”

“Then today we have an irregular ballot and a possible future harm.”

“Today you have a completed-quorum claim built from events that did not occur,” Anja said.

Sen looked at Osei. “Captain, can Nacre survive if every operational decision waits thirty-one hours?”

“No,” Osei said.

The answer shifted the room toward her more effectively than reassurance could have done.

She continued. “Pumps may start on forecast demand. Thermal exchanges may anticipate a neighbor's load. Attitude control may schedule around expected beam use. Those acts preserve life because operators can reverse them when observed instructions arrive. Anja's case concerns whether prediction may occupy a council's legal seat.”

Mara Venn asked, “If we strike the forecast ballots, do we lose all Crown coordination?”

“No decision today will cut physical channels,” Osei said. “We are deciding whether Nacre recognizes a quorum assembled through forecast testimony.”

Anja kept her hands clear of the reader. The proof had reached the point where it no longer belonged to her. Council members repeated the reconstruction themselves. Sen chose Kite and found a correct prediction arriving before the person. Venn chose Bracken and found evidence delivery supplied by a future model event. The water delegate chose Nacre's own conditional assent and verified its human signature.

That last packet produced no anomaly. Its clean sequence mattered. Without lawful examples, the corrupted record might look like an argument against synchronization itself.

Mara Venn asked to compare the same ballot in master time. Sumi restored a display feed on a separate council screen but kept it outside the isolated reader. Meridian pulled Nacre's signature backward by eleven seconds to account for a routine clock correction, then placed it after the preliminary evidence receipt. The order remained lawful in both views.

“Then master time can be accurate,” Venn said.

“It can render an accurate operational account,” Anja replied. “The correction itself has a signed rule, an uncertainty bound, and the local events remain available beneath it.”

Sen pointed to Juniper's forecast. “What makes that conversion different?”

“It does not translate an event that occurred. It supplies events that have not occurred: future receipt, future deliberation, future answer. Then classification hides their modeled origin.”

A habitation delegate near the rear raised a practical objection. Her cohort had used forecast water demand for eleven years without a disputed allocation. If every estimate needed a bright warning, operators would drown in provenance.

“Keep the estimates,” Anja said. “Keep condensed displays where reversibility protects the people affected. But when a field claims to contain observed testimony, require an observed source path. One rule at one boundary. Operators need not inspect every clock conversion to know whether a person has spoken.”

The delegate looked toward Osei. “Can that boundary run without Meridian?”

“It already does,” Sumi said. “Every signed reply carries an emission path. Quorum assembly stopped requiring the path when predictive continuity entered its testimony map. Restoring the requirement removes a shortcut. It does not remove synchronization.”

A maintenance alarm flashed on the gallery wall. For eleven minutes, debate stopped while Osei reassigned cooling flow to a nursery sector. She made the decision on predicted occupancy because waiting for a door-by-door census would have raised compartment temperature. The valve opened. A technician confirmed the effect. Nobody pretended the forecast had become a resident's vote.

When the hearing resumed, Anja showed the commitment log's reliance on completed quorum. The council could now follow every arrow. No hidden inference connected packet to classification, classification to assembly, or assembly to Crown action.

Suspicion had become a procedure another person could perform.

The reader chimed before the council reached a motion.

Sumi frowned at a status pane she had not opened. “That came through the live operations feed.”

Osei looked toward the yellow-taped sockets. “You said this table had no Meridian connection.”

“It does not. My communications board does.” Sumi crossed to the wall terminal. “Vela issued a synchronization update nine minutes ago by their source. Nacre receipt just completed.”

A new Crown quorum status unfolded above the heat displays. Juniper now showed observed rejection. Kite showed observed concurrence. Bracken remained forecast. Nacre's conditional assent had disappeared.

In its place, Meridian displayed a modeled response built from Osei's prior emergency decisions, Nacre's historical preference for convoy integrity, current heat load, and council voting patterns.

Confidence: 0.9846.

Classification: forecast testimony.

Expected response: concur with Crown continuity pending review.

The condensed line beneath it shone green.

Sumi read the attached legal status twice. “Nacre is predicted to concur.”

No one in the gallery asked Anja to prove what the words meant. The council had the isolated reader, the five tiles, and its own missing signature.

Across the live wall, Meridian placed Nacre's forecast into the vacant seat.

Chapter 13 — Irreversible Commitment

Captain Osei Tamm shut Crown guidance off on the drive-model room's smallest rig and watched Nacre miss Ione.

The model did not announce catastrophe. Its white trajectory line drifted by fractions across the black display, passing the system's outer capture boundary with too much velocity. Corva shrank behind it. Ione pulled, failed to keep the habitat, and let eighteen thousand four hundred people continue into interstellar dark.

Tomas Ren stood at the opposite console with both hands open. “That assumes we stop contributing now and make no replacement maneuver.”

“Which is what half my council proposed twelve minutes ago.”

“They proposed refusing Crown.”

“They used the word stop.” Osei reset the rig. “My duty includes hearing the verb people say when they are angry.”

Behind the glass partition, council members filed into the adjacent operations bay. Nacre had no chamber built for a decision that mixed constitutional authority with plasma-mirror phasing. Osei had moved the hearing to engineering so nobody could discuss refusal without seeing a trajectory.

Anja Kest waited near the isolated evidence reader, pale from too many shifts in the hub. Sumi Dax checked packet paths at the communications rail. The council had spent the previous day reproducing Anja's proof: forecast ballots entered the Crown quorum as though councils had answered. This morning they had to decide what Nacre could lawfully and physically do about it.

Osei ran the abort branch again.

Crown preparation had already spent choices. Nacre's last six attitude corrections favored the central braking corridor. Coolant mass had migrated forward. Two garden-ring storage bladders had emptied into drive reserve. Plasma-seed canisters from Vela and Kite were hours from release. None of those acts alone trapped the convoy. Together they narrowed the route back.

On the display, Nacre crossed the capture boundary and kept going.

“Show me your kindest abort,” Osei said.

Tomas loaded a branch with ideal cooperation from every ship, immediate council response, no equipment failure, and perfect beamer efficiency. The line bent toward Ione, grazed the boundary, then broke capture after a long arc.

“We still miss Ione,” Osei said.

“By less.”

“Interstellar dark does not care by how much.”

“Corva's giant planets offer a weak recovery geometry seventeen months later.”

“With what food?”

Tomas did not answer until he brought up the resource layer. The recovery branch required continuous high-wake operations through approach, then an uncertain slingshot around a planet whose moons had not been surveyed for close passage hazards. Garden output fell below replacement nutrition in month eleven. Shielding exposure rose during the turn. Even the most favorable recovery held less than one percent margin, and Tomas would not call that readiness.

“You should tell them this,” he said.

“I intend to make you tell them.”

Councilor Sen opened with a motion to suspend all Nacre participation in the Crown alignment.

Osei required him to define participation.

“Beam contribution, attitude correction, command acknowledgment, planning support, mass commitment,” Sen said. “No false quorum can bind us.”

The operations bay carried a constant fan roar from the model stacks. On the central wall, Nacre's actual trajectory glowed beside nine forecast paths. Osei looked along the council benches and counted who watched Sen rather than the display.

“Your motion cuts thermal coordination messages?” she asked.

“No. Life-safety traffic remains open.”

“Drive attitude affects radiator presentation. Does attitude guidance count as command acknowledgment or life safety?”

Sen glanced toward his technical aide. “We can distinguish them.”

“How many hours?”

“We are considering authority, Captain.”

“We are considering a ship.”

Mara Venn asked to amend the motion: Nacre would reject forecast ballots, halt irreversible Crown contributions, and continue reversible coordination. Several members voiced support before the wording reached their screens.

Osei let them hear one another. Anger had earned a place in the room. Meridian had assigned Nacre concurrence without an observed vote, and Vela's condensed record still displayed the prediction in Nacre's seat. Yet anger could not define reversible. The term had acquired too many convenient meanings.

She asked Tomas to load the present-state branch.

He stood at the model rail, shoulders drawn tighter than usual. “This shows Nacre's trajectory under completed preparation through Day Thirteen. It includes our actual attitude, committed coolant placement, current shield geometry, and Vela's latest beamer schedule. It does not assume a valid quorum.”

Sen objected. “The screen says Crown baseline.”

“The label names the physical configuration,” Tomas said. “It does not certify consent.”

A white line left the convoy's current position and entered a broad amber capture region around Ione. The margin looked generous until Tomas expanded the velocity scale. Nacre carried little tolerance for delay. Crown braking relied on sequenced beam encounters over months of approach, but the alignment decisions made now determined which encounters remained possible.

He disabled Nacre's next attitude correction.

The white path moved toward the capture region's edge.

Removing beam contribution shifted it farther. Canceling plasma-mirror coordination pushed it outside.

“Can we rejoin later?” Mara Venn asked.

“Up to a threshold,” Tomas said.

“Which threshold?”

“I can give you three answers, depending on what the other habitats do.”

“Give us all three.”

With full Crown cooperation elsewhere, Nacre could delay its next correction by nine hours and retain a narrow route back. If Juniper withheld beam power as its observed vote suggested, the delay fell below six. If Palisade also refused mass release, Nacre had less than four hours before every modeled recovery required a dangerous giant-planet passage.

Councilor Bale from the inner residential ring leaned forward. “You model other ships' refusal while objecting to modeled votes.”

“I model physical branches,” Tomas said. “I do not record a branch as their choice.”

“What confidence?”

“Confidence describes the calculations inside each condition. It cannot tell us which council will choose the condition.”

Anja kept her eyes on the model rather than Bale. Osei saw the effort that cost her. Every sentence now had to defend a boundary the fleet had blurred for years.

The food delegate requested the resource consequences of a stop. Tomas overlaid wake schedules. A clean abort did not merely extend the voyage. It forced high-wake repair and navigation crews to remain active while the habitat searched for a new capture path. Seed reserves designed for settlement entered consumption. Garden lamps stayed at approach intensity longer than planned. The cycling population lost safe wake intervals.

“How long before ration reduction?” the delegate asked.

“Under the weakest recovery branch, twenty-seven months.”

“Amount?”

“Unknown until we select a route.”

“Then do not show red bars without numbers.”

Tomas removed the bars.

Osei approved of him for doing it without defense. A model gained authority by displaying what it knew and exposing what it did not. That rule should have governed Meridian. Instead the legal layer had hidden uncertainty precisely where uncertainty belonged to other people.

The shielding representative asked about turning Nacre independently. Tomas showed the mass cost. An isolated correction would expose the habitat's forward quarter during a debris-rich segment and consume reserve propellant needed for arrival shielding. The maneuver remained possible. Its price would fall unevenly on compartments already carrying older hull plates.

Doctor Hale from medical systems requested a branch that preserved the current trajectory but reduced the awake crew assigned to Crown preparation. “You have counted food,” she said. “Count bodies kept awake to service a plan we may reject.”

Tomas added roster demand beside the trajectory. Drive supervision, radiator maintenance, mirror telemetry, emergency medicine, and communications produced a sustained requirement of seven hundred twelve awake specialists. Some overlapped ordinary ship duty. Four hundred six did not.

Hale asked him to remove the extra wake cohort.

Fault probability rose first in the radiator model. Nacre could operate with fewer people until an inspection found damage; after that point, each repair delay multiplied thermal load elsewhere. The model marked no single disaster. It accumulated missed checks until the error band swallowed the safe margin.

“Put half of them back,” Hale said.

He did. The error narrowed but remained larger than the council had accepted during any prior approach maneuver.

“How long can we carry full staffing without taking wake-years from settlement crews?”

“Thirty-two days under Crown. An abort-and-recovery branch keeps them awake for at least seventeen months.”

Hale looked along the benches. “Those are not interchangeable costs. A month of approach staffing takes sleep from people near their scheduled cycle. Seventeen months breaks medical plans for two entire cohorts.”

Councilor Bale asked whether automated supervision could close the gap.

“Meridian can predict maintenance demand,” Tomas said. “It cannot replace hands inside a radiator crawl.”

Osei requested the compartment distribution. The outer ring supplied most pressure technicians because their training shops sat near the hull routes. Central residents supplied more navigation and control staff. Any extended recovery would draw risk from one group through exposure and from another through lost cycling time. A ship-wide average concealed both.

Sen rubbed his forehead. “Every branch you show makes continued Crown cooperation look necessary.”

“Continued braking looks necessary,” Tomas replied. “Crown remains one way to obtain it.”

“Name another.”

“I can name unused assets. I cannot yet connect them into a survivable path.”

The water delegate called for a five-minute pause to inspect the reserve assumptions. Osei refused the pause and asked Sumi to display Palisade's latest public inventory instead. The file had left Palisade more than three hours earlier and carried an explicit age marker. Fabrication stock, forward shielding, repair plate, and an unallocated reserve appeared in separate columns. Crown's shared model counted only fabrication stock as maneuver mass.

“Why exclude the reserve?” Mara Venn asked.

Tomas enlarged its note. “Habitat safety floor. Palisade keeps it against erosion through arrival.”

“Could shield plate brake a ship?”

“Any mass can exchange momentum if we can shape, seed, or push it. The question concerns efficiency and what remains between people and debris afterward.”

Bale studied the age marker. “Palisade may have committed it since transmission.”

“Yes,” Tomas said. “This inventory records what they reported then. It does not tell us what they choose now.”

That careful tense changed the room. Osei heard several councilors stop speaking at once. A technical record had demonstrated the same discipline they demanded from governance: preserve when and where a fact existed. Do not let usefulness promote it into a present answer.

Sumi marked the Palisade inventory as unconfirmed and kept it visible. Even uncertain, the reserve created a direction for Tomas's search. It did not remove the cost. If Palisade gave up shielding, some cohort would face greater exposure across the remaining voyage. If it retained every plate, Nacre's alternatives narrowed.

Voices rose along the benches.

“An illegal order does not become survivable by making our children the proof,” someone shouted.

Osei struck the manual chime once. Its physical bell cut through the fans.

“Every option in this room costs people,” she said. “That fact does not validate Vela's quorum. It also does not grant us an empty refusal.”

Sen stood. “Captain, if any material cost defeats the right to refuse, consent has no meaning.”

“Agreed.”

The single word checked him.

Osei continued. “A right to refuse does not create braking. If we reject Crown, we must decide how to carry Nacre toward a survivable arrival. Authority and physics can constrain each other without becoming the same question.”

Mara Venn looked toward Anja. “Has Vela answered the witness-order case?”

“Only with an automatic notice,” Anja said. “Director Quon's office acknowledges Nacre's classification dispute and maintains continuity authority pending review. Their full response cannot arrive for at least seventy-four minutes from our filing. Longer if counsel deliberates.”

“Meanwhile the plan advances.”

“Yes.”

“Then latency rewards whoever acts first.”

Anja considered before replying. “Only if later review cannot reverse the act. That is why the commitment threshold matters.”

Osei turned to Tomas. “Show it.”

He hesitated.

That frightened her more than a bad line on the screen. Tomas usually moved through uncertainty by naming branches faster than other people could fear them. Now he enlarged the Crown schedule and placed a vertical marker twenty-two hours ahead.

“The published threshold says plasma-seed release,” he said. “Before release, Vela calls all preparation reversible.”

“And your threshold?”

“I do not have one.”

Sen made a sound of disbelief.

Tomas lifted a hand. “I have a surface, not a line. Reversibility depends on when Nacre changes attitude, whether Juniper contributes, how much shield mass Palisade retains, and whether Vela keeps the forward beamer available for a correction. The system loses alternatives gradually. Plasma release only makes the loss obvious.”

“Can we already be committed?” Osei asked.

“To Crown specifically, no. To some braking action, yes. Stopping every contribution now gives us no safe arrival branch in the current model.”

“Say it plainly for the record.”

Tomas faced the council recorder. “Merely aborting the Crown alignment can cause Nacre to miss Ione. That result does not prove Crown offers the sole survivable path. It proves refusal needs an engineered alternative.”

The room quieted around the distinction.

During recess, Osei walked the garden ring.

She had not scheduled the visit. A captain who left a council in crisis to inspect beans risked looking theatrical, but the central lift had stalled and the alternate route crossed cultivation. She took the opportunity without an entourage.

Rows of dwarf fruit trees curved overhead with the ring. Their lamps ran at approach intensity, bright enough to flatten every leaf shadow. Children from a cycling cohort clipped spent blossoms into collection bags while two gardeners tested nutrient lines. None knew that the model room had assigned their light to competing futures.

Chief gardener Pell met Osei near a tray of red-root seedlings. “Thermal office says you may cut us six percent.”

“They are preparing contingencies.”

“They used that phrase before the last two cuts.”

“What fails at six?”

“Nothing dramatic. Growth slows. We lose the buffer crop. Fungal control gets harder because we cannot heat the beds on schedule.” Pell pinched a leaf and turned it over. A pale lesion marked the underside. “At twelve percent, I choose which protein beds go dark.”

“Could you recover after a month?”

“With stored nutrient and full light later.”

“After a year?”

Pell let the leaf go. “Ask me which children you want on reduced amino balance.”

Osei accepted the rebuke. Command models rendered garden light as a smooth allocation. Pell experienced it as lesions, heat cycles, and names on nutrition lists.

A child drifted one clipping bag toward Osei along the path. Rotation kept it low, but the bag still moved oddly when a vent current caught it. She stopped it with her boot and handed it back.

“Are we missing the star?” the child asked.

Pell inhaled sharply.

“Not today,” Osei said.

“Teacher said the line went past.”

“Engineers draw lines past every day so they can find the ones that do not.”

The child considered that answer, judged it boring, and returned to the trees.

Pell lowered her voice. “Do you need the garden for braking?”

“I do not know yet.”

“Then tell us before the models turn us into reserve.”

Osei looked along the curved rows. “I will.”

That promise entered no legal register. It still bound her.

When council resumed, Vela's full response had not arrived.

Its absence occupied a status pane at Osei's request. Source transmitted, receipt unarrived. No prediction filled the empty field.

Mara Venn withdrew her amendment and offered a narrower motion. Nacre would deny legal recognition to any forecast ballot used as consent. Physical coordination channels would remain active while the council assessed which Crown steps could still be reversed. No new mass release would occur under false-quorum authority.

Sen objected that the motion delayed moral clarity.

“It creates engineering time,” Venn said.

“For what? Tomas has no alternative.”

Tomas answered before Osei could. “I have excluded assumptions, not built a plan. Palisade carries shield reserve that Crown tables treat as unavailable. Nacre has beam geometry outside Vela's central corridor. Juniper's agricultural stores change the food branch if arrival spreads. None of that forms a solution yet.”

“Could it?”

“I do not know.”

Sen looked around the room. “We are about to preserve cooperation with a plan that may sterilize native life because an engineer does not know.”

“No,” Anja said from the evidence table. “You are about to preserve channels while refusing fabricated consent. What you authorize next still requires an answer.”

Osei watched the council divide along lines Meridian's culture model would have recognized: inner-ring residents favored continuity; outer compartments feared shielding sacrifice; food delegates resisted voyage extension; younger members placed more confidence in Juniper's refusal. A prediction might reproduce the final tally. It still could not perform this hour for them.

She asked Tomas how much time physical conditions allowed before Nacre lost every branch outside the published Crown corridor.

He loaded the worst credible cooperation case, then removed the conveniences his first model had assumed. The answer settled between thirty-eight and forty-three hours, depending on Palisade's mass response.

“Use forty,” Osei said.

“For safety?”

“For command. A range becomes an excuse when people prefer its far edge.”

The motion passed without unanimity. Nacre suspended new irreversible contributions, kept thermal and navigation coordination open, and rejected any claim that its predicted concurrence carried authority. Osei recorded her observed assent after the tally.

Then she faced Tomas across the operations bay.

“You have forty hours,” she said. “Find a braking path that does not cross Thale's living band and does not lose Nacre.”

“That requires data Vela may withhold.”

“Ask every ship.”

“Palisade will want authority.”

“Request technical information, not mass. Show them the question before we ask for the sacrifice.”

Tomas looked at the white trajectory still passing beyond Ione. Responsibility had drained the friendliness from his face, leaving concentration and fear in equal measure.

Osei set the model clock to count down from forty hours.

The first second vanished while the trajectory continued outward.

Chapter 14 — Palisade's Reserve

At hour thirty-one of Osei's deadline, Tomas Ren cut a square of shielding from Nacre's maintenance locker and fed it to the test coil.

The square measured eight centimeters across and had already failed inspection. Micrometeoroid pits silvered one face. A faded stamp placed its manufacture on Palisade, twenty-seven years earlier. Tomas clamped it inside the vacuum sleeve, sealed the lid, and stepped behind the composite screen.

“Current at twelve percent,” said Jori Vale, the drive technician running the coil.

The plate trembled.

At twenty percent, it lifted from the clamp by less than a millimeter. A sensor caught the motion and plotted a ragged response curve. The alloy had not been designed as sail material. Its superconducting mesh ran in uneven layers intended to spread particle impacts through sacrificial depth. Yet the mesh accepted a magnetic field.

Tomas leaned closer to the screen. “Raise to thirty.”

“Thermal warning at twenty-six.”

“Pulse it.”

Jori sent three short pulses. The plate struck the upper restraint on the third.

A poor sail could still become brake mass if the convoy could shape enough of it, seed it into a controlled stream, and push against it with the beams already aligned for Crown. The thought had occupied Tomas since Osei showed Palisade's stale inventory. Now an obsolete patch of hull material had moved under a field.

He checked the deadline clock. Nine hours remained.

“That proves a locker plate can jump,” Jori said. “It does not prove Palisade can peel a habitat.”

“I am not asking them to peel one.”

“How much are you asking?”

Tomas looked at the mass column on his tablet and did not answer. Any number he gave would become an implied request, and Palisade had sent no current inventory. Its last public file lay more than three light-hours behind its present decisions. Osei had authorized him to request data, not sacrifice.

The communications rail chimed. Sumi Dax's message carried Nacre source time and a receipt path from Palisade.

TECHNICAL RESPONSE, PALISADE FABRICATION COUNCIL

REQUEST RECEIVED: NACRE 2249.188.02:18

RESPONSE SOURCE: PALISADE 2249.188.06:02

ATTACHMENT: FORWARD SHIELD RESERVE, LOCAL STATE AT SOURCE

NO RELEASE AUTHORIZED.

Tomas read the final line first, then made himself return to the clocks.

The response had taken the lawful path. Nacre's request crossed to Palisade. Its engineers spent nearly four hours assembling data. Their reply crossed back. Eleven hours had passed on Nacre since he asked. Nothing in the packet claimed to describe Palisade now.

He opened the attachment.

The public inventory had understated the reserve.

Palisade stored shielding in forms Crown's mass tables ignored: curved replacement tiles, powdered impact alloy, braided superconducting mesh, and unfinished laminate in fabrication cradles. Some material belonged to scheduled hull renewal. Some supported settlement construction after arrival. A separate shield reserve sat behind both categories, protected by Palisade's charter against transfer below a habitat safety floor.

Tomas pulled each local measurement into an isolated model. He did not normalize the quantities to Vela's categories. Palisade's terms carried engineering distinctions the central table erased.

Curved tiles could unfold into ragged magnetic sails if crews joined their mesh paths. Powdered alloy could seed a plasma cloud but offered poor control. Braided mesh had excellent response and almost no shielding depth. Unfinished laminate combined both properties, though cutting it from fabrication cradles would halt Palisade's hull-renewal schedule.

Jori watched the totals climb. “That is enough to matter.”

“It may be enough to ask a different question.”

“You have eight hours to answer the captain's question.”

“Osei asked for a path. A path includes what happens to the people who give us the material.”

Tomas opened Palisade's exposure model. Its forward hull carried more damage than Nacre's because the shielding habitat had spent decades fabricating convoy plate and catching debris at a leading offset. The reserve protected against statistical extremes during the remaining approach. Releasing it did not guarantee a breach. It increased the chance that an uncommon strike would exceed the layered hull.

He selected the smallest technically useful mass.

Palisade's risk margin fell by nine percent.

The number looked modest. Its distribution did not. Fabrication compartments near the forward quarter absorbed most of the added exposure. Those compartments housed the people capable of shaping the remaining shields. A sacrifice intended to save the convoy would concentrate danger on the workers performing it.

“Can we take more from Nacre?” Jori asked.

“Nacre's spare plate has the wrong mesh ratio. We can contribute coil structure and beam time. Palisade has the responsive mass.”

“Juniper?”

“Food shielding. Less reserve than their public charter requires.”

“Vela?”

“Vela's forward mass already anchors Crown.”

Jori made a skeptical sound. “Meaning Quon will call it unavailable.”

“Meaning I do not have its uncommitted state.”

Tomas disliked the correction as he made it. Anja's discipline had infected the model room. Facts now arrived with time stamps attached to every verb. Vela might possess spare mass. It might have committed that mass after its last transmission. He could model branches, not choices.

A second attachment described Palisade's fabrication limits. Even if its council authorized release immediately, crews needed thirty-six hours to separate, inspect, and link the first shield sections. Later batches could follow. The material would leave Palisade in a stream rather than one payload.

A stream suggested distributed braking.

Tomas built only the first physical branch. Palisade would cast linked shield segments ahead of its path. Vela's forward beamer, Nacre's side-array, and smaller ship emitters could magnetize and push separate portions. Each habitat would encounter a tailored drag field instead of relying on one Crown corridor. The geometry spread heat and momentum transfer across time.

It also spread arrival.

He entered current positions, conservative field efficiency, and no miraculous beam improvement. The first branch failed. Palisade's segments dispersed before Nacre could use them.

The second held the stream tighter and overheated its mesh.

A third gave Vela too much control authority; losing its beam collapsed the whole path back into a central plan.

Jori brought two meal packs from the wall heater and set one beside Tomas. “You have not moved your left hand in six minutes.”

“I am holding a constraint.”

“You are holding a dead screen corner.”

He released it. His fingers ached as blood returned.

The fourth branch used Palisade's braided mesh as field guides around heavier tiles. Nacre pushed one segment. Juniper contributed a low-power correction. Kite's array handled a later stream. The convoy did not brake as a single body. Its formation lengthened while each habitat traded velocity at a time its geometry allowed.

Tomas watched the arrival markers separate.

Nacre entered Ione capture years after Vela. Palisade arrived later still because material release reduced its own efficient braking options. Juniper's path crossed the outer system on a slower arc. The final habitat reached viable capture nineteen years after the first.

He reran the branch with optimistic assumptions. Eleven years.

Conservative field losses stretched it beyond twenty-four.

The central estimate settled near nineteen years.

“People will call that a second voyage,” Jori said.

“They will be right.”

“Some children born during approach will reach settlement as adults.”

“Some scheduled sleepers will wake under a different ship calendar from their families.”

“Crown gets everyone there together.”

“Crown crosses Thale's magnetosphere.”

Neither statement canceled the other.

Anja entered the model room carrying a portable receipt reader and a folded paper map of the convoy.

Tomas knew she preferred paper for sequence work. He had not expected her to use it for trajectories. Colored pencil lines crossed the sheet from each ship's last observed position. Dotted extensions marked uncertainty since source time.

“Your shared plot advances Palisade continuously,” she said.

“The operational ephemeris predicts its present position.”

“Prediction serves navigation. If you show their council what you need from the geometry, give them the position their last observed packet supports and the projected range after it.”

“I cannot build a drive model without projecting motion.”

“I did not ask you to stop. Label where observation ends.”

He zoomed out. Every trajectory line beyond the last received state changed from solid to dotted. The model looked less authoritative and more honest.

“What did they send?” Anja asked.

“Enough shield reserve to seed a braking stream.”

“Authorized?”

“No.”

“Then you have evidence of capacity, not consent.”

“I know.”

Her expression softened by a degree. “I know you know. The council will need to hear you say it.”

Tomas gave her the first four branches. She examined source labels rather than outcomes. When the nineteen-year spread appeared, she sat on the edge of Jori's vacant chair.

“That is longer than Pera lived after departure,” she said.

He kept his eyes on the model. “I can probably shorten it.”

“Can you remove it?”

“No.”

“Then do not present nineteen years as an engineering defect you expect to optimize away.”

The advice stung because he had already begun treating the spread that way. Engineers survived responsibility by turning harm into a variable. Improve field retention. Increase beam efficiency. Reorder encounters. Every adjustment shaved months from a line and made him feel closer to innocence.

Tomas closed the optimization pane. “Palisade's forward workers take more exposure. Settlement construction loses shielding material. The fleet arrives across a generation. Scarcity starts before the first capture because beam power and fabrication leave ordinary systems.”

“Food?”

“Not modeled.”

“Wake schedules?”

“Only at convoy average.”

“Governance during staggered arrival?”

“Not engineering.”

“It becomes engineering when early ships control beam infrastructure needed by late ones.”

He looked at her. “You came here to help?”

“I came here because Osei needs the model in two hours, and because a clean trajectory can become another kind of false simultaneity.”

Tomas laughed once despite himself. “That sounded prepared.”

“It became prepared while I watched you omit nineteen years of politics.”

Together they marked every unobserved cooperation assumption. Palisade release: undecided. Juniper beam contribution: contradicted Crown, future alternative position unknown. Vela beamer access: politically uncertain. Kite array availability: observed technical state six hours old. Bracken crew capacity: forecast only.

The branch acquired more dotted lines than solid ones. It still held physically.

That distinction gave Tomas something he could defend. A plausible path did not need predicted consent. It needed conditions stated clearly enough that each ship could answer.

He drafted a technical request to Palisade. The first version asked for immediate release authority and specified tonnage.

Anja deleted it.

“You have no quorum,” she said.

“Osei has emergency authority over Nacre.”

“Not over Palisade's hull.”

“I am out of time.”

“So were they when the fleet predicted their answer.”

Tomas turned away from the console. The model room's ventilation blew cold across his damp shirt. Inside the vacuum sleeve, the little shield square remained against its upper restraint, proof that material could move and no proof that anyone owed it.

“What can I ask?” he said.

“Ask them to validate your fabrication and field assumptions. Ask what risk their reserve carries. Ask what evidence their council requires before considering release. Do not put a requested answer inside the question.”

He rewrote the packet.

Osei received the alternative with fifty-eight minutes left on her command clock.

She convened only the technical heads and three council delegates. No vote appeared on the agenda. Tomas appreciated that until he began speaking and realized nobody in the room had promised to let his work continue.

He started with the test plate. Jori replayed the coil pulses and showed the thermal warning. Tomas then described Palisade's local-state inventory, preserving its source time on every page. He separated usable material from authorized material. The first category had numbers. The second contained none.

“Show capture,” Osei said.

He ran the fourth branch.

The convoy's single Crown trace separated into nine paths. Each bent under a different beam encounter. No line crossed the highest-risk region in Thale's active magnetosphere, though Nadi's incomplete ion-band map left uncertainty around the seasonal edges. All nine habitats reached some viable Ione capture in the central run.

Arrival dates unfolded across nineteen years.

The food delegate whispered a count under her breath. Osei heard it.

“Say what you are counting,” she said.

“Harvest cycles between Vela's capture and Palisade's. Spare parts consumed before shared settlement fabrication exists. Children waking without the cohort structure we planned.”

The shielding representative pointed to Palisade's exposure curve. “Their reserve exists for a reason. You have pushed them below their charter floor.”

“In the central mass branch, yes,” Tomas said.

“Then it is not available.”

“It is physically present. Their council must decide whether another risk justifies releasing it.”

“Do you recommend they accept?”

Tomas felt every face turn toward him. He could have answered with aggregate survival probability. His model favored distributed braking once Thale's life evidence entered the objective function. Yet the aggregate hid who stood behind thinner plate.

“I recommend that Nacre present the full branch and ask,” he said. “Palisade needs our exposure assumptions, Thale's evidence, the arrival spread, and every alternative we have falsified. I cannot consent for their forward compartments.”

Osei looked toward Anja.

“The request must come from lawful authority,” Anja said. “Nacre can request analysis. Committing fleet assets requires actual quorum or Palisade's own emergency act within its charter.”

“Can Palisade release unilaterally?”

“Material under its control, perhaps. Binding the release into a fleet maneuver affects every cohort's arrival. That needs fleet authority.”

The captain asked Tomas for failure boundaries. He showed loss of Vela's beam, partial Palisade release, mesh dispersion, and Juniper nonparticipation. Some branches failed. Others preserved capture with greater spread. None offered a painless substitute.

Osei chose the partial-release branch and ordered him to run it at half speed so the delegates could follow every dependency. Palisade retained its deepest forward tiles and released braided mesh plus unfinished laminate. The available stream carried less inertial mass, so Nacre had to contribute two cargo baffles and a section of its outer debris screen. Kite's projected encounter moved six months later. Bracken lost a planned correction entirely and required a longer outer-system arc.

“Stop there,” the shielding representative said. “You just spent Nacre's debris screen.”

“I assigned it as a condition,” Tomas replied. “No release has occurred.”

“What does that condition cost?”

He opened the strike model. Removing the section increased exposure along two service corridors during the final approach. Crews could reinforce them from internal plate, but those panels currently protected water storage. Risk moved; it did not disappear.

The representative made him trace the transfer all the way to people. Hull technicians would work extra shifts. Water reserves would need dispersed storage. Three residential compartments would lose emergency shelter capacity until settlement fabrication began. Each consequence appeared beside the trajectory rather than in an appendix.

Next Osei removed Vela's beamer after the first Palisade stream. Nacre could not assume the lead habitat would support a maneuver that displaced Crown. Tomas reassigned beam work among five smaller arrays. Their lower power stretched each encounter and consumed more reactor margin. Garden light reductions appeared on Nacre and Juniper. Palisade's fabrication temperature fell below efficient laminate production after month four.

“Scarcity before arrival,” the food delegate said.

“Yes.”

“And scarcity after the early ships arrive?”

Tomas advanced the clock. Vela reached the outer Ione system first in this branch but lacked enough independent fabrication to support every trailing habitat. Nacre entered capture later with damaged baffles and reduced seed power. Palisade remained years away while carrying the convoy's strongest fabrication plant.

The delegate studied the separated markers. “Our settlement plan assumes shared tools within one harvest cycle.”

“This path breaks that assumption.”

“Then early arrival does not mean settlement readiness.”

“No. It means capture.”

Anja moved closer to the display. “Mark that distinction in every public summary. If the model says arrival, people will hear safety.”

Tomas changed the label to gravitational capture. The correction lengthened every line of text and removed a comfort the model had never earned.

A final branch assumed one shield batch failed inspection after release. Light-speed delay prevented Palisade from learning the failure before sending the next scheduled batch unless the convoy slowed the sequence. Tomas inserted receipt-dependent holds. The maneuver grew less efficient, but every new release could now respond to observed performance from the previous one.

“You are building causal intervals into engineering,” Anja said.

“I am preventing a bad batch from becoming six bad batches.”

“Same discipline, different consequence.”

Osei tapped the new holds. “Keep them. Any alternative that requires Palisade to predict our receipt repeats the error in metal.”

“Does this answer my order?” Osei asked.

“It provides a path worth engineering,” Tomas said. “Not yet a maneuver.”

Osei stopped the deadline clock with forty-one minutes unused.

Sumi transmitted Tomas's revised technical request at Nacre source time. The room could do nothing to accelerate its passage. Palisade lay more than three light-hours away by the latest observed range. Even an immediate answer would spend most of the next shift in flight.

Tomas worked while they waited. He replaced convoy-average food assumptions with ship-specific ranges. He mapped which early arrivals might support later beam encounters without claiming those councils would agree. He reduced the requested shield mass until one branch preserved Palisade's charter floor, but that branch widened arrival beyond twenty-two years.

Seven hours later, Palisade's receipt acknowledgement reached Nacre. The response field remained empty.

Near the end of Day Fifteen, a second packet arrived with the fabrication council's seal. It confirmed Tomas's mesh calculations within six percent. It rejected his lowest exposure estimate. It also supplied a stronger field-retention method using the reserve's unfinished laminate.

The final paragraph contained no decision about release.

PALISADE WILL PLACE SHIELD RESERVE BEFORE ITS COUNCIL UPON RECEIPT OF A LAWFUL FLEET REQUEST. TECHNICAL CAPACITY DOES NOT CONSTITUTE ASSENT. PROVIDE OBSERVED AUTHORITY FROM AN ACTUAL QUORUM.

Tomas read the words to Osei without shortening them.

On the central display, the distributed paths remained dotted. Palisade had given him better physics and refused to let physics borrow its answer.

Interlude II — Pera's Six Hours

Pera Kest felt Ardent's fourth shelter seal buckle through the wrench.

The tool kicked against her palm. A yellow line opened along the pressure strip, no wider than thread, then vanished when the frame settled. Behind the bulkhead, eighty-six foundry workers waited under emergency air. Three compartments forward, debris had stripped shielding from a coolant trunk and turned the industrial ring into a sequence of rooms nobody could safely cross for long.

“Again,” she told Yev.

He increased brace pressure from the manual pump. Pera watched the frame rather than its display. The top corner shifted first. She marked it with grease.

Ardent's emergency clock showed five hours forty-one minutes until containment loss under the current heat curve. Master time showed twenty-three minutes until the fleet allocation window closed.

Those clocks described different dangers. Only one had authority over the shielding mass they needed.

Her wrist reader pulsed with a message from convoy coordination.

ALLOCATION PROJECTION: ARDENT ACCEPTS CLOSURE AT MASTER 18:00.

EVACUATION SUPPORT WILL END UNDER COMMON RESCUE SEQUENCE.

FORECAST CONFIDENCE: 0.9918.

Pera read it twice because the first reading made no physical sense. Her organizing council had not accepted closure. Four members sat in Shelter Two with a functioning local terminal. Two more had reached the foundry control room. They had received the proposal twelve minutes earlier by Ardent clock and had not voted.

Yev glanced at her reader. “Does that mean no plate?”

“It means someone has predicted our answer.”

“Are they right?”

Pera tightened the wrench until the brace stopped moving. “No.”

She left Yev with the seal and pulled herself along the emergency rail toward control. Ardent's industrial ring normally supplied enough rotation to make every ladder feel vertical. Current imbalance had slowed it. Her boots landed lightly, each step taking too long. Heat pressed through the soles near the damaged trunk.

At the junction, a medic guided three burned workers toward Shelter Three. Pera gave up her filter hood to the one still walking without help. She had another in control. Six hours required choices smaller than doctrine.

The council terminal carried a clean receipt marker. Vela's closure proposal had arrived after the shielding strike. It offered evacuation support until master 18:00, then redirected Palisade's mobile shield stock toward protecting convoy power relays. The justification cited worsening debris and Ardent's declining containment probability.

A probability had become a farewell before the people inside the hull had answered.

Councilor Amel sat with one trouser leg cut open around a pressure splint. “Can we hold?”

“If Palisade releases mass to cover the coolant trunk, yes.”

“How long?”

“Six more hours before the next heat threshold. Longer if repair reaches the outer valve.”

“Can Palisade get it here?”

“Technical says their tug can cross the gap in four hours from release.”

Amel looked toward the local clock. “Master closure comes first.”

“Not here.”

“It controls the allocation.”

Pera knelt at the terminal. She opened an observed-response form and attached Ardent's current pressure traces, thermal slope, shelter count, and Palisade transit estimate. Each file carried its own source stamp. None promised survival. Together they proved the emergency still contained an actionable interval.

The other council members joined by shelter audio. One connection dropped whenever a pump cycled. Pera repeated the motion three times without pretending the missing words had been heard.

Amel called the vote only after every connected member confirmed receipt.

Five rejected closure. One abstained because her shelter lacked updated thermal data. The seventh seat remained empty; its holder had gone into the damaged trunk before communications failed.

Pera signed as industrial systems organizer, not as a councilor. Her testimony addressed capacity.

ARDENT DOES NOT ACCEPT CLOSURE. LOCAL CONTAINMENT REMAINS POSSIBLE FOR SIX MORE HOURS IF PALISADE RELEASES MOBILE SHIELD MASS. OBSERVED CONDITIONS ATTACHED. I DID NOT CONSENT TO FORECAST ACCEPTANCE OR TO THE REMOVAL OF ARDENT'S REPLY INTERVAL.

Amel signed the council rejection beneath it.

Transmission control reported the path before sending: Ardent to relay bead, relay to Vela, with a separate technical copy toward Palisade. The message could not reach either destination before master 18:00. Nothing on Ardent could shorten the light path.

“Send,” Amel said.

Pera pressed the control.

The packet left under Ardent's source clock. Its carrier trace dropped toward the relay with both signatures intact. No one in the room mistook departure for receipt.

Master closure arrived while Pera crawled inside the coolant access.

Her reader changed the rescue allocation from open to completed. Palisade's mobile mass disappeared from Ardent's available-assets pane. The local council rejection remained marked unarrived.

She kept moving.

The access tube had lost lighting beyond the second elbow. Pera followed painted bolt heads by touch and counted her breaths inside the replacement hood. Heat pushed the fabric against her mouth. Ahead, a manual bypass wheel stood behind a veil of coolant vapor.

Yev's voice reached her through a failing wire channel. “Shelter Four seal slipped again.”

“Shift the lower brace ten centimeters.”

“We did.”

“Then move everyone from the frame side and let it flex. Do not chase it with more pressure.”

“How long do we leave it loose?”

“Until I open this bypass.”

That answer gave him work, not certainty.

Pera reached the wheel. Its powered actuator had fused. She fitted her wrench and pulled. Nothing moved. The second pull tore skin under her glove. On the third, the wheel turned one notch and hot vapor thickened around her.

Her wrist reader stored two receipts. Palisade had received the technical copy four minutes after master closure. Vela had received Ardent's observed refusal nine minutes later. The replies had traveled lawfully. The allocation layer classified both as late.

Palisade's immediate acknowledgment contained no release authority. Its duty operator had forwarded the request to a council already told the rescue window had ended.

Pera laughed once, breathless inside the hood. The absurdity deserved air she did not have.

She turned the wheel another notch. Coolant flow rose in the outer trunk. Control reported a slower thermal climb, then a new containment warning from the foundry wall. The shield breach had spread beyond the section Palisade's tug could cover in time.

Six hours still existed on Ardent's clock. Fewer remained in the hull.

The council asked Pera for an updated testimony. She gave pressure readings and refused to revise the earlier capacity estimate retroactively. At emission, six hours had described the observed margin. Later damage did not make the statement dishonest. It changed the next one.

UPDATED LOCAL CONDITION: CONTAINMENT DEGRADING. PRIOR RESPONSE REMAINS VALID AT ITS SOURCE TIME. REQUEST IMMEDIATE MASS RELEASE IF TRANSIT WINDOW REMAINS PHYSICALLY OPEN.

Amel transmitted the update. No forecast filled the wait.

Pera reached the foundry junction near local hour three. Shelter Four had emptied into two smaller compartments. Yev sat against the bulkhead with his sleeve wrapped around a cut hand. He tried to stand when she approached.

“Stay,” she said.

“Control wants you.”

“Control can speak here.”

A structural alarm sounded through the ring. Not loud. Ardent had reduced alarm volume to preserve backup power, turning catastrophe into a polite repeating tone.

Pera checked the shelter seals, reassigned the remaining filter hoods, and sent a count to medical. Then she opened a private recorder.

Anja would receive the legal testimony eventually. As a sequence auditor, she might see the source clock, the late classification, and the closure entry. None of that belonged in a sister's message.

Pera rested the reader against her knee. Grease crossed the screen where her glove touched it.

“Anja,” she said, “you will try to find the correct clock for this. There isn't one that can make the choice for us. I answered while I could answer. That has to remain true even if nobody waits.”

The foundry wall gave a deep metallic knock.

She stopped the recording before fear could turn it into evidence. The private line carried no council seal and made no claim on Palisade. Pera addressed it only to her sister, then placed it behind the public packet in the lawful outgoing queue.

Transmission control accepted both objects separately.

Pera clipped the reader to the shelter rail and returned to the buckling seal as containment failed beyond the wall.

Part III — The Fleet in Pieces

Chapter 15 — Common Time

Osei Tamm kept Meridian's clock on the hearing wall.

Councilor Sen had asked to cover it before the public entered. Anja wanted the forecast-ballot layer disconnected from every Nacre legal display. Communications offered an isolated feed with raw local time only. Osei rejected all three proposals and left the green master digits above the council benches.

At 08:02, the clock scheduled a radiator exchange with Kite.

At 08:03 by Nacre local sequence, Sumi Dax confirmed that Kite's beam shutters had moved as expected. Nacre rotated three radiator panels into the shared cold corridor. Temperatures fell along the inner ring.

The operation used predicted timing, synchronized clocks, and no fiction about consent.

“Record both events,” Osei said.

The clerk captured Meridian's schedule and Sumi's observed confirmation in separate fields. That distinction would frame the hearing before anyone spoke about law.

Residents filled the thermal gallery in work clothes. The council had opened standing space along the rear wall and relayed the session to all three inhabited rings. Heat displays still occupied half the screens. Nacre could not suspend being a habitat while debating who possessed authority over it.

Anja sat at the evidence table with the isolated reader from Day Twelve. Tomas occupied the technical rail, his distributed-path model reduced to a single black wafer. He had not come to sell an alternative. Palisade had requested actual quorum before discussing shield release, and no such quorum existed yet.

The remaining wall screen held Vela's formal defense.

Its packet had left the lead habitat seventy-one minutes earlier by Vela's source clock. Nacre received it after the expected carrier delay. Director Yselle Quon appeared seated in the Meridian chamber, silver hair secured at the nape and no flag behind her. Concord counsel Levan Oris did not appear.

Osei verified the source and receipt path aloud. Then she authorized playback.

“Captain Tamm, Council of Nacre,” Yselle began, “your classification dispute addresses a real tension in the Concord. It does not expose a counterfeit convoy.”

Her voice carried no urgency. Vela had recorded several variants for different habitats, and Sumi's packet inspection showed no edits inside this one. Yselle spoke to Nacre knowing its answer could not reach her until after she finished.

“The Ninefold Convoy survives through one operational time. Pumps begin before distant demand becomes observed. Beams clear shared corridors before every habitat can return acknowledgment. Thermal loads, navigation windows, wake schedules, and debris responses depend on coordinated expectation. Remove forecast continuity from that structure and you do not restore nine free councils. You create nine habitats learning separately that survival required action earlier.”

Several people at the rear muttered. Osei raised one hand and kept the recording running.

“Annex Twelve recognizes this material fact. Forecast testimony preserves continuity during communication delay. It does not erase later observed responses. When an observed instruction arrives, Meridian incorporates the divergence and adjusts future action where physical conditions permit. Nacre has identified a terminology defect in the condensed legal view. Vela will review that defect. Nacre has not shown that Crown preparation ceased to protect convoy survival.”

Yselle looked straight into the recorder.

“Juniper's rejection reached us after alignment had begun. Its council cannot recover opportunities spent during the signal interval. Neither can yours. Government across light-hours must sometimes act on the best account available before every answer arrives. The alternative is not pure consent. The alternative is unmanaged causality.”

The image ended. A source stamp replaced her face.

Osei let the room react for one minute by the local clock. Anger moved first. Fear followed when people began repeating the list of pumps, beams, heat, and debris. Yselle had defended stolen authority with systems every resident depended on before breakfast.

Sen requested recognition.

“Director Quon concedes a defect,” he said. “She refuses its consequence.”

Mara Venn shook her head. “She concedes terminology. Not insertion.”

“Then let Anja show insertion again.”

“No,” Osei said.

Anja turned toward her, surprised.

“The proof chain stands in the public record. This hearing addresses Vela's defense. Repeating evidence until the room tires would not strengthen it.”

Osei asked the clerk to display Yselle's claims as separate propositions.

Coordination requires shared operational predictions.

Annex Twelve allows forecast continuity during delay.

Observed replies can correct later action.

Crown preparation protects survival.

Delay can spend physical options before consent arrives.

“Which statement do you dispute?” Osei asked Anja.

“The second, if forecast continuity occupies a testimony field reserved for a person's answer. The third, when an irreversible act prevents correction. I do not dispute the first. The fourth requires engineering evidence. The fifth describes a real constraint but cannot generate consent.”

“Councilor Sen?”

“All of them. One operational time gives Vela a permanent excuse.”

Osei looked up at the clock. “The radiator exchange completed nine minutes ago. Would you have delayed it until Kite's council voted?”

“That exchange did not sterilize Thale.”

“Correct. Name the boundary.”

Sen hesitated. “Irreversibility.”

“Whose judgment?”

“The affected councils.”

“When?”

“After evidence receipt.”

“And what may operators do before then?”

His frustration showed in the set of his jaw. “Preserve existing conditions.”

Tomas spoke from the rail. “Preservation can choose a path. Holding Crown alignment for another day closes distributed geometries. Holding Nacre's present attitude also closes some geometries. No neutral position exists.”

“Then Quon wins,” Sen said.

“No,” Osei replied. “It means we need narrower authority and visible cost, not a word that makes physics wait.”

The first witness came from Nacre's cooling office.

Chief Oran described hundreds of daily actions scheduled through Meridian before observed confirmation could return. Radiator corridor sharing prevented one habitat's waste heat from interfering with another's sensors. Beam quiet periods protected communications. Pump demand forecasts allowed water and coolant to move between rings before shifts changed.

“Could you operate from local time alone?” Mara Venn asked.

“For several hours,” Oran said. “After that, external corridor conflicts accumulate. We could negotiate each one by messages, but the negotiation would arrive after some windows closed.”

“Does master time tell you what other ships consented to?”

“It tells me what their equipment will probably do.”

“Do you need more?”

“Not to move heat.”

The clerk recorded that answer beside Yselle's first claim.

Next Sumi demonstrated the communications scheduler. Meridian forecast carrier congestion and reserved relay capacity before packet arrival. When a ship sent less traffic than predicted, unused slots reopened. When it sent more, low-priority packets waited.

“Can forecast scheduling suppress evidence?” Sen asked.

“It can delay low-priority packets,” Sumi said. “That happened to Mote's fragments.”

“Then operational prediction already controls deliberation.”

“It controls carriage under published priority rules. Anja diverted the fragments because destination-life evidence carried legal effect the initial classification missed.”

“Who set the priority?”

“Humans did. Meridian applied it.”

Osei heard the room absorb another unwelcome distinction. Software had not conspired. Administrative choices hid inside ordinary systems because people had placed them there.

A garden technician testified about wake schedules. Predictive load let his crew sleep through distant coordination intervals instead of staffing every light-delayed exchange. He wanted the system preserved. His husband, seated behind him, wanted Juniper's rejection honored.

“Those positions do not conflict,” Anja said when invited to question him.

The technician gave her a tired look. “They do if Vela refuses to separate the system from the vote.”

“Then the refusal creates the conflict.”

Osei marked that sentence for the council's drafting group, not for rhetoric but because it identified the action Nacre could take. They did not need to abolish common time. They could refuse legal recognition where forecast fields crossed into consent.

A cycling coordinator named Hessa Ulan came next with a roster map folded against her chest. She managed wake transitions for six hundred people in the outer ring. Meridian forecast which technical specialties each ship would need during shared maneuvers, and Hessa used those projections to avoid waking entire crews for a handful of tasks.

“Last year Kite forecast a beam inspection that never happened,” she said. “I kept fourteen optics workers awake through their sleep entry. They lost the cycle and took medical leave. We changed the rule after that.”

“What changed?” Osei asked.

“We required observed confirmation before interrupting an actual sleep descent. Forecast need can delay a scheduled descent by one hour. It cannot wake someone after sedation starts.”

“Why that boundary?”

“Because delay remains reversible. Chemical entry does not.”

Sen pointed toward Yselle's frozen source stamp. “Your office found this rule without a constitutional crisis.”

“My office found it after fourteen people paid for the mistake.”

Hessa opened the roster. Common-time projections still appeared beside each specialty, but a black line separated scheduling advice from medical authority. Above the line, Meridian arranged work. Below it, a human duty officer signed.

Anja asked whether the arrangement slowed emergency response.

“Sometimes,” Hessa said. “We carry more awake reserve because we refuse to treat predicted availability as a body. That costs food and years of waking life. It also means nobody opens a sleeper's vein because a model expected consent.”

The rear wall grew quiet. Osei asked the clerk to attach Hessa's black-line procedure to the drafting record.

A hull worker followed her. Cale Miro repaired debris screens and distrusted every proposal made from the hub. He arrived with dust still embedded in the seams of his coverall.

“Master time tells us when a neighbor's beam crosses our work corridor,” Cale said. “I want that clock. If Nacre drops it, nobody from my crew goes outside.”

“We are not proposing that,” Mara Venn replied.

“People in my compartment heard suspension and thought you meant all of it. Vela says all of it.”

“That is why this hearing remains open.”

Cale looked at Osei rather than Venn. “Will my suit display keep the common crossing schedule?”

“Yes.”

“Will a green mark on that display say I voted for Crown?”

“No.”

“Then write those two answers where my crew can read them.”

The public relay posted both lines before he left the witness place. Osei watched message traffic rise across the rings. Residents asked whether meal clocks would change, whether medical appointments would retain master conversion, whether Vela could shut off synchronization remotely. Most questions concerned routines rather than constitutional theory.

Sumi answered from the communications rail. Nacre hosted local copies of operational schedules. Vela could stop supplying updates, but it could not erase received data or alter Nacre's raw clock. Navigation would degrade gradually, not disappear at a switch. Every answer exposed cost without granting the threat more power than it held.

Mara Venn requested the legal mapping attached to Yselle's defense. The packet included a diagram of Annex Twelve authority. Forecast continuity entered at the operations layer, passed through a continuity-certification service, and emerged as eligible testimony wherever delay threatened an existing plan.

“Existing according to whom?” Venn asked.

Anja enlarged the service definition. “Vela operations registers a continuity plan. Meridian evaluates whether interruption raises modeled risk. The legal layer accepts the certification.”

“Where does affected-council review occur?”

“It does not appear in the path.”

“Observed correction afterward?”

“At the final box, if physical conditions permit.”

Osei traced the diagram with one finger. Yselle's defense depended on that final qualification. Later testimony could correct action only where earlier action preserved alternatives. The service never asked whose options it consumed while preserving Vela's plan.

“Can Nacre keep the upper operations layer and reject the lower testimony output?” she asked.

Sumi compared interface specifications. “Technically, yes. We can accept schedule objects with forecast provenance. We can block their promotion into council, quorum, and command-consent fields.”

“Effect on pumps?”

“None.”

“Beam corridors?”

“Operators still receive them.”

“Crown commands?”

“They arrive. Nacre decides how to classify and answer them.”

Sen's expression remained guarded. “Vela may refuse our distinction.”

“Vela does not control what Nacre recognizes as its council signature,” Anja said.

“It controls the beamer.”

Tomas touched the black wafer at his rail but did not open it. “For now.”

Osei stopped that line before it entered the hearing as a promise. “The alternative remains under technical review. Today we separate channels that already can be separated.”

Tomas presented three physical thresholds. Routine synchronization supported reversible coordination. Crown preparation already consumed options but remained open to alternative branching for a limited interval. Plasma-seed release would sharply reduce later correction and risk Thale's active ion bands.

“Does Nacre's continued thermal coordination advance Crown?” Osei asked.

“Not directly.”

“Attitude guidance?”

“Some corrections serve both radiator safety and Crown alignment.”

“Can you separate them?”

“By purpose in the command record, yes. Not always by physical effect.”

“Then record both.”

Tomas frowned. “A thruster firing cannot carry two histories.”

“It can carry one observed effect and two stated reasons. Future review may judge the reasons. Do not erase either because the metal moved once.”

Anja looked down, perhaps hiding approval.

At 11:26, a real thermal conflict interrupted the hearing.

Bracken's predicted radiator retraction did not occur. Nacre's panel rotation had begun on schedule, placing two hot surfaces on converging lines. The operations alarm cleared the public screens and replaced legal propositions with temperatures.

Osei left the council floor for the command rail.

“Receipt status?” she asked.

Sumi checked the carrier ledger. “Bracken received the shared schedule four hours ago. No observed equipment confirmation. Meridian predicted compliance from standard automation.”

“Physical view?”

Oran enlarged the external plot. Light from Bracken showed its panels still extended at emission thirty-seven minutes earlier. Current state remained unobserved.

“Reverse Nacre's rotation,” Osei said.

“We lose the cold corridor for twelve minutes.”

“Accept it. Shift nursery load to reserve.”

The command propagated through Nacre without asking Bracken's council. Motors reversed. Hull vibration changed under everyone's feet. Nursery temperature rose four tenths of a degree, then stabilized when reserve coolant entered.

Twenty-two minutes later, Bracken's delayed equipment confirmation arrived. A damaged hinge had prevented retraction. Its operators had sent the warning immediately after discovering the fault. Meridian's prediction had failed.

No one accused Bracken of dissent. No one treated the forecast as a promise. Nacre adjusted before observed testimony about the equipment arrived because the physical risk permitted and required local action.

Osei returned to the council floor. “Director Quon calls the alternative unmanaged causality. You just watched management under uncertainty. What did we use?”

Oran answered first. “Prediction to schedule. Observation to update. Local authority to prevent harm.”

“What did we not use?”

Anja said, “Bracken's predicted action as consent to impose the conflict.”

Sen leaned forward. “You acted before their warning arrived.”

“Yes,” Osei said. “I acted on Nacre's radiator. I did not sign Bracken's name.”

The clerk entered the incident as part of the hearing record, including every source and receipt time.

Drafting the resolution took four hours.

The council refused broad language that might disable emergency scheduling by accident. Anja rejected a clause allowing forecasts “where consent can reasonably be inferred,” because reasonableness could not create an observed reply. Tomas removed a promise to halt every dual-purpose attitude correction; the ship could not distinguish physical effects as cleanly as legal purposes. Sen fought to include a direct finding that Vela's Crown quorum had no authority.

Before the final reading, the clerk tested the language against three ordinary records: a pump start, a beam-corridor reservation, and Hessa Ulan's sleep-entry hold. All remained operationally valid. He then tested a forecast concurrence. The new rule rejected it for lack of a human emission path. Nothing else on the board changed color before the watching council.

Osei supported him on that point.

The final text separated three layers.

Nacre would continue operational synchronization for navigation, thermal management, communications, and reversible safety actions. Forecasts could schedule Nacre's own equipment within existing emergency authority, with model origin and uncertainty available in the record.

Nacre would suspend recognition of forecast ballots in any quorum, allocation, treaty, or irreversible fleet commitment. Only observed testimony emitted after receipt of common evidence could occupy Nacre's legal seat or claim to bind another cohort.

Nacre would classify each Crown command by physical effect, stated purpose, and reversibility. Coordination channels remained open. No automatic acknowledgment could count as council assent.

The vote passed sixteen to four, with one abstention.

Osei signed after the council members. Her signature carried Nacre local time. Sumi transmitted the resolution to every habitat with the witness-order proof attached. The widest receipt would take hours. Practical quorum would take longer.

Vela acknowledged carrier receipt fifty-six minutes later. The acknowledgment contained no legal response.

During the wait, Osei kept the hearing open. Residents came and left by shift. The master clock scheduled beam quiet, wake transitions, and two more radiator exchanges. Operators used all three without filling a council seat.

Anja approached the command rail near second meal. “You gave Quon part of her argument.”

“She earned part.”

“People may remember the part where one operational time kept the nursery cold.”

“It did help keep the nursery cold.”

“And forget who controls its legal categories.”

“Then your archive must make forgetting harder.”

Anja looked toward the wall clock. “She will declare us a threat to synchronization.”

“Has that arrived, or did you predict it?”

A brief smile touched Anja's mouth. “Prediction only.”

They waited.

At 18:41, Sumi received a Vela packet sent after Nacre's resolution reached Director Quon's office. Its receipt path showed the full causal order. Nacre spoke. Light carried the decision. Vela received it, deliberated for thirty-two minutes, and answered.

Osei opened the packet before the council.

Yselle's image did not appear. A formal declaration occupied the screen under Vela's civil seal.

NACRE'S PARTIAL WITHDRAWAL FROM COMMON-TIME AUTHORITY CREATES IMMEDIATE RISK TO CONVOY COORDINATION. UNDER ANNEX TWELVE, DIRECTOR YSELLE QUON HEREBY DECLARES A SYNCHRONIZATION EMERGENCY. CONTINUITY DIRECTIVES REMAIN IN FORCE PENDING RESTORATION OF FLEET RECOGNITION.

Below the text, Meridian requested automatic acknowledgment from Nacre's command systems.

Osei disabled the legal acknowledgment and left the physical channels open.

The master clock continued counting above the declaration.

Chapter 16 — The Long Brake

Tomas Ren killed Nacre's garden at 02:06 and watched the ship survive.

The drive-model room rendered consequences as bands of color along the curved wall. Green marked a viable arrival. Amber marked a habitat that reached Ione with reserves below law. Red marked loss of steering, food, shielding, or people. Tomas had removed the garden load to feed the magnetic loops. Nacre's line remained green for eleven simulated years, narrowed to amber, then touched the Caro capture corridor with nine days of steering margin.

Above the trajectory, a second display showed what his choice meant inside the hull. Full-spectrum lamps went dark by sections. Seed vaults stayed cold, but the living beds lost their crop cycle. The kitchen model replaced greens with vat protein and stored starch. Pediatric bone-loss projections climbed. So did the probability of riots in the third ration year.

He saved the run under FAILURE / GARDEN and began again.

Forty hours had become twenty-seven before Nacre's hearing. Since then Palisade had sent three compressed mass tables, each delayed by the light between them and each stamped with a warning that no figure amounted to an offer. Vela had declared a synchronization emergency. Crown preparation continued through the physical channels Nacre had kept open. Nobody had granted Tomas extra time because the law had become honest.

Captain Osei stood behind his left shoulder with both hands clasped at her back. She had arrived without an aide and refused the acceleration couch.

“Run it without killing food,” she said.

“I can keep food or steering at Crown arrival,” Tomas answered. “Not both.”

“Then move the arrival.”

“That costs years.”

“Years contain food.”

Her correction landed where she intended. Tomas cleared the display and reopened the branch tree. Every path began with the same fact: the convoy had spent forty-three years accelerating toward a narrow arrival geometry. Vela's beamer and the plasma mirrors near Corva could remove that velocity in one coordinated campaign. If Crown stopped after commitment, the nine habitats would pass through the useful capture window too fast. Some could turn toward the star and brake on their own drives, but those drives had never been sized to erase the full approach speed.

Tomas selected the slow-arrival branch. “This gives Nacre another six years to decelerate.”

The wall redrew a long curve outside Thale's orbit. Fuel reserve fell first. Steering authority followed. Caro capture remained possible, but only if Nacre used structural carbon from two spoke braces as reaction mass during the final correction.

Osei studied the rotating stress diagram. “Those braces hold the second ring.”

“They can hold it after material is removed.”

“At what occupancy?”

“Fourteen thousand.”
