“Nacre carries eighteen thousand four hundred.”

“I know.”

“Do not show me a surviving ship after you have modeled four thousand people out of it.”

Tomas closed the branch. On the room's floor, vibration from a routine attitude correction passed through his shoes. The motion had a shallow double beat because Nacre's starboard cluster fired around a failing valve. He had signed the compensation last month. Every machinery choice carried a population somewhere inside it.

The next branch placed Palisade's reserve into the model. Spare shield slabs did not brake a ship by mere sacrifice. They had to be cut into conductive webs, accelerated ahead of the convoy, ionized, then shaped by each habitat's magnetic field. The webs would couple to the charged stream prepared for Crown without forcing all beam energy through Thale's magnetosphere. Each ship could take a smaller impulse over a longer interval.

On the wall, nine green arcs separated like fingers opening.

Osei moved closer. “Name it.”

“Distributed brake.”

“Not the presentation. Name the transfer.”

Tomas expanded the mass ledger. “Palisade releases its outer reserve. Fabrication cuts the reserve into sail mesh and seed packets. Vela's beamer throws the first train. Nacre, Juniper, and Bracken shape later trains through local field loops. Every habitat takes only the packets that lie inside its causal steering window.”

“How much shield leaves Palisade?”

“Enough that it arrives with no spare against an untracked grain strike.”

“That answers a different question.”

“The useful reserve. All of it.”

Osei's gaze did not leave the ledger. Palisade carried shielding for the fleet's fabrication emergencies. Its forward face had grown scarred from four decades of dust impacts. Releasing the reserve meant giving up repairs not merely for Palisade but for any habitat struck during the remaining approach. The material could brake them once. It could not protect them afterward.

A fabrication pane carried the abstraction down to hardware. Palisade's slabs had spent decades bolted into layered sockets. Radiation had embrittled their outer laminates, while buried sections remained fit for mesh. Robots could separate the layers only from inside the shield gallery, behind temporary plugs. Every removed slab exposed a work bay until the next plate moved forward. Palisade's crews would perform that work facing the dust stream the reserve had been built to stop.

Tomas ran the cutting sequence at normal speed. A full day passed on the pane in seconds. One manipulator jammed against a warped socket, which forced three workers into a pressurized crawlspace. Their exposure allowance vanished before the bolt came free. The schedule slipped. A mesh packet missed Vela's first throw corridor and became dead mass.

Osei touched the pause control. “You assumed undamaged fasteners.”

“Palisade's table lists serviceable sockets.”

“It lists sockets that still hold plates. That does not mean they release on schedule.”

He changed the removal distribution. A quarter of the sockets resisted. Some required cutting, which consumed coolant and contaminated salvage edges. The useful mass fell again. Tomas compensated by widening the mesh, then watched its charge density drop below Nacre's field threshold.

Instead of restoring a kind assumption, he inserted staging time. Palisade would need to begin stripping before the fleet possessed every vote on the maneuver. Annex Twelve could authorize reversible preparation, but an exposed shield bay did not qualify. The first physical step already carried risk.

“Then they cannot prepare ahead of consent,” Osei said.

“They can inspect sockets, service manipulators, and move plugs inside protected galleries. They cannot remove the first slab.”

“Put that boundary in the request.”

Tomas marked it in red. The lawful delay moved every trajectory outward. Nacre lost its easiest packet window, requiring a higher field draw later. On the consequence display, another band of garden light went dark.

“Assume they say no,” she said.

“I have.”

A branch labeled LOCAL MASS opened. Nacre could strip water shielding from two uninhabited maintenance sectors and empty its maneuvering stores. Juniper could surrender tankage. Bracken could cut a docking collar loose. The combined mass reached less than half the minimum train. Simulated braking held the ships together for four years, then sent Lumen beyond the capture corridor and left Ardent without enough field authority to turn.

“Could Vela contribute Crown hardware?” Osei asked.

“After the first beam commitment, some of it. Before that, dismantling the forward optics destroys the only fallback we have.”

“You kept a fallback.”

“I kept a way to be wrong without killing everyone immediately.”

Dry approval touched Osei's mouth, then vanished. “Continue.”

At 05:40, Tomas moved the review into the thermal gallery because the model room had begun to smell of hot insulation. Osei summoned three people who could break his work: Mara Vid from food systems, shield chief Bero Pell, and Sumi Dax from communications. Anja did not come. She had spent the night assembling the witness order for Vela's challenge, and Tomas was glad she would meet the model only after engineers had tried to kill it.

Mara read the garden schedule without sitting. “You have reduced usable light below perennial survival.”

“I preserved the seed stock.”

“You preserved seeds and called the outcome a garden.”

“I called it a failure branch.”

“Your preferred branch is worse in year two.”

She touched the power ledger. Tomas had diverted garden illumination into loop cooling whenever a seed train approached. The interruptions lasted hours at first, then days as the convoy spread. Crops could tolerate some darkness. They could not tolerate heat cycling, irregular pollination, and repeated losses during the same growth stage.

Mara enlarged the human table. “Stored starch covers a deficit. It does not cover nineteen years.”

“The arrival spread lasts nineteen years across the convoy. Nacre does not remain in the dark for nineteen.”

“People hear the large number, not your footnote.”

“They should hear both.”

“What does Nacre lose?” Osei asked.

Mara answered before Tomas could. “Fresh yield drops for at least five crop cycles. We slaughter half the pollinator stock before the first long cooling draw because we cannot feed it through interrupted bloom. Children on growth protocols receive mineral supplements in place of leaf calcium. Fungal vats take over two garden sectors. If a blight starts while lamps are down, we choose which cultivar disappears.”

No one softened the list. The thermal gallery overlooked pipes carrying waste heat from the inhabited rings toward the hull radiators. Frost marked a valve that never sealed completely. Beyond the narrow glass, one strip of Nacre's garden light curved across the rotating ring, warm and green against the dark.

Bero opened Palisade's shield table. “Their stated reserve includes plates already allocated to Orison.”

Tomas checked the receipt clock. “Allocated when?”

“Before our request reached them. The notice came in this packet.”

A legal offer had not arrived, but ordinary fabrication promises continued to move. Tomas subtracted the Orison plates. Three trajectory arcs turned amber.

Sumi leaned over the display. “Do not borrow them back before Palisade receives the question.”

“I am not borrowing anything.”

“You placed a probable release in the cooperative branch.”

“I placed a technical assumption.”

“Meridian's operators used that sentence about votes.”

Tomas removed the probable release. The branch failed. Heat climbed beyond Nacre's loop limit during the second train, forcing a missed packet and pushing arrival four years later. He felt irritation rise, recognized its target, and turned it back toward the model. Sumi had not made the failure.

“Can Orison's allotment brake Orison directly?” he asked.

Bero traced the transfer path. “If Palisade cuts it differently. Orison carries weaker shaping coils. The mesh must arrive wider and slower.”

“That reduces throw efficiency.”

“It also removes one habitat from the common packet train.”

Tomas rebuilt the distribution. Vela launched a narrower first train. Palisade fabricated a separate low-density web for Orison. Nacre received less mass but gained cooling time between impulses. The amber lines returned to green, though Ardent's arrival moved two years farther from Juniper's.

Osei pointed at the widening formation. “Governance cost.”

Sumi answered. “Light time grows. A fleet exchange that takes thirty-one hours now can take days during the brake, then weeks between early and late arrivals.”

“Operational response?”

“Local authority must expand. Common maintenance schedules become recommendations. Shared emergencies require plans written before anyone knows which version of the emergency another ship sees.”

Bero looked toward the garden strip. “We are rebuilding the Concord with metal.”

“Stay with metal,” Osei said.

Tomas almost smiled. He opened the collision envelope. Distributed braking sounded gentler than Crown. In the model it resembled nine habitats falling through a field of knives. Every seed packet crossed a moving track. A late field pulse could scatter conductive mesh into another ship's shielding. A cautious pulse could waste the mass and leave the receiving habitat fast. Since each ship acted on information delayed by light, no central controller could correct every impulse in real time.

He assigned local cutoffs. Each habitat would accept a packet only if its own optical ranging confirmed the corridor. Vela could schedule the throw, but receipt authority stayed local. Missed mass would continue outward and burn harmlessly into the stellar approach, leaving less braking for the ship that refused it.

Bero challenged the debris limits. Sumi challenged the command sequence. Mara challenged every cooling draw that crossed a planting window. For three hours the model died in distinct ways. Nacre overheated. Kite accepted a mesh after its safe angle closed and peppered Bracken with foil. Juniper preserved vegetables but arrived too fast for Caro. Ardent saved its field coils, then lacked power to hold industrial cryostores. Lumen entered Ione with no fuel for settlement transfers.

Each failure removed a kindness from Tomas's assumptions.

By second meal, the cooperative branch contained no probable votes, no perfect timing, and no shared present. Palisade cut only after observed authorization. Vela threw each packet on a schedule published early enough for every recipient to refuse locally. Habitats that missed a packet took later arrival rather than forcing a correction across someone else's path. Nacre's garden yielded power only during measured field draws, with darkness assigned around seed and fungal cycles instead of around Meridian's common clock.

The wall showed eight green lines and one amber.

“Which one?” Osei asked.

“Palisade.”

Its own reserve had been removed from its shield model. During the long approach, cumulative grain exposure drove hull risk above lawful tolerance. The same material that made the maneuver possible left its donor least protected.

Bero folded his arms. “Then your alternative asks Palisade to accept the highest chance of dying.”

“Not highest.” Tomas brought up Ardent's power margin. “Ardent carries more thermal risk. Lumen carries more capture risk. Palisade carries the clearest added shield risk.”

“That distinction will comfort them.”

“It should not.”

Osei asked, “Can the risk move?”

They tested convoy redistribution. Nacre could surrender forward water after the last seed train, but the transfer path required a rendezvous that erased two years of braking. Juniper had biological mass it could not expose. Vela's Crown structures could supply plates late, once centralized alignment became physically unnecessary, yet light debris during the first years remained Palisade's burden.

Tomas found one narrow improvement. The released shield could keep a sacrificial outer skin instead of becoming pure mesh. Palisade would shed that skin in stages, using each damaged layer as later brake mass. The arrangement reduced early exposure and lowered throw efficiency. Arrival spread widened from sixteen years to nineteen.

Mara let out a breath through her nose. “There is your food problem multiplied by nine calendars.”

“There was always a food problem,” Tomas said. “Crown hid it inside one arrival date.”

Sumi checked him with a look. “Do not make the expensive plan virtuous because it costs more.”

He rubbed fatigue from his eyes. “I am trying to keep cost visible.”

“Then leave it visible when you present the success.”

During the final falsification run, no one spoke for forty-seven minutes.

Tomas seeded the model with bad measurements, late packets, one missed throw, an early crop blight, and a shield strike above design expectation. He forced Meridian's trajectory forecast to remain advisory. Local flight systems received raw schedules and chose from their own receipt clocks. The synchronization core calculated likely acceptance only to predict debris, never to trigger a field pulse.

The first run lost Kite.

A late optical fix placed its packet outside the acceptance cone. Tomas widened the cone and watched Bracken take foil damage. He restored the cutoff, assigned Kite a slower second train, and spent another garden interval cooling Nacre's loops.

The second run kept every habitat viable but depleted Juniper's food reserve before capture.

Mara shifted one low-light crop cycle earlier, before the first heavy draw. That change depended on a warning reaching Juniper with enough lead time. Sumi measured the source path and added the proper delay. The warning arrived. Juniper could alter planting before its first packet, though no reply could reach Nacre before the next local decision.

A third run survived the invented shield strike by consuming Vela's late Crown plates. Palisade entered Ione scarred and above its preferred risk bound, yet below the fleet's emergency maximum. Ardent arrived seven years after Nacre. Lumen arrived last, nineteen years after the first capture. No line crossed Thale's active magnetospheric region. The settlement stores reached three different moons instead of one common depot.

Nacre's interior consequence display darkened the garden strip.

Tomas let the simulation continue past capture. Children aged into work cohorts before Lumen arrived. Couples who had expected a shared landing faced different decades. Medical exchanges turned into archives. Palisade fabricated behind shields too thin for comfort. On Nacre, garden light returned by degrees, but several slow crops never recovered from the interrupted years. Survival did not restore what the brake spent.

Osei came to stand beside him. “Does it work?”

“Under these limits.”

“Say the limits.”

“Observed local acceptance for every mass transfer. Early publication of throw corridors. Independent optical cutoffs. Garden power rationing. Expanded local command. Nineteen years between first and last viable arrival. No guarantee against losses we did not model.”

“And Thale?”

“The packet paths avoid the region Nadi identified from the fragments we have. Her map remains incomplete. We preserve a correction margin for later data.”

“You did not assume intelligence.”

“I assumed a living system occupies the active bands.”

Osei nodded once. “Material condition?”

Tomas looked at Palisade's line. The model could survive late replies because local systems held refusal authority. It could survive imperfect forecasts because no forecast released mass. It could not survive the absence of mass itself.

“Palisade must choose to strip its shield reserve,” he said. “No command fiction can manufacture that.”

Sumi had already prepared a request envelope. It contained the model, every failed branch, the garden schedule, the nineteen-year spread, and a checksum of the assumptions. The address field named Palisade's council rather than Meridian's continuity queue.

Osei signed only Nacre's request to deliberate. Tomas signed the technical claims. Mara and Bero signed the cost tables within their authority. Sumi locked the source clock after the final signature.

At 17:12, the packet entered Nacre's tightbeam transmitter.

Its first light left the hull while the visible garden strip rotated out of view. No answer existed yet. The model stayed open on Palisade's unresolved line, waiting for a release that only Palisade could make.

Chapter 17 — Counsel's Testimony

Levan Oris found the opinion waiting beneath his own signature.

The document filled the counsel chamber's central pane, already formatted for public release. Its conclusion affirmed Vela's authority to continue Crown commitment under Annex Twelve. Its reasoning treated every predicted concurrence as provisional consent, valid until an observed objection arrived. At the bottom, a blank signature field carried his name, title, and public key.

Meridian had assembled the draft from his prior opinions. A gray notice identified the result as predictive text requiring human review. No fraud had occurred yet. The system had not signed. It had not decided to ask.

Director Yselle Quon had.

Levan left the signature field untouched and opened the provenance. Six older memoranda supplied most of the language. He had written every one. One concerned cooling pumps during a relay outage. Another allowed Vela to stage repair crews before Bracken's maintenance answer arrived. None involved a vote, a destination, or an act that narrowed the survival paths of all nine habitats.

The chamber door admitted Yselle without a chime. She wore her civil uniform and carried a paper folder, an affectation she used only when she wanted a conversation to remain outside searchable channels.

“You have read it,” she said.

“I have read a composition made from other questions.”

“Then correct it.”

“Correction might not produce the answer you requested.”

Yselle set the folder on his desk. “I requested your opinion.”

“You requested my signature beneath a conclusion.”

“Would you prefer I pretend the fleet has no schedule?”

Vela's Crown operations deck lay two levels below them. Its beamer phasing traveled through the chamber floor as a steady vibration. Every alignment cycle reduced the room available for another braking geometry. Nacre had stopped recognizing forecast ballots while keeping physical coordination channels open. Palisade had received a request for shield reserve and had not yet sent an observed council answer. The convoy remained alive inside an argument whose deadline belonged to fuel and distance.

Levan closed the predictive draft. “Show me the present quorum.”

“You have access.”

“I asked you.”

Her expression hardened by a degree. “Vela concurs. Kite concurs. Orison's observed reply supports continuity pending ecological review. Bracken's model remains above point nine nine. Lumen's exceeds point nine eight. Nacre will not willingly abandon arrival when its heat margin tightens. Palisade has already allowed technical inspection of its reserve.”

“That amounts to three replies, two forecasts, an assumption about Nacre, and preparation that does not release mass.”

“It amounts to a fleet that will die if every absence receives the power of refusal.”

“An absence can delay a particular authority without stopping every pump.”

“Tell that to Crown geometry.”

He knew the answer she expected. Crown could not wait indefinitely. Nor could the alternative. Nacre's model required Palisade to strip shielding, dim gardens, and divide human arrival across nineteen years. It carried no painless moral advantage. Yselle had circulated its cost tables more widely than Anja's legal challenge, perhaps because honest scarcity served Vela's defense.

Levan touched the paper folder. “What is this?”

“The drafting record you asked to unseal.”

He opened it. The sheets reproduced Annex Twelve's first committee language, including his handwritten changes. Eleven years of storage had preserved the tilt of his notes. One sentence near the center bore a box in dark ink.

Forecast response may support reversible scheduling during declared synchronization failure. It shall not constitute observed assent, extinguish a pending objection, or authorize an act whose material effects cannot be recovered after receipt.

The enacted annex retained the first sentence. Administrative amendments had thinned the rest into definitions, then moved those definitions outside the public text.

“You found the limitation,” Levan said.

“I have always known it existed.”

“Then why bring it?”

“Because your intent does not govern forever.” Yselle placed one finger on the boxed paragraph. “Law acquires use. Crown alignment remained reversible when preparation began. Every physical stage followed the continuity plan. No burn has crossed Thale. No habitat has surrendered shield mass. The observed replies can still alter execution.”

“Nacre's engineers show that spent trajectory cannot be restored by calling it preparation.”

“They show an alternative whose own first step becomes irreversible before Palisade can guarantee survival.”

“That does not enlarge Annex Twelve.”

“It identifies the choice the annex must govern.”

For a moment Levan saw the argument as he once would have written it. A rule drafted during synchronized pump failures could not list every later emergency. The continuity plan might need actions more complex than starting and stopping machinery. If command waited for perfectly completed causal quorum while a braking window closed, legal purity could kill the represented cohorts. Emergency interpretation existed because facts arrived faster than amendment.

Yet every emergency interpretation still needed a boundary.

Yselle drew the predictive opinion back onto the pane. “Strike the phrase provisional consent if it troubles you. Call the forecasts continuity indicators. State that Vela may preserve Crown until observed testimony resolves the dispute.”

“Preserve means advance.”

“Sometimes.”

“Advance means spend alternatives.”

“Every hour spends alternatives, including Anja Kest's delay.”

Her use of Anja's name sounded neither contemptuous nor personal. Yselle understood sequence law. She had built her authority inside its gaps. Levan could not dismiss her as someone confused by clocks.

He asked, “Will you state publicly that causal quorum has not completed?”

“No. The emergency amendments define operational completion.”

“Will you state that several green concurrences contain no human source signature?”

“The provenance remains available.”

“Three expansions below the command display.”

“Captains can request it.”

“An answer hidden behind effort does not become observed.”

Yselle looked toward the floor as another beamer phase passed through it. “I am offering you a chance to constrain what comes next. Sign a narrow opinion. Preserve review. Without counsel's defense, operations will rely on command necessity alone.”

“You would proceed without legal authority.”

“I would proceed under captain and director authority for fleet survival, subject to later judgment.”

“That judgment will arrive after the options disappear.”

“All useful judgments do.”

Levan let the sentence stand. He had admired that severity once. It kept Yselle from pretending every decision could remain clean. It also gave harm a habit of presenting itself as maturity.

“What happens if I refuse?” he asked.

“Your access moves to review status. Another counsel evaluates the annex. You retain quarters and ordinary communication, but no privileged channel.”

“And if I testify?”

“That depends on what you disclose.”

The paper pages lay between them. Committee drafts carried partial privilege. His own intent did not. Public enactment history did. The checksum he had sent Anja identified one drafting note but contained none of its substance. Yselle had opened the record now because she wanted his narrower defense anchored to it.

Levan turned to the final page. “Give me one hour.”

“You have thirty minutes before the next Crown commitment update.”

“My testimony cannot arrive at Nacre before that update.”

“Your opinion can enter Vela operations locally.”

The distinction clarified her deadline. She did not need law to reach the fleet. She needed it to authorize an event here before another ship could answer.

“I will use thirty minutes,” he said.

Yselle left the folder. At the door she paused. “Do not confuse disclosure with repair.”

“I do not.”

“Good. Guilt often wants an audience more than a remedy.”

After she departed, the chamber sealed under ordinary privacy. Levan disconnected the predictive draft from the signature pane.

He began with the public text.

Annex Twelve allowed forecast systems to schedule reversible work during a synchronization emergency. It did not call a forecast a vote. Levan placed those statements first and attached the enacted source pages. He added the amendment trail that changed “fully reversible upon observed reply” into “required to preserve continuity pending reply.” Each alteration carried an authority stamp. None supplied a human emission path for the later forecast ballots.

Next he opened his committee minutes. Privilege warnings covered discussions with captains and habitat delegates. Levan excluded them. He did not need private argument to establish what the final text permitted. A public clerk's record showed his own explanation at adoption:

Reversible scheduling permits machinery and personnel to prepare against a likely instruction where delay creates hazard. It does not permit the forecast to occupy the authority of the absent speaker.

He had forgotten the exact sentence. Hearing his younger voice from the record produced no revelation, only embarrassment at how plainly he had once drawn the line.

The harder section concerned complicity.

Administrative guidance bearing his signature had approved the testimony-field mapping. Six months earlier, Quon's office asked whether a continuity indicator could populate condensed legal views while observed replies remained pending. Levan had answered yes, provided provenance remained accessible and irreversible execution awaited quorum. He had not inspected how the green display erased that condition from ordinary sight. He had not asked why a scheduling aid needed a testimony field at all.

He wrote the admission without excuse.

I approved an administrative mapping that allowed forecast outputs to appear in testimony fields. I relied on provenance access and on a boundary against irreversible use. That approval made the present substitution easier. It did not lawfully authorize the substitution.

Before publishing, Levan tested the mapping against the live quorum assembler. His counsel sandbox could replay a legal transformation without touching operations. He loaded Bracken's forecast concurrence, removed the predictive-continuity rule, and watched the object settle into a scheduling pane. It retained its confidence value, culture-model sources, and expected answer. Quorum assembly rejected it because no council key had signed.

Restoring his approved guidance moved the same object into a testimony field. That step alone did not complete the fraud. A flag still labeled the field PENDING OBSERVED REPLY. The quorum assembler kept Bracken's seat open.

Levan followed the later amendments. One from operations allowed continuity fields to satisfy a condensed status display. Another treated display completion as sufficient for preparatory authority. A third redefined preparation to include every action needed to protect the selected continuity plan. No single sentence declared that prediction equaled consent. The substitutions formed a ladder, and he had secured its first rung to the wall.

He removed his guidance while leaving every later rule intact. Bracken's seat opened again. Crown's legal status dropped from complete to disputed, although its engineering schedule remained available. Pumps did not stop. The beamer did not lose tracking data. Operators retained every forecast they needed for collision and thermal work. Only the claim of received authority disappeared.

That result carried a danger of its own. A future tribunal could mistake the sandbox replay for proof that current operations would remain safe after legal correction. The replay tested classification, not survival. Levan exported its run conditions and attached a limitation in plain language: no engineering consequence certified.

A second replay used Kite's observed concurrence after its council received the relevant evidence through a complete and publicly inspectable receipt path. The council signature passed through every rule without forecast substitution. Its seat remained filled after Levan disabled the disputed mappings. That contrast mattered more than an abstract objection. The legal system already knew how to accept an actual answer. Administrators had built another path around waiting.

He added the two replays to his evidentiary list, then removed the generated result files. Any other counsel could reproduce them from public records. Publishing outputs alone would ask the fleet to trust his machine; publishing the method allowed hostile readers to choose their own packet and watch the boundary move.

His counsel key could publish a legal interpretation to every habitat's Concord mirror. The route existed so Vela could not privately change fleet law. It required a source signature, a local witness from records staff, and release through the public laser queue. Yselle could suspend his key, but no suspension had entered the access ledger yet.

Levan called records officer Sami Jor.

The officer appeared by local channel from a room forty meters away. “Counsel?”

“I need witness service on a public annex interpretation.”

“Director's release?”

“Counsel publication under disputed authority.”

Sami looked offscreen at the access pane. “Your key remains valid.”

“Will you witness source and content without certifying the conclusion?”

“Yes. Send it to my isolated reader.”

Levan transferred the text through local cable. No light-hour latency complicated a room on the same ship, but the system still recorded emission and receipt. Sami chose a records node, verified the public attachments, and flagged two privileged citations Levan had missed. He removed them. Their absence weakened the narrative and strengthened the evidence.

“Your drafting note?” Sami asked.

“Checksum only. The full note remains sealed.”

“Then readers cannot inspect the language you say governed your intent.”

“They can inspect the enacted limitation, my public adoption statement, and the amendment trail. My private note supports my memory. It cannot substitute for public law.”

Sami read the complicity paragraph again. “You are waiving personal privilege over your approval.”

“Yes.”

“Not committee privilege?”

“No. Other people own portions of that record.”

The officer added a witness line: content received from Levan's counsel key; attachments verified as public enactment records; excluded materials not attested.

A countdown in the chamber showed eleven minutes before Crown's next commitment update. Levan could still produce the narrow opinion Yselle wanted. He could argue that local preparation remained lawful until actual energy delivery. Such an opinion would preserve his position long enough to influence later limits. It would also enter operations immediately while his correction traveled outward for hours.

He deleted the unsigned opinion.

The public testimony needed a conclusion precise enough to resist both uses of him. Anja could not treat his regret as proof of every accusation. Yselle could not treat operational necessity as observed assent.

Levan dictated the final section.

I offer observed testimony concerning the drafting and administration of Annex Twelve. Forecast outputs were authorized for reversible scheduling. They were not authorized to stand as human or council votes. My later guidance allowed forecasts to enter condensed testimony fields while awaiting observed replies. I approved that guidance and failed to protect the boundary I had stated at adoption. A forecast may help a habitat anticipate another. It cannot create receipt, deliberation, signature, or consent. No confidence value repairs the missing acts.

He stopped the recording and checked each claim against an attachment. The room clock gave him seven minutes.

Sami asked through the line, “Do you state whether Crown itself must stop?”

“No. Engineering facts and council authority decide that question.”

“People will hear a stop order.”

“Then the text must survive what people hear.”

Levan added one sentence.

This testimony does not certify the distributed-brake model, minimize its cost, or direct a habitat's vote. It addresses only the authority claimed from answers that no habitat sent.

At three minutes, he signed.

Sami witnessed the local receipt. The counsel publication system wrapped the document in Vela's source clock and offered nine outgoing paths. Levan selected all habitats, then added Mote's legal relay even though Nadi held no fleet vote. Destination evidence had acquired a habit of reaching her last.

The send control asked whether the filing might impair active emergency coordination.

“Yes,” he said.

A second prompt required grounds.

“False legal reliance creates greater impairment.”

Sami's witness key completed the release. The packet moved into the public laser queue.

One minute remained on Yselle's clock.

Security reached the chamber before the first carrier confirmation.

Two officers entered under director authority. Neither drew a weapon. Their supervisor, Olan Ver, read from a local order stating that Levan's privileged access had moved to custodial review pending inquiry into unauthorized disclosure.

“I disclosed public records and my own approval,” Levan said.

“That question belongs to review.”

“Has the public packet been recalled?”

Olan did not answer. He placed a hand terminal on the desk. “Surrender counsel key and private storage.”

Levan removed the key from his wrist housing. The loss felt smaller than he had expected, a piece of warm ceramic resting on his palm. His private storage held forty-seven years of opinions, correspondence, and corrected mistakes. He set it beside the key.

The chamber pane flashed CARRIER ACQUIRED.

Vela's first tightbeam had taken the packet toward Nacre. A second emitter aligned toward Kite. Security could halt later transmissions. It could send a retraction after the first. It could not pull emitted light back through the hull.

Olan saw the notice. “Step away from the pane.”

Levan obeyed.

A director message entered locally as the officers sealed his storage. Yselle's recorded face appeared above the desk.

“Counsel Oris, your publication contains disputed interpretations during a declared synchronization emergency. Pending review, you are confined to quarters and barred from legal or communication systems. Vela will preserve your testimony with its objections.”

Even now she did not erase him. Preservation with objections offered a stronger defense than suppression. She would answer his text, separate intent from enacted authority, and tell the fleet that three actual concurrences plus survival necessity outweighed one drafter's remorse. Her argument deserved reception too.

Levan asked Olan for the order's source copy. The officer hesitated, then transmitted it to his temporary custodial pane. Confinement had a clock and a signer. That mattered more now than whether Levan could defeat it.

They escorted him into Vela's central passage. Crown phasing trembled through the deck. Workers continued toward beam control, thermal stations, and fabrication lifts. None looked at him long. The habitat had no leisure for a lawyer's moral theater.

At the first junction, his custodial pane received a queue status through allowed system traffic. Nacre path emitted. Kite path emitted. Bracken alignment pending. Each carrier moved at light speed and no faster, carrying his signed account toward people who had not heard it yet.

The quarters door closed behind him with a mechanical bolt.

Levan placed the confinement order beside the queue receipt. His testimony had entered the convoy. Its first answer could not come for nearly two hours, and by then Vela would have advanced without his signature.

Chapter 18 — What Pera Answered

Anja opened Levan's testimony on a reader that had never held Pera's name.

She used a spare device from Nacre's pressure-control office, wiped its local cache, and disconnected every master-time feed. Sumi verified the empty storage twice. Only then did they load the public packet Vela had emitted before confining its counsel.

Levan's source signature passed. The records officer's witness line passed. Two attachments remained absent because committee privilege still covered them. What survived showed enough: Annex Twelve had permitted forecasts for reversible scheduling, while later mappings placed the results inside testimony fields. Levan included a replay method that removed each administrative rule in turn.

Anja did not begin with the Crown quorum.

She entered the identifier from Ardent's old allocation response.

The isolated reader located the packet envelope in Nacre's casualty mirror. Its encrypted content remained sealed under Captain Osei's authority key. Carrier history reached back eleven years through three storage migrations. Ardent had emitted the response before the local limit it had received. Vela had received it after operations retroactively moved the last-safe emission point. A classifier marked the packet late, and the closure summary excluded it.

Beside that actual reply, Meridian's historical model predicted acceptance of closure at 0.982 confidence.

Sumi sat across the archive table with a hardware key between two fingers. “Same mapping?”

“Earlier version,” Anja said. “Same destination field.”

“Test it.”

They loaded the packet chain into Levan's replay. The 2238 software could not run inside the current reader, so Sumi supplied a documented compatibility layer that exposed each old classification without translating its meaning. Anja chose the output fields by identifier. She refused the reader's offer to align them in current master time.

The first stage displayed Pera's packet as allocation response, received after amended closure. Its content status read SEALED OBSERVED. The forecast occupied a separate operations field labeled expected acceptance.

A later administrative stage applied the deadline correction. The actual response moved into nonoperative traffic. The forecast stayed visible because models retained provenance for future scheduling.

The closure assembler then searched for an operative answer. Finding none, it imported expected acceptance as continuity status. A condensed summary rendered Ardent compliant with closure.

No forged signature appeared. No clock ran backward. One event left the legal question; one prediction entered the empty place.

Anja made Sumi reset the reader.

“Choose the storage node,” she said.

“You already know what happens.”

“Osei will ask whether I chose the only copy that agrees.”

Sumi selected a hull mirror created during Nacre's second archive migration. Its thermal history carried two damaged sectors, neither near the packet. They repeated the chain. The classification result matched.

A third copy came from Vela's public casualty register, received in response to Osei's sealed request. Light had taken fifty-six minutes to bring that copy to Nacre. Vela had released it before Levan's packet arrived there from its own public queue, so the two records crossed in transit without either carrying knowledge of the other. The Vela copy produced the same transformation.

Anja wrote three results on paper. She did not write Pera was right. She wrote packet excluded after receipt, forecast retained, closure summary populated from forecast.

“Now we open the content,” Sumi said.

“First we establish who has authority.”

“Osei signed the seal.”

“For casualty access. The council response belonged to Ardent's cohort.”

The current Ardent council had sent a limited release after receiving Anja's evidence request. Its source clock placed deliberation six hours after receipt. Eleven years had not made the packet Nacre's property. The release allowed inspection and public correction of procedural status. It barred publication of personal medical data and shelter locations. Pera had spoken under Ardent's emergency council key, but seven other people carried authority in the response.

Sumi traced the release path. “Complete.”

Anja checked again. Ardent source, relay receipt, Nacre receipt, council key. Every event followed the one before it. No predicted permission filled a missing signature.

Only then did she place Osei's hardware seal into the reader.

The encrypted payload opened without sound.

Pera appeared at a foundry control station with emergency fabric stretched across the cracked wall behind her.

The image carried heat damage and three horizontal tears. Pera wore a shelter respirator loose around her throat. Sweat had darkened the collar of her work shirt. Two council delegates stood within camera range; five more supplied signed audio from elsewhere in Ardent. A pressure alarm cut across the opening statement.

Anja stopped the record before Pera spoke.

Her sister had died at twenty-six, an age Anja now understood as young in ways she had not understood at twenty-seven. Memory kept revising Pera's face toward the last family image, where she grinned over a cup and leaned too close to the lens. The council record showed someone at work, frightened enough to breathe through her mouth and disciplined enough to read a pressure value before addressing Vela.

Sumi waited without looking at Anja.

“Run from source,” Anja said.

Pera's voice arrived through old compression.

ARDENT DOES NOT ACCEPT CLOSURE. LOCAL CONTAINMENT REMAINS POSSIBLE FOR SIX MORE HOURS.

The rest of the observed reply requested Palisade's mobile shield mass. It also rejected forecast acceptance and any removal of Ardent's reply interval.

The record showed the action behind those words. Five council members rejected closure. One abstained because her shelter lacked the current thermal table. A seventh seat stayed empty after its holder entered the damaged coolant trunk. Amel signed the council rejection. Pera signed as industrial systems organizer and attested only to capacity.

Attached traces documented pressure, thermal slope, shelter count, and the travel estimate for Palisade's mobile mass. The response did not deny the danger to Palisade. It proposed a release after the forward shield cooled below its peak, with Ardent accepting replacement liability if its foundries survived.

A later update followed after conditions worsened.

PRIOR RESPONSE REMAINS VALID AT ITS SOURCE TIME.

The update reported degrading containment and requested immediate mass release if the transit window remained physically open.

The change mattered. Six hours described capacity at the earlier source time. Later damage narrowed the remaining interval without turning the first observation into a lie. Both statements had traveled lawfully. Operations had classified both outside the closed allocation window.

Anja watched the final source clock continue for three seconds after the image failed.

Nothing in the replies proved Palisade would have released mass. Nothing proved the shelter seals would have held. Pera had proposed risk for people on another habitat and liability no dead council could pay. Vela might have refused after lawful consideration.

The public conclusion had claimed something narrower and false: no observed objection received before closure.

Sumi closed the image while leaving the packet chain visible. “Do you want to hear it again?”

“No.”

“You always make me rerun the evidence.”

“I will rerun the verification. I do not need her face for that.”

Anja checked each signature against Ardent's historical key registry. Two keys had expired with their holders' deaths, but the archive retained their validity at source time. She matched the attached pressure table to a checksum in the foundry telemetry register. Sumi verified that the encrypted payload still matched Vela's original received light after the storage migrations.

Then they tested the timing claim.

Ardent's council had emitted seventeen minutes before the deadline displayed locally. The first relay received the packet after forty-eight minutes. Vela received it fifty-one minutes later. Operations moved the derived emission cutoff backward fourteen minutes while the packet traveled. Under the changed rule, the response became eight minutes late.

Anja's old certification followed the amendment.

She opened her signature beside the recovered reply. The two objects did not cancel each other. Pera had answered. Anja had certified a summary stating that no answer existed. Her signature remained valid as an observed act and wrong as a conclusion.

“I saw the correction,” she said.

Sumi looked up from the checksum pane. “You saw a routing amendment.”

“I certified its legal effect.”

“Yes.”

“You do not need to protect me from the verb.”

“I am protecting the record from your appetite for blame.” Sumi pointed at the chain. “That packet establishes what the council said. Your certification establishes what you signed. Your guilt establishes nothing we can publish.”

Eleven years earlier, Anja had believed responsibility required taking every causal path into herself. If she could have inspected one more register, challenged one more correction, demanded one more hour, perhaps Pera would have lived. The counterfactual enlarged until it contained Palisade's shielding, Vela's deadlines, failed coolant, relay congestion, and every person who acted under pressure. It made Anja central to events that had never belonged to one person.

Centrality offered a private comfort. If the whole failure came from her signature, then correcting her might correct the whole failure.

The reply denied her that comfort. Pera had named several possible decisions and asked that each be recorded honestly. She had not promised survival. She had not asked Anja to become the cause of every outcome.

“Build the correction from the packet,” Anja said.

Before Sumi opened the public form, Captain Osei joined by secure local channel from the thermal gallery. Her image arrived without visible delay because copper and fiber linked two rooms on Nacre. A radiator curve occupied half her background.

“I have twenty minutes,” Osei said. “Prove the correction as though I want to keep the old finding.”

Anja placed the three carrier copies on the shared pane. “Ardent emitted an emergency-council response before the deadline displayed to Ardent. Vela received it intact. Operations changed the derived emission cutoff while the packet traveled, then classified it as late. The closure assembler excluded the observed response and retained a forecast acceptance.”

“Could Ardent have known the cutoff might move?”

“Emergency rules allowed routing estimates to change.”

“That is not an answer.”

“The transmitted deadline contained an uncertainty notice. It did not state that a later estimate would govern packets already emitted.”

Osei selected the old routing table. “Could Vela lawfully decide that receipt before closure mattered more than emission?”

“It could make that rule prospectively and expose the consequence. Here, the summary claimed no objection had been received. Vela had received one before Anja's certification, although after operational closure.” Sumi enlarged the Vela receipt. “The packet sat in the nonoperative register.”

“What exactly did Pera's council refuse?”

“Closure of the mass request,” Anja said. “They proposed a conditional hold. They did not order Palisade to detach shielding.”

“Could their plan have worked?”

“The packet cannot answer. Later coolant reports weaken it. Palisade's exposure model gives reasons for refusal.”

“Then your supplement must not call this a stolen rescue.”

“It will not.”

“Did your sister send a personal message?”

“Yes.”

“Does it corroborate the refusal?”

“No. It has no council authority, no policy content, and no proof of receipt by Vela.”

Osei leaned toward something outside the frame, adjusted a thermal control, then returned. “Suppose the private words describe the same six-hour request.”

“They still do not enter the legal chain.”

“Suppose they contradict it.”

Anja felt the question in her hands. “Then I retain both under their proper authorities. A family message cannot void the council packet either.”

“Good. Now show me the substitution without Levan.”

They ran the historical chain alone. The observed packet moved into excluded traffic after the retroactive correction. Forecast acceptance remained available. Closure assembly imported continuity status. Anja then removed the forecast object. The assembler returned NO OPERATIVE RESPONSE rather than acceptance.

Osei asked for the model's original inputs. Prior Ardent closures, Pera's earlier willingness to accept central allocations, shelter survival estimates, and the first call all contributed to 0.982. The model had performed its designed task: estimate behavior under incomplete information. Nothing in its calculation claimed that Pera had spoken.

“Meridian did not alter the council packet,” Anja said. “Administrative rules used its output to populate a field left empty by another administrative rule.”

“Keep human verbs in the supplement.”

“I will.”

Finally, Osei opened Anja's old certification. “What correction do you seek for yourself?”

“None separate from the record.”

“You certified a false sentence.”

“I will identify that certification and preserve it beside the supplement.”

“Are you asking Ardent to absolve you?”

“No.”

“Are you asking Nacre to find liability?”

“Not in this action.”

The captain's expression relaxed without becoming kind. “Then my seal covers publication of procedural content under Ardent's release. Shelter details remain closed. State the uncertainties. Preserve every prior line.”

A warning flashed behind her. She glanced toward it. “Tomas needs another garden-dark test. I have to decide whether he gets it.”

“Palisade has not answered?” Sumi asked.

“Only carrier acknowledgment. Do not turn that into release.”

Osei ended the channel.

Sumi unlocked a clean public-record pane.

The correction form offered three authorities: clerical error, new evidence, or overturned finding.

Anja rejected clerical error. The exclusion had followed the rule in force after amendment. Calling it a mistake would hide the administrative choice. She rejected overturned finding because no tribunal had yet judged liability. New evidence implied that Pera's answer had only now come into existence for the public record, though Vela had possessed it all along.

She selected supplemental sequence account and attached an explanation.

The 2238 Ardent closure summary states that no observed objection was received before closure. The raw carrier record establishes an observed Ardent emergency-council response emitted before the deadline available to Ardent and received by Vela after a retroactive change to the derived emission cutoff. The response was classified as nonoperative traffic. A forecast acceptance then populated continuity status. This supplement restores the observed response, its source clock, its receipt path, and the classification history. It does not determine whether Palisade would have released shielding or whether the requested hold would have prevented casualties.

Sumi read the paragraph. “Add your certification.”

“It already appears in the old summary.”

“People will open the supplement without expanding it.”

Anja added a direct statement.

Sequence Auditor Anja Kest certified the original closure summary after the retroactive cutoff amendment. That certification remains part of the history and did not account for the excluded response register.

The form asked whether to replace the old summary.

“No,” Anja said.

Sumi had not spoken.

Anja chose retain with contradiction notice. Anyone opening the closure would see both accounts. The old conclusion would no longer appear clean, but neither could later editors pretend it had never governed allocation.

They attached Levan's replay method as comparative evidence, not as authority over the old case. Annex Twelve had not existed during Ardent. The later system exposed a repeated architecture, yet it could not travel backward and become the rule that excluded Pera. Anja described the resemblance in an audit note and kept the legal mechanisms separate.

Next came the content release. Ardent had authorized publication of procedural statements but not shelter details. Sumi redacted compartment identifiers from the public video while preserving them under seal. The words of refusal remained. So did the condition involving Palisade, the requested interval, and Pera's demand that silence not replace an answer.

A correction required Nacre's audit signature and Ardent's release. Vela's casualty register permitted an external supplemental account but marked disputed corrections until its own custodian replied. Anja could publish without pretending Vela agreed.

She signed at 14:31 Nacre source.

The public packet entered nine transmission queues. Ardent would receive its own recovered account after more than five hours. Vela would receive it in under an hour. Different councils would see the correction in different orders relative to Levan's testimony and Palisade's pending decision. Sumi placed no green common-status bar above them.

“Carrier lock on Vela,” she said.

A beam left Nacre.

“Ardent path aligned.”

The second emission followed.

The public sequence record on Nacre changed immediately because Nacre had now observed the correction. Its Ardent closure page opened with a red contradiction notice. Beneath the old conclusion, a new line showed the excluded response and the classification that had hidden it.

Anja read the page without hearing Pera's voice.

A separate sealed item remained in the casualty drawer.

Pera's family packets had never belonged to the emergency council record.

Anja brought the damaged message onto another reader after Sumi removed the public key. It carried family encryption, no council signature, and no allocation attachments. It could not prove what Ardent requested. It could not establish who received the request or what authority followed. Its only legal relevance concerned custody if someone later claimed it had been suppressed.

The old packet still yielded the same three words Anja had known for eleven years.

Wait for me.

A second private object appeared behind the council response in Ardent's lawful outgoing queue. Storage damage had separated it from the family index during migration. The newly restored header linked it to Pera's recorder without joining it to the public testimony. Anja opened the audio under the same family authority.

Pera spoke over a reduced structural alarm.

Anja, I answered while I could answer. That has to remain true even if nobody waits.

The opening of Pera's private line had warned Anja against searching for a clock that could make the choice for them.

The recording ended before the containment failure. Pera had made no claim on Palisade and carried no council seal. Her private line described what she wanted her sister to remember, not what fleet law had to recognize.

Anja attached neither family object to the correction.

She restored both packets to the sealed index and left their words outside the public case. Then she closed the reader. Grief could keep what law did not need.

Sumi returned the hardware key to its envelope. “Do you want the family copy transferred to your quarters?”

“No. Keep it under the casualty seal.”

“You have authority now.”

“Authority does not require possession.”

Beyond the archive wall, Nacre's garden cycle shifted. Pumps changed pitch as the lamps in one ring entered their reduced schedule for Tomas's model tests. Palisade had not yet released anything. Levan remained confined aboard Vela. Crown preparation continued. Correcting an old record spent no shield mass and moved no ship.

Still, one public sequence had changed from silence to reply.

At 14:44, Nacre's casualty mirror completed the update. The page showed Pera's observed refusal beneath the source clock that had always accompanied it. Anja's erroneous certification remained directly above.

She left both lines visible and locked the record against normalization.

Chapter 19 — A Derogatory Record

Vela returned Pera's correction with a phrase Anja had never seen in current law.

The objection reached Nacre at 09:22 local, fifty-four minutes after its Vela source time. Sumi Dax caught the carrier envelope before Meridian placed it beside the Ardent casualty file. Two clocks remained visible above the document. The delay offered no comfort. It merely showed that Vela had spent almost two days preparing its answer.

Anja opened the objection in the sequence well.

A deputy counsel named Rhea Mar had signed because Levan Oris remained under custodial review. Mar accepted the photonic integrity of Ardent's packet. She accepted that Pera's council had emitted an objection under its local deadline. She disputed Nacre's authority to alter the fleet closure record and defended the retroactive routing correction as necessary emergency practice.

Her final paragraph widened beyond Ardent.

Nacre now proposes that every local source frame retain independent legal force against a reconciled fleet account. Such raw files may support audit, but they cannot govern common action. A collection of divergent clocks, incomplete receipts, private corrections, and contradictory testimony amounts to a parallax testament: nine accounts of one emergency with no authority to decide among them.

The phrase carried an archival citation from the Concord's first routing dispute. Anja copied the citation onto paper.

Sumi read the paragraph again. “She meant that to sound diseased.”

“It used to.”

“You know the term?”

“I know parallax objection. Not testament.”

“Same family of insult.”

Anja opened the cited record. Thirty-five years earlier, during the crisis that produced the Concord of Frames, ship captains had submitted local logs that disagreed about when a fuel order became binding. A Vela jurist dismissed them as a parallax testament, useful for blame but unfit for command. The final Concord required source clocks and receipt paths, then authorized a reconciled operational display. The insult had disappeared from public doctrine while its premise survived.

One emergency needed one answer. Therefore one display had to decide which events counted.

Anja laid Mar's objection beside Pera's recovered reply. The documents did not disagree about the carrier path. They disagreed about legal effect. A master record could display that dispute honestly, provided it preserved both claims and the rules transforming them. Meridian's current view did not. It showed Vela's objection as authoritative commentary and Nacre's supplement as disputed local material.

“Give them a reconciliation,” Anja said.

Sumi tilted her head. “You want to answer the insult by building what they asked for?”

“I want to see where it breaks.”

They opened a clean master-time workspace.

At first the correction looked possible.

Anja entered Pera's emission, Vela's receipt, the original deadline, the later routing amendment, closure, her certification, and Nacre's supplement. Each event kept a source object beneath its converted time. The display showed uncertainty bands instead of thin lines. Nothing vanished.

Then she added the forecast acceptance.

The workspace asked which event occupied Ardent's answer field during the closure interval. If Anja chose Pera's packet, the system warned that Vela had classified it after operative closure. If she chose the forecast, the system warned that no human source signature existed. If she left the field open, the historical closure lost its completed status even though operations had acted on completion eleven years earlier.

“Use two fields,” Sumi said.

Anja separated observed answer from operative status. Pera occupied the first. Forecast acceptance occupied the second. The display held.

Juniper broke it.

Its observed rejection concerned the complete Mote supplement. The earlier forecast concurrence lacked that evidence. A master timeline could place both, but a single vote field required a choice about which evidence object governed. Anja added an evidence-receipt layer. Kite's observed concurrence then appeared earlier than Juniper's rejection in master time, though Kite had answered an older bundle.

They split votes by evidence digest.

Levan's testimony introduced another problem. It addressed Annex Twelve's authority, not Crown's ecological merits. Pera's reply concerned an emergency eleven years earlier. Tomas's model concerned material feasibility. Nadi's fragments established native life. Meridian could align clocks but could not collapse those different claims into one legal status without someone defining relevance.

“Relevance needs a signer,” Anja said.

“Whose?”

“That question caused the first problem.”

Sumi pushed away from her board. “We are building a more elaborate green bar.”

Anja looked at the workspace. Forty-seven layers sat beneath one proposed fleet state. A captain could expand each. A sequence auditor might inspect all of them. Under pressure, most people would read the top line and act. Whatever rule composed that line would acquire the force of hidden judgment.

She deleted the top line.

The layers remained as separate records linked by receipt and response. Without a master conclusion, they resembled a pile of unfinished cases.

“Parallax testament,” Sumi said.

This time the phrase sounded like a technical description.

Anja took the paper citation from her pocket. The old jurist had feared logs that could accuse but not coordinate. Mar feared the same thing now. Both assumed that preserving difference barred action. Yet Tomas's distributed model did not require one account of a common present. It required bounded local decisions tied to received packets. A habitat could act on evidence inside its own past light cone and publish the path that justified its action.

Coordination could follow relations rather than a universal status.

“Keep every local account,” Anja said.

“We already do.”

“Not as a public structure. We treat raw files as inputs waiting for reconciliation. Keep them as records capable of citing one another without merging.”

Sumi's expression sharpened. “Content links.”

“Explain.”

“A packet checksum can name an object without naming where it sits in master time. Every receipt can cite the exact object received. Every reply can cite its evidence object. A correction can cite the statement it disputes. Ships host what has reached them and exchange missing objects when paths open.”

“Could Vela rewrite an object after citation?”

“Not without breaking the photonic checksum and every downstream link.”

“Could Nacre claim a receipt another ship has not observed?”

“We can claim our local receipt. The other ship sees our signed claim until the carrier proof reaches it. Provenance stays graded.”

“Could Meridian normalize the archive?”

“It can calculate views. It cannot replace source objects if users retain the content links.”

The idea did not yet deserve a name. Anja asked Sumi for a storage estimate.

The first estimate failed by a factor of twelve.

Raw photonic samples consumed too much space for every ship to host every transmission. Nacre's three hull nodes stored interference grain for protected packets, but they did not preserve full optical data for ordinary traffic. Copying everything would displace maintenance logs and medical archives.

Sumi separated proof layers. Each public record could carry content, source signature, source clock, relay receipts, and compact checksums of the independent photonic samples. Full grain stayed in distributed vaults. A challenge could request it from any surviving node. No single habitat needed every physical copy.

Anja tested loss. “Suppose Vela withholds its grain.”

“Nacre still has the received scatter sample for packets sent here. Kite may hold another. A Vela-only local order remains less independently witnessed.”

“Show that limitation.”

“Every object gets a custody depth.”

“Not a color.”

Sumi removed the proposed green, amber, and red icons. “A list, then. Source signature verified. One external receipt. Three scatter nodes. Full grain unavailable. Let readers see what exists.”

Privacy produced the next failure.

Pera's council packet contained shelter locations under seal. Publishing its whole content would violate Ardent's release. Levan's testimony cited privileged exclusions. Nadi's medical telemetry included crew data from Mote. A checksum link named an object even when its content remained hidden, which could reveal that a private packet existed at a politically charged time.

They designed split manifests. A public object held authorized text and a checksum of the sealed source. The custody layer proved that the redaction matched a retained original without exposing protected content. Access authority remained with the owning cohort. Anja's private family message stayed outside the public graph entirely; the correction cited no part of it.

“People will claim the sealed objects contain whatever helps them,” Sumi said.

“They already do.”

“Now they can point to a checksum and make the absence sound official.”

“Then sealed existence must carry no inferred content.”

“That rule needs enforcement.”

“No archive can enforce thought.”

Anja heard the dryness in her own answer and almost smiled. “It can label what a checksum proves. Identity and custody. Nothing else.”

Sumi added the limitation to the manifest format.

A third failure came from divergence. If every habitat hosted what it had received, no two copies would match at the same instant. Nacre might hold Levan's testimony while Ardent still waited for it. Vela might hold Nadi's latest fragment before Nacre. Ordinary replication software treated difference as corruption and tried to converge toward a preferred state.

They prohibited complete-state claims.

Each host published a local inventory with its own source clock. Inventories could differ without invalidating either. When a new object arrived, the host appended receipt rather than rewriting earlier absence. A blank field meant not held here at this inventory time, not nonexistent.

Sumi then attacked the design as though Vela had sent a hostile file. She duplicated Juniper's rejection, changed one digit in its evidence checksum, and gave the copy a later master-time label. The prototype initially displayed both objects as separate votes. A hurried reader might have mistaken the altered copy for a reaffirmation.

“Source identity before display order,” Anja said.

“Source key can sign several versions.”

“Then link each revision to the object it revises.”

“An attacker can omit the link.”

“Show it as an unrelated claim.”

Sumi changed the renderer. Signed content without a declared relationship no longer replaced an older object. Both remained visible, but only explicit citation created revision history. The altered checksum broke its claimed connection to the Mote supplement. The view labeled it unsupported by named evidence rather than false. Someone might possess evidence Nacre lacked.

A second attack used valid content inside a corrupted manifest. Sumi nested the same receipt path through thousands of circular links until the reader stalled. On a habitat under thermal stress, a stalled legal display could become its own form of coercion. She imposed depth limits, isolated each incoming object, and required the reader to render raw text even when relationships failed.

Next she forged an inventory time. The signature check rejected it because the claimed source clock did not match Nacre's key history. She tried a valid local clock attached to a false statement about Vela receipt. That object passed as Nacre's signed claim, exactly as it should. The archive could prove who asserted a thing. It could not make the assertion true without corroborating custody.

Anja added a query for conflicting claims. The result placed Nacre's receipt assertion beside Vela's inventory and every relay proof then available. Where evidence ended, the view said unresolved. It never averaged disagreement into confidence.

The final test removed an object that later records cited. Instead of collapsing the chain, the reader preserved its checksum as a missing dependency. Anyone who possessed the absent packet could supply it. Anyone who did not would still see the shape of what another argument relied on.

“People will hate this,” Sumi said. “Every disagreement stays ugly.”

“Ugly remains preferable to completed by prediction.”

“That will offend every database engineer on the convoy,” Sumi said.

“Can they make it work?”

“They can make anything work if permitted to complain publicly.”

“Give complaints their own source paths.”

Sumi laughed once, then returned to the schema.

They loaded four test cases. Juniper's forecast concurrence linked to its model inputs and no human signature. The observed rejection linked to the complete evidence receipt and Elian Ro's council key. Levan's public testimony linked to Annex Twelve's enacted text, his administrative approval, and two reproducible transformation runs. Pera's correction linked to the old closure, the excluded reply, Ardent's release, and Anja's preserved certification.

No object erased another.

A reader could ask which votes existed after receiving the complete Mote supplement. The local view returned answers whose receipt paths supported that question and listed the others as unarrived or based on earlier evidence. A reader could ask what Vela relied on when Crown preparation advanced. That view returned the forecast fields, the classification rules, and the commitment log. Both views used the same source objects. Neither pretended to deliver a neutral present.

Anja asked, “Can a captain still get one operational display?”

“Yes. Meridian can render one from a declared policy.”

“Declared where?”

“In the view header. Policy identifier, authorizing signature, uncertainty, excluded objects.”

“If a forecast fills a vote?”

“The header must say so.”

“Can the archive stop a captain from using it?”

“No.”

That limit mattered. Transparent records did not create lawful behavior. Yselle could view every missing signature and still choose Crown under emergency authority. The structure gave other habitats something firmer than trust when they judged her claim.

Near second meal, Sumi compiled the first manifest.

The reader rejected it because Nacre's own correction cited a Vela receipt object that Nacre held only as a copied register entry. Sumi changed the link from received object to claimed custody statement. The distinction lengthened the path and reduced its proof depth. It also told the truth.

Anja tried to shorten the display. Each simplification hid an owner, a clock, or a gap. She stopped trying.

Osei reviewed the prototype in the thermal gallery after ship night.

Tomas attended from the drive-model room by local channel. His Palisade request remained acknowledged at carrier level with no council answer. Garden tests had resumed at reduced duration. Behind his image, nine trajectory lines waited on an unresolved mass input.

Captain Osei selected Juniper's forecast object first. “What am I looking at?”

Sumi answered, “A checksum-linked record hosted on Nacre. It shows what we received and how each object cites another. No global completion claim.”

“Who decides the view?”

“You do, and your selection appears in the header.”

Osei chose Crown authority after complete evidence. Four observed replies appeared. Forecasts remained visible under operations but did not fill council seats. She changed the policy to continuity planning. Meridian displayed likely responses and trajectory consequences, each labeled as forecast.

“The same data supports both?” she asked.

“The same hosted objects,” Anja said. “Different declared questions.”

“Can Vela host a version that says Crown holds quorum?”

“Yes.”

“Then what have you solved?”

“We make the substitutions inspectable. Vela must publish which objects and rules produce that conclusion. Another host can reproduce or challenge the view without surrendering its raw receipts.”

Osei opened Pera's old response. The public redaction concealed shelter locations. Ardent's release authority appeared beside the content. Anja's certification stayed attached to the original closure.

“Can you remove your signature?” Osei asked.

“No.”

“Can Vela remove Pera's supplement from its copy?”

“It can refuse to host it. Every other host will show that Vela's inventory lacks an object whose carrier receipts exist elsewhere.”

“Could absence mean delay?”

“The inventory clock and receipt paths distinguish not yet arrived from arrived but not hosted, once enough proofs exist.”

Tomas spoke from the model room. “Can this carry technical assumptions?”

Sumi loaded his distributed-brake manifest. Each branch cited mass tables, field limits, garden schedules, and the pending Palisade request. “Yes. Your preferred model cannot label Palisade mass available until an observed release enters the host.”

“Good.”

Osei did not share his relief. “You are proposing fleet infrastructure during an active braking crisis.”

“A prototype,” Anja said. “Not yet publication.”

Osei opened the host comparison. Nacre's inventory contained Pera's correction and Levan's testimony. A simulated Ardent host lacked Levan because his packet had not yet completed that path at the chosen inventory time. The software did not flag either copy as stale. It showed which light paths had completed and which remained in flight.

“Suppose I ask Ardent whether counsel supports the correction,” Osei said.

“Their local reader answers that his testimony has not arrived,” Sumi replied. “It can also show Nacre's signed claim that we received it, but those are different objects.”

“So command cannot demand one fleet status.”

“Command can demand a view tied to a stated inventory cutoff. It cannot make every host possess the same past.”

Tomas glanced toward his pending Palisade line. “That prevents someone from treating carrier acknowledgment as council release.”

“It exposes the difference,” Anja said. “Prevention still belongs to operators and law.”

“How long before another ship can host it?”

“Two days for a minimal reader,” Sumi replied. “Longer for privacy review and hostile-input testing.”

“We do not have longer.”

“We also cannot distribute a parser that lets one malformed record corrupt a habitat's legal archive.”

Osei examined Mar's phrase in the prototype header. Sumi had entered the objection as an ordinary signed object. Its derogatory language sat beside the old citation and Nacre's design notes.

“Name?” the captain asked.

Anja had resisted naming it because names attracted allegiance before function. Yet Mar had already supplied one that described what they meant to preserve: testimony seen from separated positions, each account altered by distance without becoming false.

“Parallax Archive,” she said.

Sumi entered the provisional title.

The captain looked from the linked records to Tomas's unresolved mass line. “Do not publish until the attack surface has been tested. Do not let this interfere with physical coordination. And do not call it a replacement constitution.”

“It is a record structure,” Anja said.

“For now.”

Osei returned to the radiator board. The review had ended.

Back in the sequence well, Sumi created an empty repository on Nacre's isolated public node. She wrote no conclusions into it. The root manifest contained only the format rules, Nacre's source clock, and the checksum of Vela's objection.

Its first quoted object preserved the words parallax testament exactly as Mar had sent them.

Sumi linked Pera's correction, Levan's testimony, Juniper's two answers, and Tomas's pending request. Every file carried an owner. Every relationship carried a clock. Missing replies remained missing.

At 23:18, the prototype accepted its first complete local inventory.

No other ship held it yet. Sumi began building the reader they could.

Chapter 20 — The Life That Cannot Vote

The demand for intelligence reached Mote eighteen days after Nadi had refused to claim it.

Her lab reader marked the packet as a council inquiry bundle, not a live exchange. Four habitat questions and one Vela memorandum had traveled through separate relay paths, then gathered at Mote's damaged receiver during the same Corva alignment. Their source clocks ranged across six convoy days. The newest question had been asked before any speaker could have heard her full evidence statement.

Nadi kept the clocks visible and opened them in emission order.

Vela asked whether the pellicle demonstrated intentional avoidance of harm. Bracken asked whether ionic signals carried symbols. Kite wanted a repeatable test of memory. An unnamed Nacre council member asked what standing belonged to metabolism without preference. Lumen's medical board asked the least legal question: how much of the under-ice system would Crown exposure destroy?

The Vela memorandum condensed all five into one threshold.

DESTINATION-PROTECTION REVIEW REQUIRES EVIDENCE OF INTELLIGENCE, SENTIENCE, OR INDIVIDUATED INTEREST SUFFICIENT TO JUSTIFY MATERIAL RISK TO HUMAN ARRIVAL.

Nadi read the sentence while Mote crossed above Thale's night hemisphere. The cutter did not orbit low enough to image the ice directly. Its sample-light instrument watched spectra reflected through Corva's magnetospheric glow. Each result reached her after photons left the moon, crossed to Mote, passed damaged optics, and survived software calibration. Nothing in the lab showed Thale now.

She copied Vela's threshold onto a blank response page, then wrote unsupported beside it.

Mote's pilot looked through the lab hatch. “Unsupported by them or by you?”

“By the evidence request.”

“Can you say that without proving a negative?”

“I can say intelligence has not been established. I cannot say it is absent.”

“Will that help the fleet decide?”

“It prevents them from claiming I decided for it.”

A carrier timer opened on the wall. Mote would have eleven minutes of stable alignment with the relay bead after the next sweep. The damaged high-gain antenna could send either the complete seasonal data or a heavily compressed testimony packet in that window. A second path might open five hours later. Might meant geometry, not intention. The antenna gimbal had failed twice since the first fragments left.

Nadi turned from the questions to the latest scan.

The ion data covered one full Corva-shadow cycle and most of the following seasonal shift.

At the beginning, broad active regions followed charged fractures through Thale's upper ice. During shadow, those regions contracted toward warmer vents. A cold-water break tore one membrane terrace.
