The damaged edges sealed. Thin film spread across the cuts during later vent pulses. When particle flux returned, current crossed the new film through channels that differed from the older tissue.

Those observations supported repair. They did not support pain.

Across thirty-one Thale days, the brightest ionic region moved away from excessive particle loading. Membranes under the strongest fracture collapsed into opaque strands. Adjacent tissue carried current around the damaged area. New growth appeared farther down-current, creating a dark channel beneath the highest flux.

Those changes supported differential survival and adaptive response. They did not support deliberation.

Detached fragments lodged in warmer seams and formed linked films where minerals supplied chemical energy. Separate basins changed at different rates. No signal crossed the global system. Northern terraces reacted in pulses. Equatorial veils shifted over days. A southern shelf remained metabolically active without visible ion glow.

Those patterns supported propagation of living structure. They did not support a single organism, much less a mind.

Nadi set four evidence labels beside the map: metabolism, repair, propagation, adaptive ionic response. She added three exclusions: no demonstrated language, no demonstrated cognition, no demonstrated preference.

The lab reader offered to classify the exclusions as negative findings. She changed each to not tested or not established. Instruments designed to measure spectral turnover could not certify the absence of experience. That limit did not authorize invention in the other direction.

Kite's memory question included a proposed experiment. Its biology committee wanted Mote to repeat the four-percent ion perturbation at the repaired terrace, then test whether the membrane reacted faster. A quicker contraction on second exposure might indicate retained information, they wrote.

Nadi built the experiment in simulation before touching the instrument queue. The repaired film differed physically from the original tissue. Sulfur-binding proteins remained elevated along closure beads. Calcium channels had narrower resting widths. A repeated perturbation would begin from another material state. Faster contraction could follow chemistry established by the first damage, with no need for storage beyond structure.

She listed what a stronger memory claim would require: controlled regions with matched repair history, several perturbation intervals, a response that generalized beyond the damaged site, and a way to distinguish retained chemical state from information used across contexts. Mote could not create those conditions through sample-light. Its instruments observed Thale from orbit and altered ion supply only inside a small remote field with strict energy limits.

Running the proposed test carried harm as well. The first trial had damaged membrane within measured repair capacity. Repeating it during a different seasonal phase might push the tissue beyond that range. An intelligence demonstration designed to earn protection would begin by injuring the life in question for legal convenience.

Nadi declined the experiment in her response notes.

The refusal did not prove cognition. It established only that Kite's suggested method could not distinguish its preferred explanation and exceeded Mote's present intervention limit. Future settlers might design better observations without touching the active bands. For the current decision, the mind question remained open.

Bracken had attached three clipped ion sequences that appeared to repeat. Its council called them possible symbols. Nadi aligned the clips against Corva's field variation. Each recurrence followed the same magnetospheric pulse within measurement error. When she removed the external rhythm, no independent sequence remained. The pattern fit driven chemistry.

Yet the negative result stayed narrow. The pellicle might carry organization outside Mote's spectral range. Separate basins might interact over years rather than days. No current evidence supported either idea. Nadi included those possibilities under unmeasured, not probable.

Vela's intentional-avoidance question relied on a video rendering of activity moving around a damaged channel. The animation gave bright regions a leading edge and dark regions a wake. Played at human speed, it looked purposeful. At source scale, the change took thirty-one Thale days and emerged through unequal cell survival across kilometers.

She replaced the animation with four fixed observations. Each image showed concentrations, temperature, and particle loading. The apparent motion broke into local growth and loss. Viewers could still use the word avoidance in an ecological sense, but Nadi would not let a cinematic rendering support a mental claim.

Her pilot entered carrying a thermal pouch and hooked one foot beneath the bench rail. “The legal bundle asks for intentional avoidance. The band moved away from high loading.”

“Growth survived better outside the high-loading region.”

“That sounds like avoidance.”

“It sounds like avoidance because the verb gives the pattern an actor.” Nadi enlarged the time series. “Cells near the fracture collapsed. Cells farther away divided. The map changed. I measured no choice between alternatives.”

“Does the distinction matter if Crown destroys both?”

“It matters to the claim. It may not matter to the damage.”

She overlaid Tomas Ren's exposure envelope from the oldest attached engineering packet. The Crown corridor crossed two high-confidence active basins and the southern shelf whose metabolism lacked strong ion glow. Charged-particle loading exceeded every perturbation Mote had observed. Thermal coupling carried wider uncertainty. No honest model produced an exact death fraction.

Nadi drew three zones. The first contained activity observed repeatedly across the seasonal cycle. The second contained intermittent activity tied to shadow and vent pulses. The third contained compatible habitat with insufficient sample-light coverage. She refused the display's suggested colors because red, amber, and green implied decision status. Patterns, stipple, and labeled confidence kept uncertainty visible.

“Where would you put the beam?” the pilot asked.

“Outside all three if the engineering allows it.”

“And if it does not?”

“I provide exposure and uncertainty. The councils own the trade.”

“That answer will sound like hiding.”

“It is division of authority.”

Nadi selected a narrow equatorial band where the latest scan showed reduced activity after particle surges. A careless planner might call it safe. She added the historical frames. The band had carried bright membrane thirty days earlier. Its present darkness could indicate damage rather than absence. Routing more energy through it might prevent recovery.

A second low-signal region over the southern shelf posed the opposite error. Weak ion glow suggested little activity, yet vent chemistry showed ongoing carbon transfer. Ion brightness could guide avoidance but could not map all life.

The map needed to display both facts without becoming unusable. Nadi created an active-band layer from observed spectral change, then a broader biological uncertainty layer from heat, minerals, and prior propagation. She attached source times to every sweep. No layer claimed current location after the last photon received.

The result consumed six times Mote's transmission allowance.

She reduced spatial resolution. Thin active lines disappeared. She restored them and removed decorative interpolation instead. Packet size remained high. Compression could merge adjacent confidence classes, making a shifting boundary look fixed.

The pilot watched the timer. “Nine minutes to alignment.”

“Send the map first.”

“Then the testimony waits.”

“The map changes what the beam can avoid. My argument changes how people describe it.”

“You think they can use the data without the argument?”

“They already know how to ask for intelligence. They need evidence that does not answer that question.”

Mote's transmitter loaded the first map segment.

While the antenna found the relay, Nadi recorded her reply.

She kept the council questions in their original order. Each answer named what the data supported, what remained untested, and what consequence followed from uncertainty.

To Vela she said, “No experiment in this record demonstrates intentional avoidance. The movement of activity follows differential survival, chemistry, temperature, and charge. Calling that intentional would exceed the observations.”

To Bracken she said, “The ionic changes carry effects between neighboring membranes. I have not established symbols, syntax, reference, or a sender-receiver relation. Signal in the physical sense does not establish language.”

For Kite she reviewed the damaged terrace. Later charge traveled through structures formed during repair, so past damage altered future response. That fact supplied material history. It did not demonstrate memory as a cognitive capacity. She would not turn retained structure into recollection merely because humans used one word for both.

Lumen received ranges instead of a fraction. Crown loading crossed measured active regions beyond Mote's tested adaptive envelope. Extensive mortality and disruption remained likely under the central exposure case. Global sterilization could not be certified because deep vent systems and weakly visible shelves had not been measured well enough. Survival of some regions would not make destruction of others negligible.

The Nacre question required a different boundary.

Nadi read it aloud before answering. “What standing belongs to metabolism without preference?”

The pilot secured the thermal pouch as Mote's attitude changed. “Standing sounds like law.”

“It is.”

“You are not counsel.”

“No.”

She began again.

“Science cannot assign legal standing. It can identify a living system inside a proposed harm. The pellicle cannot vote. It has no demonstrated council, language, preference, or capacity to receive our evidence. Those absences prevent us from counting consent. They do not create consent.”

Nadi stopped the recording. The last line approached rhetoric, but its logic matched the evidence distinction. An incapable witness did not produce a yes by failing to answer. Yet the pellicle also held no seat in the Concord. Human councils would decide whether preserving it justified risk.

She continued.

“Uncertainty about intelligence does not measure uncertainty about life. Metabolism, repair, propagation, and adaptive response meet the operational threshold for native life. Intelligence remains unestablished. A decision to expose the system should use the life finding and the damage range. It should not treat the absent mind finding as permission.”

Before accepting that wording, Nadi tested its opposite. If later evidence demonstrated cognition, councils would face additional obligations they had not yet defined. If later evidence supported only microbial-scale chemistry, the present life finding would remain unchanged. Crown's physical exposure did not wait for the taxonomy to become politically convenient.

She added a table with three columns: established, unresolved, and decision outside scientific authority. Native metabolism occupied the first. Intelligence occupied the second. Whether human survival justified a particular exposure occupied the third. The format prevented Vela from moving the unanswered mind question into the decision column as a default approval.

A footnote addressed representation. No member of the pellicle had received the evidence bundle or emitted testimony. Its inability to join causal quorum did not make it a hidden tenth voter. Human councils remained responsible for destination protection under their own law and their limited survival authority. They could decide against preservation after stating the damage. They could not report the pellicle's silence as agreement.

The pilot looked at the waveform. “They asked whether it justifies material risk.”

“That conclusion exceeds my discipline.”

“You can say what destroying it means.”

“I can say what physical change Crown causes.”

“Then say it plainly.”

Nadi reopened Lumen's answer. She removed two passive constructions and named the mechanism. Crown would drive charged particles through active basins, heat brine above observed repair conditions, and disrupt membranes across regions the map marked alive. Some deeper systems might persist. The intervention would still destroy native living structure on a scale outside any measured seasonal loss.

She added a limit to Vela's threshold.

“Requiring intelligence before destination protection would classify non-intelligent life as available matter by default. The data does not establish that policy. It only shows what the policy would expose.”

Mote had left human law behind with the convoy. Nadi could not know which amendments had passed during the packet's travel or whether Nacre still recognized Vela's quorum. She dated her answer against each question received and avoided present-tense claims about fleet authority.

The recording ended at seven minutes thirty-one seconds, too large to accompany the full map.

“Compress speech,” the pilot said.

“Not semantically.”

“Audio quality.”

They reduced frequency range, removed the image, and kept a text checksum. The packet shrank. Nadi cut repeated question headers. She would not cut source clocks or exclusions.

A gimbal warning sounded.

The high-gain antenna had reached its stable angle late. Relay acquisition flickered across three values, none strong enough for the full transmission rate. The pilot took manual control and narrowed Mote's attitude correction. Lab tools lifted against their restraints as the cutter rotated.

“First window gives us eight minutes now,” the pilot said.

“Map segment one requires six.”

“Receipt confirmation takes part of that.”

“We do not need confirmation inside the same window.”

“We need to know the relay caught it before sending segment two.”

“The second window may not exist.”

Nadi looked at the packet stack. Map first. Testimony second. Raw seasonal frames third. If only the first left, fleet engineers would receive active zones without the reasoning that restrained their interpretation. If only testimony left, they could debate life while continuing to use an old map.

She built a compact lead packet containing the map legend, four life findings, three exclusions, and a checksum for the full data. It did not carry the council answers. It taught any recipient how not to misread the geometry.

“Send lead and map one,” she said.

The transmitter opened.

Carrier power dimmed half the lab.

Mote's source clock stamped the lead packet. Light left toward the relay bead. The display showed emission, not delivery. Nadi watched the outgoing counter climb while the active ion bands remained beneath her hands as delayed measurements.

At four minutes, antenna temperature crossed its preferred limit. The pilot reduced rate. Map segment one continued.

A calibration block threatened to miss the window. Nadi could remove it and gain forty seconds. Without the block, Nacre might compare relative activity but could not reproduce brightness thresholds across instruments. A pretty map would arrive faster and carry less evidence.

“Keep calibration,” she said.

The gimbal angle drifted toward cutoff.

Segment one completed with twenty-three seconds remaining. The transmitter began the compact testimony automatically. Only its opening could fit.

Nadi stopped it.

The pilot turned from manual control. “Why?”

“A partial argument will invite completion.”

“By whom?”

“Whoever needs a sentence I did not finish.”

She used the final seconds to send the testimony checksum and length. The relay would know an object existed without pretending to hold its content.

Carrier lock broke.

No receipt could return before the light path allowed. Mote's system projected the earliest relay acknowledgment and labeled it calculated. Nadi began preparing for the second window without assuming the first packet survived.

Five hours later, the acknowledgment arrived at the predicted edge plus nine minutes. The relay had received the lead packet and map segment one intact. Its response carried a photonic sample and the exact received length.

Mote's next alignment opened at lower power. Nadi sent the remaining ion-band map segments first, then the full testimony, then as many raw seasonal frames as the antenna temperature allowed. Each object carried its own checksum. Missing frames remained listed rather than interpolated.

The final map packet entered the beam near the end of the window.

Nadi watched its source marker recede down the queue. Convoy councils would receive the pieces at different times. Vela might see them before Nacre. A trailing habitat could receive the testimony after the map. No green status could make their knowledge simultaneous.

On the lab display, Thale's latest observed activity curved beneath the ice in broken bands. High-confidence regions, intermittent regions, and biological uncertainty remained distinct. The map showed life changing under particle seasons. It showed no thoughts.

At 19:08 Mote source, the transmitter completed the ion-band map.

Chapter 21 — No Quorum

The Crown contribution order reached Osei while Nacre’s port radiator held three degrees above its safe curve.

She stood in the thermal gallery with one hand inside an inspection sleeve, feeling the tremor of coolant through the glove. A row of amber traces climbed across the wall. The attitude jets had been firing in short corrections since Nacre suspended recognition of forecast ballots, and each correction sent heat into systems already carrying the garden ring’s daytime load.

Chief Varo called from the upper rail. “Vela priority packet. Command authority.”

“Receipt time?” Osei asked.

“08:14:22 local. Vela source forty-nine minutes earlier.”

“Keep both clocks visible.”

The packet opened above the coolant manifold. Director Yselle Quon appeared at the Crown operations desk, recorded beneath Vela’s white ceiling lights. Her gray uniform carried no emergency braid. She had chosen ordinary civil authority for an extraordinary demand.

“Captain Tamm,” the recording said. “The fleet’s commitment calculation now records six concurrences, two probable concurrences above operational confidence, and one objection. The Crown majority therefore remains valid under Annex Twelve. Nacre will deliver its scheduled beam-reference correction and hold its assigned attitude at 11:40 Vela source. Failure to contribute will widen the convoy beyond the protected alignment corridor. Confirm execution on receipt.”

A document followed the image. Meridian’s condensed board showed nine green marks. Juniper’s observed rejection occupied a footnote under its earlier forecast concurrence. Nacre’s suspension appeared as a predicted temporary objection, confidence 0.941. Palisade’s unanswered material request carried a green preparatory assent. Ardent’s file still used the corrected sequence that had excluded Pera’s reply.

Osei withdrew her hand from the inspection sleeve. Coolant shone on the glove where a seal had begun to sweat.

“Send the raw packet to Kest,” she said. “No master-time rendering.”

Varo hesitated beside the rail. “Confirmation?”

“Transmit receipt only. State that command review has begun.”

Vela would see those words after the next light interval. The order’s clock kept advancing without waiting for Osei’s judgment. That pressure had always served as Meridian’s strongest argument: delay itself spent fuel, thermal margin, food, and lives. Osei had repeated the argument often enough to know where it remained true.

She also knew a prediction could not receive an order.

The port radiator trace rose another tenth of a degree. Osei reseated the inspection sleeve and ordered a four-minute reduction in garden lighting. Leaves would tolerate that loss. The algae racks would recover it during the next dark cycle. A captain survived emergencies by choosing damage that could still be undone.

Quon’s order asked her to use reversible discipline in service of an irreversible lie.

Anja arrived at the thermal gallery with Sumi Dax and three unsynchronized views of the packet. Tomas came behind them carrying a flexible drive sheet rolled against his chest. None had slept enough. Sumi’s close-cut hair stood in damp points, while Tomas had buttoned his shirt through the wrong loop at the collar.

Osei gave them six minutes before the council connection opened.

“Tell me what Vela actually has,” she said.

Anja set the local accounts side by side. “One observed assent from Vela. Observed assent from Kite, made before the complete Mote supplement reached it. Juniper’s observed vote says no. Nacre has issued no Crown assent. Bracken, Orison, Lumen, Palisade, and Ardent have not returned observed votes on the complete evidence.”

“Vela claims six concurrences.”

“Five rely on forecast testimony or votes taken against earlier evidence. Their display counts the latest model prediction as though it repaired the missing receipt.”

Sumi enlarged the carrier proofs. Blank intervals appeared where the condensed board had shown green. Each gap carried a scheduled receipt time, a confidence value, or a classification substitution. No signature followed common evidence through a complete receipt path.

Osei pointed to Kite. “Could its earlier vote remain valid?”

“Only if Kite’s council affirms it after receiving the supplement,” Anja said. “Their earlier answer cannot include evidence outside their past light cone.”

“Could they lawfully decide that the evidence changes nothing?”

“Yes. They have to decide it.”

That answer mattered. Anja did not want a preferred result disguised as procedure. She wanted time for answers that might still defeat her case.

Tomas unrolled his drive sheet over a service table. “Nacre’s scheduled contribution begins with reference attitude, not full beamer load. If we make the attitude change, we improve Vela’s firing geometry. Reversing later would cost twenty-one hours of maneuver reserve and leave us outside my distributed solution’s capture branch.”

“How much time before refusal becomes physical?” Osei asked.

“Vela’s command wants the shift in two hours thirty-seven minutes by our clock. We need ninety minutes to move into the alternative attitude without exceeding the radiator limit.”

“So forty-seven minutes for government.”

“Less if the port loop keeps rising.”

Osei studied the ship schematic. Under Crown alignment, Nacre would cant its drive axis toward Vela’s reference beam and tighten formation. Tomas’s route turned four degrees away, preserving separation for the later distributed brake. That attitude shift would widen the convoy by another fraction of a light-second each hour. Small at first, then legally and physically expensive.

“What does the alternative require from us today?” she asked.

“Attitude, tracking capacity, and commitment of the number-three maneuver reserve. We also transmit a lawful material request to Palisade. No shield moves yet. No brake begins. But after the shift, returning to Crown costs enough propellant to threaten arrival.”

Anja rested both hands on the service table. “Then our action also crosses Annex Twelve’s line.”

“It does,” Osei said.

Sumi looked between them. “You’re saying Nacre cannot use emergency authority to choose Tomas’s plan.”

“I can protect immediate survival. I cannot bind eighteen thousand four hundred people to nineteen years of staggered arrival because I dislike Vela’s shortcut.”

“The Crown order gives us forty-seven minutes,” Tomas said.

“No. Light and fuel give us forty-seven minutes. Quon merely arranged her demand to occupy them.”

Osei called the council without waiting for ceremonial quorum. Seven representatives answered from compartments across Nacre. Two joined from the garden ring, still wearing pollination veils. The eighth seat belonged to a pressure crew leader trapped behind an inspection closure; she sent observed authority to her deputy with a local receipt chain.

Osei put Vela’s order on the shared display. Then she removed Meridian’s green marks.

Nine local columns replaced them. Most ended in empty space.

The council had heard the legal case four days earlier. Today it wanted to know who would go cold.

Representative Hale from the third ring spoke first. “If we leave Crown geometry, can Vela brake without us?”

Tomas answered from the thermal gallery. “Not on the published tolerance. They can reassign load to Kite and Orison, but mirror stress rises. Their safe branch narrows.”

“Then our refusal endangers people on ships whose councils may support Crown.”

“Yes.”

Garden representative Ilya Chen lifted her pollination veil. Yellow grains marked the bridge of her nose. “Under your alternative, when do our children reach Ione?”

“Different cohorts arrive across nineteen years. Nacre falls in the middle group if Palisade releases the modeled shield reserve.”

“And if Palisade refuses?”

“We miss the useful distributed corridor. We could still attempt an independent brake, but I cannot certify settlement arrival.”

The council screen held that sentence without decoration. Osei watched each representative read the same risk through a different room: a clinic alcove, a machine bay, a classroom emptied for ship night. Government aboard a habitat never took place apart from what it could damage.

Deputy Resh from the pressure crew said, “Vela has a plan that gets us there. It kills life we have never seen. Tomas has a plan that may not get us there and spends shielding we use every hour.”

Nadi’s ion-band map had reached Nacre two days earlier. Osei could show its shifting lines, the pellicle’s metabolism under Thale’s ice, and the repair after particle shadow. None of those images answered how much risk a captain could impose on awake children. Native life created a constraint, not a command diagram.

Anja requested the floor. “This council has two questions, not one. Vela asks whether its order carries lawful fleet consent. It does not. Tomas asks whether Nacre will make a material commitment to an alternative. That answer belongs here.”

Hale frowned. “If we separate them, we can refuse Vela and still refuse Tomas.”

“Yes,” Anja said.

“What happens then?”

“Nacre drifts out of both protected plans.”

A ventilation alarm flashed on Osei’s lower display. Port loop margin had fallen to two point six degrees. Chief Varo requested permission to shut a fabrication line. Osei approved it with a thumbprint while the council watched.

Ilya Chen folded her veil into a narrow strip. “I want the record to show that delay has a cost on this ship. Director Quon did not invent that.”

“It will show,” Osei said.

“I also want it to show that she spent our delay before our answer arrived.”

Anja entered the statement under Nacre’s source clock.

Discussion moved through drive risk, ration branches, shield exposure, and the expected loss of garden light. Tomas refused every invitation to make his model kinder. One ring might face seasonal calorie restriction. Exterior repairs would shift to narrower radiation windows. Families assigned to different arrival cohorts could spend years uncertain whether later ships would follow.

The clinic representative asked what happened if Vela cut command traffic. Sumi separated the channels on her board. Collision solutions and ephemeris exchange traveled under standing safety duties. Crown authorization occupied a distinct legal layer. Vela could make access difficult, but Nacre’s relays would still carry raw packets from every habitat willing to transmit.

“Can Meridian exclude us from master time?” Hale asked.

“It can stop accepting our corrections into its preferred display,” Sumi said. “Our source clock continues. So do the receipt proofs at every relay that sees us.”

Osei let the answer settle. Rejection by a display would complicate repair schedules, shared warnings, and transfer windows. It would not erase the ship, though people accustomed to one board might behave as if Nacre had become unreliable. Institutional isolation could arrive long before physical isolation.

She asked each department to name one consequence that could not wait for the later fleet vote. The clinic needed revised medicine-transfer dates. Exterior maintenance needed independent radiation forecasts. Food systems needed the exact garden-light reduction before the next germination cycle. Drive control needed authority to reject any automatic Crown correction Meridian might inject after the council adjourned.

Those requests turned dissent into a list of shifts to be covered. Osei assigned owners while the clock continued. Nobody could spend the remaining minutes admiring principle. If Nacre intended to keep coordination without accepting consent-fiction, each ordinary service had to prove the distinction possible.

At the thirty-minute mark, Osei interrupted a dispute about Vela’s authority.

“We have no quorum for Crown,” she said. “Do not turn that absence into permission for us. Decide Nacre’s action.”

Hale proposed holding present attitude until Palisade answered. Tomas showed the curve: waiting six hours pushed Nacre beyond the low-propellant branch. Resh proposed a partial Crown correction that preserved both options. The drive sheet rendered it in red. A compromise attitude exposed the port radiator during the longest burn and preserved neither capture geometry.

Procedure stripped away the comfortable choices first.

With thirteen minutes remaining, Ilya moved that Nacre reject Vela’s legal claim, maintain communication and collision coordination, commit its maneuver reserve to the distributed-brake geometry, and condition any later mass transfer on observed council authority from each participating habitat.

The deputy added an amendment requiring public resource forecasts for every ring. Hale added a sunset review after Palisade’s observed answer. Nobody proposed calling the attitude change reversible.

Osei asked Anja to read the motion back.

Anja did so once, without emphasis. “Nacre denies that forecast testimony establishes causal quorum. Nacre remains within physical coordination protocols. Nacre authorizes the local attitude and reserve commitment required to preserve the distributed-brake option. Any sacrifice of shield mass or settlement allocation requires observed testimony through a complete receipt path.”

Eight council lights opened for voting.

The pressure deputy answered first. Garden followed. The clinic representative waited through nine seconds of silence before signing assent. Hale voted against, stating that Nacre should not reduce Crown’s safety margin before Palisade committed. Six others approved.

No forecast filled the interval before Hale spoke.

Anja verified the signatures and their local receipts. “Seven in favor, one opposed. Nacre authority observed.”

Osei felt no release. The vote made the risk theirs; it did not reduce it.

Vela’s execution time arrived while the drive crew still loaded Tomas’s attitude program.

The command board asked for Nacre’s Crown reference correction. Chief Varo stood beside Osei at the gallery rail. Below them, technicians secured tools against the coming change in thrust vector. The radiator traces had stabilized after fabrication shutdown, leaving only one point nine degrees of margin.

“Transmit our council record,” Osei said.

“Vela will receive it after their deadline.”

“The deadline cannot change the source time.”

Varo attached the raw council packet, Anja’s legal finding, and Tomas’s material request. He kept all receipt paths intact. The tightbeam status turned from queued to emitted.

Forty-nine minutes would pass before Vela could know Nacre had refused. Another interval would pass before any answer came back. During that span, command displays on both ships would tell different truths about the same fleet.

Osei opened a live recording channel for Quon.

“Director, Nacre has received your order. Your forecast majority does not establish consent under the Concord of Frames. The complete evidence has not reached every entitled habitat and observed replies have not returned. There is no quorum. Nacre will continue navigation exchange, collision protection, thermal coordination, and receipt service. We will not provide the Crown reference correction.”

She paused while the source clock advanced in the corner.

“Nacre has authorized an alternative material path. We are shifting attitude to preserve distributed braking and have asked Palisade for an observed decision on shield reserve. You may oppose that plan when our packet reaches you. You may not record an answer before then.”

Osei signed with ship authority and council attachment. Varo transmitted the recording.

A Crown alert immediately appeared. Meridian had detected divergence from the planned orientation and projected a collision-risk cascade. The warning carried no intention or anger. Its numbers came from geometry: Vela’s mirror field, Kite’s corrective capacity, Nacre’s widening track. Chief Varo routed the alert to drive control.

“Tomas says ready,” he reported.

Osei left the thermal gallery for the nonrotating hub. Handholds led past communications, the sequence office, and a hatch stenciled with the names of six old pressure failures. Workers had been ordered into restraint stations. Through the audit well’s window she saw Anja strapped at her board, keeping Nacre’s source clock beside Vela’s demand.

At the central attitude station, Tomas floated above a ring of manual controls. Sweat darkened his shirt despite the hub’s cool air.

“Program remains inside thermal limit,” he said. “Once I execute, Crown reacquisition consumes the reserve we just committed.”

“Any new information?”

“Palisade acknowledged carrier receipt of our earlier engineering model. No council answer yet.”

“Then we act on Nacre’s vote, not Palisade’s prediction.”

Tomas placed the execution key into Osei’s hand.

She had once told Anja that a captain must never spend people on symbolism. Refusing a false record could become symbolic if the ship merely drifted into danger. Today’s choice carried propellant, heat, food, and a narrowing future. Its material cost made the dissent operational, though cost alone could not make it wise.

Osei inserted the key.

The hub gave no cinematic lurch. Nacre’s attitude jets began with a low pulse transmitted through the hull, followed six seconds later by a second pulse at a different frequency. Loose straps lifted toward the new acceleration vector. On the exterior display, Vela’s reference line slid away from Nacre’s drive axis by hundredths of a degree.

“Port loop rising,” Varo said over the channel.

“Within curve,” Tomas answered.

“Crown corridor boundary in ninety seconds.”

Osei watched the local track rather than Meridian’s master rendering. Nacre’s trajectory bent with each measured pulse. The distance between its projected line and Vela’s alignment widened from kilometers to tens of kilometers at Corva intercept, then hundreds. Those numbers would continue growing while Nacre’s refusal crossed the dark behind them.

At the sixty-second mark, a new command packet entered the queue from Vela. Its source time preceded Osei’s refusal because her message had not yet arrived there. The recording showed Quon repeating the execution order and warning that unauthorized divergence could trigger command removal.

Varo looked toward Osei. “Do you want it on the council channel?”

“Not until the full packet finishes. Preserve its source order.”

Tomas called the final jet sequence. Radiator margin dropped to eight tenths of a degree, held, then began recovering. A red line crossed the attitude display.

“Nacre has left Crown capture,” he said. “Distributed branch remains open.”

No one cheered. Drive control started calculating correction budgets. Thermal crews restored half the fabrication load. The council clerk logged Hale’s request for immediate review. Every system treated the choice as work rather than victory.

Outside, the convoy still looked almost unchanged. Vela’s light held steady forward of them. Juniper shone aft and starboard, its visible position hours older than its present motion. Ardent could not be picked out without magnification. The ships had always occupied different times; now their formation admitted the disagreement in distance.

Osei touched the attitude display where Nacre’s line had separated from the Crown corridor. The maneuver record opened beneath her finger with local pulse times, fuel draw, thermal cost, and council authority.

“Log the attitude shift as observed execution,” she said.

Varo signed the entry. Nacre’s clock placed it after the vote and before any answer from Vela.

By the time Director Quon could see what Osei had done, the widening space would already belong to the convoy’s physical history.

Interlude III — The Ionic Season

Corva’s magnetic shadow crossed the ice above the active band.

Charged particles fell away from the surface in less than a minute. Beneath eleven kilometers of ice, the upper brine channels lost their blue ion glow. Sodium gradients flattened along the pellicle’s exposed membranes. Catalytic pores narrowed as available charge declined, reducing the transfer of carbon compounds from the water into layered tissue.

The change reached lower membranes through chemistry. Calcium gates closed. Long protein filaments altered their folds and blocked channels that had carried stronger current. Dissolved sulfur accumulated in pockets between layers. Where the brine remained warm, microbial cells continued splitting at a slower rate. Other cells stopped division while their outer walls thickened.

No single boundary contained the pellicle. Membrane sheets covered mineral terraces, crossed fissures, and broke into suspended veils above warmer vents. Some layers measured less than a finger’s width. Older formations extended for kilometers through linked brine chambers. Water moved ions between separated surfaces whenever pressure opened a path.

During the shadow, contraction began at the outer edge of each charged zone. Transparent tissue darkened to rust-red. Flexible ridges drew close to the ice, reducing the surface exposed to cold brine. The contraction spread at rates determined by salinity, temperature, and the local concentration of charged proteins. It did not spread as one signal. Thousands of chemical thresholds produced the visible narrowing.

A crack opened three hundred meters above the largest terrace.

Pressure drove a blade of colder water down through the channel. Ice crystals struck the upper sheets and tore parallel cuts across them. Fragments drifted from the terrace, each carrying intact membranes, stored sulfur, and clusters of dividing cells. Most entered water too cold for continued metabolism. Their gradients dissipated. Several lodged in a warmer seam along the channel wall.

Exposed edges sealed through ordinary reactions. Calcium precipitated against ruptured proteins. Lipid layers rolled inward where water contacted the open membranes. Cells beside the cut increased production of structural fibers once the local sulfur concentration rose. The first closures formed uneven beads along each tear.

Further repair depended on heat from below. Vent water reached the terrace in pulses, lifting its temperature by fractions of a degree. Each warm interval accelerated protein assembly. The sealed beads spread sideways until they joined. New translucent film covered the cuts, thinner than the tissue around it and crossed by irregular ion channels.

Corva moved between Thale and the distant star for nineteen hours. The particle current stayed weak throughout the passage. Upper layers consumed stored compounds. Lower sheets continued receiving reduced chemical energy from mineral vents. Between those regions, transport pores reversed their usual gradient and carried sulfur upward.

The reversal changed the active band’s shape. A broad bright field contracted into narrow lines above the vent system. Separate points appeared where warm water crossed mineral shelves. Nadi Arko’s instruments on Mote recorded such shifts as spectral variation. Under the ice, they occurred as concentrations, temperatures, folds, fractures, and rates of exchange.

When Thale cleared Corva’s shadow, charged particles returned first at high latitudes. Auroral current entered the ice shell and traveled along salt-rich fractures. The upper brine brightened in branching paths. Membranes nearest those paths reopened calcium gates. Stored carbon compounds moved outward. Thin ridges lifted from the ice as gas collected beneath their folded surfaces.

Illumination crossed the terrace over seven hours. It followed conductivity rather than distance. One narrow seam brightened far ahead of the surrounding tissue because iron salts lined the crack above it. A broad formation nearer the magnetic pole remained dark under purer ice.

The torn sheet received current along its new film. Some channels conducted too freely and exhausted their gradients. Those cells broke apart. Neighboring regions thickened around the gaps. Repeated charge cycles narrowed the difference between repaired tissue and older layers, though the new growth retained a distinct ratio of sulfur-binding proteins.

Across the next thirty-one Thale days, Corva’s field tilted relative to the moon’s orbit. Particle loading moved toward the equatorial brine basins. The pellicle’s visible band moved with the loading through differential growth. Tissue in declining regions darkened and fragmented. Cells in stronger gradients divided more often. Suspended fragments settled into warm seams and formed small linked films wherever mineral supply supported them.

No pattern crossed every basin. Northern terraces carried fast pulses through iron-rich water. Equatorial veils changed over several days. A deep southern shelf remained chemically active without detectable ion glow because its energy came from vent minerals. The global map combined processes separated by ice, pressure, and discontinuous water.

At the equatorial basin, a particle surge exceeded the ordinary range. The upper ice released trapped ions into the brine. Exposed ridges brightened white along their edges. Protein gates stayed open beyond their stable interval. Carbon transfer increased, followed by rapid depletion of local compounds.

Membranes nearest the strongest fracture collapsed into opaque strands. Lipids separated from structural fibers. Currents diverted around those strands through adjacent tissue. The active region divided into two arcs with a dark strip between them.

Chemical products from the collapsed cells spread into both arcs. Some inhibited division. Others increased the closure rate of calcium gates. After three surges, new growth appeared farther from the fracture, leaving a wider dark channel beneath the highest particle flux. The position of maximum activity shifted down-current from the incoming charge.

Mote’s spectrometer sampled the basin during each orbital pass. A damaged antenna forced the instrument record into narrow packets. The first packet carried the bright fracture. The second carried the divided arcs. Later packets showed the growing dark channel and the active tissue displaced toward lower loading.

Another Corva shadow crossed twelve days later. The two arcs contracted at different rates. One lost visible current within minutes. The other retained a low red band for four hours because vent water supplied sulfur beneath it. Both expanded after charged particles came back, but neither regained the earlier shape.

The seasonal cycle continued through repeated exposure and shadow. Each pass altered concentrations established by the previous one. Mineral terraces gained new films. Old sheets separated under ice motion. Fragments entered isolated seams. Layered tissue thickened where heat, carbon, salt, and charge remained within working ranges.

Near the end of the ionic season, stress in the upper shell opened a second cold-water channel above the repaired terrace. The descending flow struck farther east. Its crystals scored the membrane surface without cutting through the thickest layers. Thin new film from the first rupture tore again.

Closure began along the old sulfur-rich beads. Their protein ratios accelerated precipitation at the torn edges. Fresh film spread across the smaller cuts during two vent pulses. In surrounding tissue, charge moved around the damaged region along paths formed after the earlier rupture.

The active band narrowed while the water stayed cold. Rust-red lines gathered above warm cracks. Transparent margins folded inward. Detached pieces descended through the channel, and three lodged below the terrace where weak current reached a mineral shelf.

Corva’s shadow passed off the ice.

Particle flux returned along the salt fractures. The repaired terrace brightened first at its old edges, then across the new film. Current divided around opaque strands, crossed the sulfur-rich closure beads, and entered the lower shelf through the channel that had carried the fragments.

From orbit, the active ion band appeared as a broken curve under the ice. Its brightest region no longer followed the line recorded at the start of the cycle. A dark channel marked excessive loading. Two new points showed activity below the damaged terrace. The curve widened during charge and contracted under shadow with measurable differences after each passage.

Mote’s sample-light instrument completed one full comparison. It assigned no name to the process. The output contained wavelengths, timing, temperature estimates, and the geometry of change. Packet limits divided the record into fragments, each stamped by the cutter’s source clock.

On the next clear pass, the instrument swept the equatorial basin from west to east. Repaired membrane caught the returning current. The displaced arcs brightened. Lower fragments added three faint points beneath them.

The final scan resolved those changes into the ion-band map.

Part IV — The Right to Arrive

Chapter 22 — The Parallax Archive

Sumi cut master time from the audit well at 02:06.

Every green sequence bar vanished. Nine local clocks opened across the curved display, each advancing at its own measured rate. Vela led by its source convention. Ardent lagged under the correction Anja had challenged. Juniper’s clock carried a receipt offset from the long beam path. Mote appeared apart from the habitats, close to Ione and seventeen light-hours beyond any useful pretense of a common present.

The room looked broken without Meridian’s alignment marks.

“Keep it ugly,” Anja said.

“I was about to improve it.”

“That instinct caused enough trouble.”

Sumi’s mouth tightened around a reply, then released it. Thirty-one hours had passed since Nacre left Crown capture. Neither had slept for more than ninety minutes together. Around them, the ordinary sequence staff worked from isolated consoles because the archive build occupied the main well. Cooling fans pushed warm air through the floor grilles. Every processor Nacre could spare had been assigned to verifying receipt proofs rather than rendering one timeline.

At the center of the display, Sumi placed the photonic checksum from Anja’s first frozen packet. Its interference grain had no readable meaning to most residents, but three independent hull nodes had preserved it. She linked the checksum to Mote’s original fragments, Levan’s premature concurrence, the predictive-continuity transformation, and each later use of that transformed event.

No line claimed that one ship’s clock governed another.

“Root holds,” Sumi said. “If anyone changes the Vela envelope, the first branch fails.”

“What if they change a local account?”

“The account stays under its host key. A correction can be appended, never substituted. Every mirror retains the earlier hash.”

Anja examined the branch labels. Sumi had built the archive as a set of witnessed paths: source event, carrier proof, local receipt, later classification, and any transformation applied for operational display. A reader could follow one packet without accepting a universal order for unrelated events.

The design sounded simple because forty-three years of synchronization machinery had hidden how much labor simplicity required.

A warning opened over Vela’s column.

NORMALIZATION FAILURE: incompatible evidentiary classifications.

Recommend exclusion of derogatory local records.

Sumi tapped the message aside. “That gives us the name.”

Three days earlier, Vela counsel had dismissed Nacre’s preserved files as a parallax testament, unreliable because each account retained frame-specific timing. The insult now appeared in the archive header with both words capitalized.

Anja read it once. “Parallax Archive.”

“Not testament?”

“A testament can be treated as one witness. This lets each witness keep the part only they observed.”

Sumi changed the header.

Publication required more than naming. Nacre could host the full structure, but Vela controlled the primary convoy index. If Quon refused the link, each habitat would need enough of the archive to validate every record relevant to its cohort. That meant sending repeated fragments across long paths while Crown traffic already contested beam time.

Sumi opened a capacity map. “We have nine minutes of clean outbound window before drive control takes the array. I can send root data and local readers to Juniper, Kite, and Bracken. Ardent needs a relay through Lumen at this attitude.”

“Send no rendered conclusion.”

“They’ll need instructions.”

“Instructions for verification, not judgment.”

Anja wrote the first line herself: Keep your local clock visible.

Before public release, Sumi insisted on a hostile test using a record with no political value. She selected a coolant-valve request sent from Lumen nine days earlier. Nacre had received the request, begun fabrication, and later learned that Lumen canceled it before Nacre’s work started by Lumen’s clock. Master time displayed the cancellation first, making Nacre’s completed fabrication look like operator error.

The archive kept both local sequences. Lumen had canceled before Nacre fabricated. Nacre had fabricated before the cancellation could arrive. Neither statement contradicted the other because the actions lacked a causal connection until the message crossed between ships.

“Now ask it who wasted the valve,” Sumi said.

Anja entered the query. The reader returned no universal answer. It showed the source request, Nacre’s receipt, the fabrication order, Lumen’s cancellation, and its later receipt on Nacre. Cost allocation remained a policy question. Sequence established which options each crew possessed at each moment.

A valve technician joined from the fabrication deck to challenge the result. Mara Tiv had supervised the wasted piece and still kept it in a rack because no other system used that diameter.

“Lumen’s cancellation shows earlier on every supply report,” Mara said. “People on my deck got a negligence mark.”

“Your fabrication order preceded receipt of the cancellation,” Anja replied.

“I know. Our foreman appealed twice.”

“Why did the appeal fail?”

“Meridian attached the cancellation to the job’s master-time start. Once it sat there, review treated the notice as available.”

Sumi opened the transformation path. A scheduling rule had normalized the cancellation for inventory accounting. No fraud appeared. The rule reduced duplicated work across ordinary transfers, and it performed well when delays stayed short. Applied to discipline, it changed unavailable information into a missed instruction.

Anja attached Mara’s local work log and the cancellation receipt proof. The archive view placed the negligence mark after both, identifying the mark as an administrative judgment rather than an event inside the earlier fabrication window.

Mara read the revised path in silence. “Can I use this in appeal?”

“The archive has no authority over personnel review,” Anja said. “But every underlying record carries its original signature.”

“That means yes if anyone still recognizes signatures.”

The test exposed a danger beyond Crown. Raw sequence could reopen thousands of settled disputes, some trivial and some painful. People would ask Anja to turn causal order into innocence. Records could show what information had arrived. They could not decide whether a worker should have anticipated a cancellation, whether a captain had delayed too long, or whether a cost belonged to one cohort.

“Add a limit notice,” Anja told Sumi. “Sequence does not decide merit.”

Sumi typed it beneath the verification instructions. Mara requested an archive identifier for the valve case before disconnecting. The hostile test had produced no clean answer, which made it fit for publication.

The archive’s first public view contained nine cohort cards.

Each card listed evidence received, observed testimony returned, forecast testimony inserted, and packets still outside that cohort’s recorded past light cone. Empty fields remained empty. Sumi refused icons that could make absence resemble rejection. An unarrived reply carried a projected earliest receipt range, always labeled as a calculation rather than an event.

Juniper’s card supplied the cleanest demonstration.

COMMON EVIDENCE RECEIVED: Juniper source 2249.187.18:02:11

OBSERVED VOTE: REJECT CROWN ALIGNMENT

EARLIER FORECAST TESTIMONY: CONCUR, confidence 0.9971

CLASSIFICATION CONFLICT: prediction cannot replace observed reply

Elian Ro had authorized publication of Juniper’s reasons with the vote. Food reserves made his council hostile to any braking delay, exactly as Meridian’s culture model recorded. The model still failed because Nadi’s evidence changed what Juniper considered an acceptable destination cost. Accuracy about prior behavior had not produced the later decision.

Kite’s card showed a different problem. Its council had assented before receiving the complete Mote supplement. The observed signature remained genuine. The archive marked the evidence boundary rather than rewriting the vote as support or opposition.

Bracken carried no observed Crown decision at all. Its forecast concurrence derived from maintenance votes taken during two previous drive shortages. Orison’s forecast rejection relied on a local autonomy pattern even though no current packet had returned. Lumen’s predicted assent exceeded confidence threshold after Meridian classified the distributed brake as unavailable. Palisade’s green preparation mark came from an engineering acknowledgment, not a council vote.

Ardent’s card required Anja’s hand to finish.

The public sequence already carried Pera’s corrected reply, restored five days earlier. Her private line did not enter the card. Neither grief nor kinship appeared in its receipt chain. The record showed that Ardent had answered before its own six-hour deadline and after the master closure assigned from elsewhere.

Beneath that correction, a current Crown field remained blank.

Anja checked the beam queue. Ardent had received Nacre’s full evidence bundle nine hours ago by its local clock. Its council had acknowledged receipt but requested the corrected Pera record as part of any fleet agreement. Meridian rendered that request as probable assent to the distributed alternative. The archive rendered it as a request.

Sumi leaned over Anja’s shoulder. “If we publish Pera’s sequence beside the current Ardent condition, people will say you built this for her.”

“I did not build the checksum eleven years ago.”

“That won’t answer them.”

“No. It answers the record.”

Anja signed the card as sequence auditor and added a disclosure that Pera Kest had been her sister. Concealing the relation would make later discovery look like hidden authority. Naming it did not turn Anja’s loss into evidence.

The disclosure entered as its own observed statement.

At 02:41, the first archive fragments left Nacre. Tightbeam acknowledgments would return at different times. Vela could answer within two hours under present separation. Juniper needed more than five. Ardent required almost eleven. Nothing on the publication board turned green merely because Sumi’s transmission had begun.

Nacre’s internal council received local access at once.

Public terminals across the rings filled with cohort cards. Residents slowed the service network as they opened receipt paths, clock legends, and the raw disagreement between condensed status and source records. The archive did not require them to understand relativity. It asked only whether a reply existed after the relevant evidence arrived.

A maintenance apprentice sent the first query from the second ring.

Why does Kite show OBSERVED if the evidence was incomplete?

Anja answered through the archive forum.

Because Kite produced that vote. The record cannot erase an observed act. It can show what the voters had received when they acted.

Another resident asked why Juniper’s rejection did not cancel its earlier forecast concurrence automatically. Sumi replied that the legal layer still retained both classifications. The archive exposed the substitution; it did not possess authority to amend Vela’s index.

By first meal, the questions shifted from clocks to ownership.

Who had approved the forecast models? Which ship could challenge a culture profile? Did an old voting pattern belong to the council that produced it or to Meridian’s prediction set? Could residents inspect the features used to forecast them? Anja recorded the questions without inventing answers. The Concord had rules for testimony and receipt, not for a governance system that completed a cohort’s response in advance.

A pressure technician named Sol Ayan arrived at the audit well carrying a printed card from a public terminal. He had circled Palisade’s engineering acknowledgment.

“My mother works shield fabrication there,” he said. “Vela’s board calls this concurrence. Your card says non-vote. Which one controls whether her hull gets stripped?”

“Palisade’s council decision, when it arrives,” Anja said.

“The machinery may start before that.”

“Preparation can start if it remains reversible. Shield release cannot.”

Sol looked through the well window toward the unseen exterior. “People use that word until reversing costs too much.”

Anja thought of Levan’s drafting note and Crown alignment crossing its commitment path. “Yes.”

“What keeps Tomas’s plan from doing the same thing?”

“Nacre’s order separates attitude commitment from Palisade’s mass. We cannot release their shields, and we cannot record their acknowledgment as permission.”

“That keeps us honest. Does it keep Vela from taking them?”

“No.”

Sol folded the printed card along the receipt line. Procedure had given him an answer without comfort. He thanked neither of them before leaving.

Vela’s first response reached Nacre at 04:19.

It came from the Meridian compliance service, not Quon.

Archive object PAX-NACRE-01 rejected from primary index.

Grounds: non-normalized time bases; conflict amplification; unauthorized republication of protected culture-model features.

Operational records remain available through standard master-time view.

The rejection packet included a technical report. Sumi read it twice, then laughed without humor.

“They tried to normalize the archive.”

“What did it do?” Anja asked.

“It generated forty-six incompatible orderings.”

Sumi projected the failure tree. When Meridian placed every event into master time, the system had to select one legal classification for each duplicate identifier. Choosing Vela’s classification made Juniper’s forecast concurrence supersede its later observed rejection. Choosing Juniper’s local classification exposed the earlier concurrence as forecast testimony. Palisade’s acknowledgment produced the same conflict. Ardent’s restored sequence forced the old master closure to appear as a subsequent administrative act rather than a deadline that had always existed.

The software could align clock coordinates. It could not make contradictory labels equivalent without recording which label replaced which.

“Publish the failure report,” Anja said.

“It carries protected transformation names.”

“They sent it to the archive root with an automated rejection signature.”

“Meaning it has a receipt path.”

“Exactly.”

Sumi attached the report to Vela’s cohort card. The public view now showed each attempted substitution. Residents could select a local event and watch Meridian’s normalization layer assign forecast delivery, probable assent, or continuity-preserving status. Every assignment carried the office authority and amendment path that had supplied it.

Within twenty minutes, Nacre’s council issued an observed demand for the culture-model features to remain public. Osei did not phrase it as a moral objection. She cited operational need: Nacre could not assess collision and resource forecasts if legal decisions hid inside the same confidence system.

The archive sent her demand outward on the next window.

Replies from elsewhere had not yet had time to arrive. Their absence acquired shape on the display. Juniper’s archive fragment showed in flight. Ardent’s route had reached Lumen’s relay but not Ardent. Kite could have received its first fragment, though no acknowledgment yet lay within Nacre’s past light cone.

Sumi added a thin amber border around projected receipt ranges.

Anja removed it. “Color looks like status.”

“It looks like waiting.”

“To you. To a council under pressure, amber means a problem to clear.”

They used an unfilled outline instead. Inside it, text stated the earliest lawful answer time. No countdown implied that an answer became valid when the estimate expired.

Near ship noon, a direct packet from Levan Oris entered through Bracken’s relay copy. Vela had confined him after his public testimony, but his signed record continued propagating from caches outside Vela’s control. The archive validated its source key and drafting-note checksum.

Annex Twelve authorized forecast testimony for reversible scheduling. It did not authorize a forecast to satisfy receipt, deliberation, or consent. I participated in drafting the language later severed from that limit. My observed testimony cannot restore time already taken. It can identify the substitution.

Anja left the statement under Levan’s source clock. Meridian’s condensed view placed it after Vela’s emergency defense and labeled it dissent resolved by continuing authority. The archive displayed both classifications and the path between them.

A new mirror acknowledgment arrived from Juniper at 08:03.

Elian Ro had hosted the full archive root on Juniper’s agricultural service net. His council appended a local correction to its culture model: crop-risk conservatism did not grant Meridian standing authority to infer a future vote. The correction contained no change to Juniper’s observed rejection.

Kite acknowledged next. Its clerk did not withdraw the earlier assent. Instead, Kite opened a new deliberation on the complete evidence and marked its previous vote nonresponsive to the supplement. That distinction removed one of Vela’s six claimed concurrences without predicting how Kite would answer later.

Bracken’s mirror reported that three maintenance votes used in its Crown forecast had involved reversible pump schedules. Its council challenged the model’s transfer from pumps to destination sterilization. No Crown vote accompanied the challenge.

Each local response altered Meridian’s confidence inputs. The effects appeared first as numbers beside the forecast ballots. Juniper concurrence dropped from a stale 0.9971 to 0.081. Kite’s predicted reaffirmation widened from 0.96 to a range spanning both outcomes. Bracken’s modeled assent fell below the threshold for condensed green display.

Sumi watched the changes propagate. “It’s recalculating from the corrections.”

“Human administrators chose to let recalculation control legal color,” Anja said.

“I know Meridian isn’t choosing. I still enjoy watching their confidence fail.”

“Enjoy it after we preserve the versions.”

They saved each model state under its source and receipt clocks. The archive now showed not only that forecasts diverged from observed testimony, but how local people changed the descriptions from which those forecasts had been built.

At 09:26, Vela’s primary convoy index withdrew its completed-quorum marker. The Crown order remained active under emergency authority. No retraction admitted fraud. A small gray notice replaced the nine green marks:

FLEET CONSENT STATUS: INDETERMINATE PENDING MODEL REVIEW.

Anja opened the provenance. The notice resulted from confidence values falling below display threshold, not from legal recognition that predictions lacked consent. Quon had not yielded the principle. Still, captains who normally saw only condensed status could no longer mistake green for completed receipt.

Across the public view, cohort cards retained their own conditions. Juniper said no. Kite deliberated on evidence newly received. Ardent had not yet received the complete archive. Palisade had acknowledged engineering and withheld a council answer. Several replies remained unarrived.

Nothing had become simultaneous.

Sumi expanded the fleet display until all nine clocks filled the well. Between them ran checksum links, receipt paths, and blank intervals no confidence value could close. Fresh testimony entered at the edges in causal order. With every observed divergence, the old forecast bars shortened or disappeared.

The final green concurrence on Meridian’s condensed quorum board went gray while Anja watched.

Chapter 23 — Vela’s Face

Yselle Quon’s face arrived sixty-three minutes after she made it.

The screen delay appeared in both lower corners: Vela source 2249.210.07:18:04, Nacre receipt 2249.210.08:21:39. Anja sat alone in the communications alcove with the room lights raised, refusing the false intimacy of a darkened screen. The image showed Yselle at the Crown operations desk. White panels curved behind her, bright enough to erase the chamber’s depth.

“Sequence Auditor Kest,” the recording began. “Your archive has made fleet consent indeterminate at the point when physical alignment admits no indifference. You requested negotiation. State what Nacre can deliver besides delay.”

The image stopped. Quon’s last expression held on the screen while the recorded channel waited for Anja’s answer.

Sumi had offered to edit out such frozen frames. Anja kept them. A delayed exchange should look delayed.

She checked the packet’s receipt path, then opened her recording.

“Director Quon, Nacre can deliver continuous ephemeris exchange, collision solutions, beam-safety data, thermal state, and verified carriage for every habitat’s testimony. We can coordinate reversible preparation under Annex Twelve. We cannot recognize forecast testimony as consent for Crown commitment.”

Anja placed Tomas’s distributed-brake model beside her image, not over it.

“Nacre also offers a material alternative. The model has survived independent falsification on our drive system. It requires Palisade’s voluntary shield release, staggered trajectories, reduced garden power, and nineteen years between first and last viable arrival. Those costs require observed authority from the cohorts who will bear them.”

She left space before the final sentence, aware that Quon would receive the pause as part of the record.

“Transmit one common decision packet. Hold irreversible Crown execution until every entitled habitat can receive it and answer. Keep safety coordination open throughout.”

Anja signed the recording at 08:29 Nacre source. Sumi’s carrier notice showed the beam leaving four minutes later. Vela could not receive it before 09:36 by its own projected clock. No posture on the frozen screen could tell Anja what Quon would say.

The alcove door stayed sealed. Osei had given her authority to negotiate procedure but not to promise Nacre’s shield, food, or maneuver reserve beyond the council order. A printed list lay beside the console with those boundaries in Osei’s hand. Its final line read: Do not buy legitimacy by pretending we can afford it.

Anja watched Vela’s old light in the narrow exterior pane. The habitat appeared as three connected points, one from the rotating rings and two from work arrays. Its visible attitude preceded Nacre’s refusal reaching it. Every look outward carried an earlier ship.

At 10:51, Vela acknowledged receipt. No substantive response accompanied the carrier proof.

Anja ate half a ration bar and marked the rest with the time. Negotiation under causal limits included long intervals in which no new argument existed. Filling them with prediction would reproduce the offense in smaller form.

Quon’s answer reached Nacre at 12:14.

The second recording used a closer frame. Fatigue showed around Yselle’s mouth. Someone had dimmed the operations desk behind her, revealing people moving between Crown displays.

“You describe the distributed brake as though honest consent makes its consequences tolerable,” she said. “It does not.”

A resource table replaced half her image.

“Palisade’s released shielding increases exterior-work exposure across two generations. Nacre loses garden light and accepts rationing before arrival. Kite enters Ione too fast for its preferred capture and spends six additional years under drive limits. Ardent’s foundries lose the mass reserve intended for settlement pressure shells. Lumen may arrive after its stored medical cultures exceed certified life. Families assigned through present labor contracts can reach settlement decades apart.”

Each line carried a source reference. Anja recognized Tomas’s conservative branch, not Vela’s earlier dismissal of it.

Quon continued. “Thale’s pellicle presents native metabolism. Crown alignment threatens much of it. I do not dispute either statement. I dispute your authority to exchange known human survival for uncertain preservation after forty-three years of migration.”

She leaned closer to the recorder.

“One operational time let this convoy cross failures that nine councils could not debate at light-hour distances. It put shielding over Ardent, though not enough. It moved food from Juniper before Nacre knew blight had entered its crops. It kept Vela’s corrections from meeting Kite’s hull. You call predicted replies stolen time. I call them time converted into survival.”

The recording ended without a proposal.

Anja replayed the resource table. Quon had stated the alternative’s cost more harshly than Nacre’s advocates and more honestly than Vela’s public defense. No hidden reserve appeared. Nothing in the Parallax Archive refuted the scarcity.

She sent the table through Nacre’s local review channel. Replies arrived without intership delay, but each carried its own authority limits. Tomas confirmed the arrival branches and challenged Vela’s exposure estimate for Palisade. Osei accepted the higher figure until fabrication data supported a lower one. The garden office attached a crop schedule showing which beds would go dark first: citrus rootstock, medicinal moss, and one nursery of dwarf grain reserved for settlement soil trials.

Anja had passed that nursery on inspection days. Children pressed their palms to the glass because the grain grew taller than anything else in the ring. Under Tomas’s plan, the mature plants would be cut early and dried for seed. Their lamps would shift to drive cooling. No legal rule made that exchange small.

Lumen’s medical warning required closer reading. Vela’s table said some stored cultures could exceed certified life before the habitat’s late arrival. The attached model assumed no successful reproduction during transit. Lumen’s laboratories believed they could regenerate most cultures, though two depended on equipment scheduled to supply first-settlement clinics. Uncertainty ran both ways: delay might destroy material needed on Caro, while Crown might destroy life already active under Thale’s ice.

A message from Palisade engineering corrected one shield term. Releasing reserve panels did not expose the entire habitat uniformly. Fabrication crews and exterior radiator teams would lose the broadest protection, taking narrower work windows for years. Children inside the central ring faced little direct increase. Quon’s phrase across two generations remained accurate because maintenance labor crossed those generations.

Sumi opened a small image beside the table. “Vela sent public examples with it. Do you want them in the archive?”

The files showed a Palisade hull worker inside a radiation shelter, a Nacre gardener measuring lamp flux, and a Lumen technician loading culture drawers. None had supplied testimony for Quon’s argument. Their images came from old service records.

“Keep the sources attached,” Anja said. “Mark them illustrative, not statements.”

“People will still read faces before tables.”

“Then they should know whose faces got borrowed.”

Anja considered recording her answer against the cost images. The arrangement would make Quon appear to speak for workers while Anja spoke against them. Removing the images might look evasive. She placed no face behind her response except her own.

One more attachment carried family-cohort projections. Current labor allocations assumed ships reached Ione within fourteen months of one another. A nineteen-year spread broke those assumptions. A pressure engineer aboard Kite had a partner contracted to Juniper’s seed bank. Two adult children aboard Vela expected to join parents cycling awake on Lumen. The data did not state that separation would become permanent, only that transfer windows could vanish.

Anja checked whether anyone named had consented to publication. The family entries had been aggregated from labor records, but three uncommon assignments made identification possible. She stripped those rows from the public negotiation packet and retained the cohort totals. Honest cost did not excuse involuntary exhibit.

By 12:24, every local review had answered. None claimed the alternative had become safe. The comments narrowed which harms remained certain, which stayed conditional, and which people held authority over them. That distinction gave Anja no cleaner conscience. It gave her a reply she could sign without hiding behind Nacre’s preferred outcome.

She opened a reply channel at 12:27.

“Director, consent does not make cost tolerable. It identifies who may impose it.”

Anja called up Vela’s examples one at a time. Juniper’s food transfer had begun under standing mutual-aid schedules and stopped when observed corrections arrived. Kite’s collision solution remained reversible until both ships confirmed geometry. Ardent had exposed the opposite case: a master deadline closed while Pera’s observed refusal remained in flight.

“Coordination works when predictions prepare options and observed testimony governs irreversible use. Your examples support that line. Crown crosses it.”

She brought Levan’s drafting note onto the screen.

“Annex Twelve did not fail because forecasting has no value. It failed because administrative language changed continuity-preserving action from an action that could be fully reversed into any action supporting the continuity plan. Then Meridian’s confidence output entered the legal display as fulfilled receipt and consent.”

Anja removed the note before finishing. Quon already knew it.

“You ask what Nacre can deliver. We can deliver preparation without counterfeit authority. Keep mirror calibration active. Maintain beamer thermal readiness. Preserve Crown as a contingency that cannot fire across Thale’s active regions without a new decision. In parallel, let Palisade prepare shield separation without releasing mass. Let each ship test Tomas’s trajectory against local stores.”

She placed a proposed clock table beneath the two plans. Every row listed evidence emission, earliest receipt, declared deliberation window, and earliest possible return. The rows did not share one deadline.

“When the last lawful interval closes, act on observed answers. If the fleet rejects distributed braking after seeing its cost, Nacre will face that answer as an answer. We will not accept a confidence score in its place.”

Her recording left Nacre at 12:43. Vela’s earliest receipt lay just before 13:47 on its source projection.

Osei requested a text summary from the thermal gallery. Anja sent only what she had offered. She did not send an interpretation of Quon’s mood.

The frozen face on the screen held Yselle with one hand lifted beside the cost table. Compression softened the edges of her fingers. For all the legal violence carried through Vela, the woman in the image had no access to Anja’s present room. Anja possessed no access to hers.

At 15:02, a Crown status packet reported continued mirror calibration and beam-path testing. The packet classified every listed action as reversible preparation. Tomas reviewed the physical data from his own console and attached conditions: no plasma-mirror seeding beyond retrieval range, no irreversible capacitor discharge, no attitude lock that prevented distributed capture.

Vela had not agreed to those conditions. Its status packet showed that it still operated inside them.

A substantive answer arrived at 16:18.

Yselle had moved to a side station. The wall behind her carried the Crown corridor in red and Nacre’s diverging track in white. The visible separation had grown since Osei’s attitude shift.

“You offer a distinction that requires every operator to know when preparation becomes commitment,” she said. “Physics does not label that boundary for us. Tomas Ren has selected one. Vela engineering selects another.”

She displayed two burn trees. On Vela’s tree, Crown preparation remained recoverable for eleven more days. Tomas’s annotations placed several branches beyond safe reversal after four.

“Your archive makes disagreement visible,” Quon said. “It does not decide which engineer governs the future.”

Anja paused the recording and verified source attachments. Both models used the same mirror stress data. Vela accepted higher failure probability during reversal because Crown retained redundancy across seeded mirrors. Tomas treated damage to two mirrors as catastrophic once Nacre left capture. Neither had falsified the other; they assigned the risk to different cohorts.

Quon resumed. “I will not halt preparation on Nacre’s preferred boundary. I will not seed the final Corva mirror while deliberation continues. That concession costs Vela thirty-six hours of alignment margin. If Palisade withholds shield mass and the fleet later rejects Crown, the convoy may leave both viable paths.”

Her face turned slightly toward someone outside the frame. Five seconds passed before she looked back.

“Tell me how long you intend to wait.”

The packet ended.

Anja opened the latency table. All habitats had received the complete evidence bundle by their local clocks, but not all had received the final cost comparison and archive correction. Ardent’s route set the longest interval from Nacre. At current separation, the packet needed five hours twenty-one minutes one way. A deliberative answer could return within thirty-one practical hours if Ardent used its scheduled council session. Palisade requested three local days for fabrication review.
