Lumen needed medical-storage modeling. Kite had reopened deliberation after invalidating its earlier vote.

No honest interval guaranteed agreement. It could only guarantee opportunity.

Sumi entered through the side door with a fresh carrier sheet. “Orison has mirrored the archive. Ardent receipt acknowledgment should arrive in forty-two minutes. Palisade asks whether Vela will honor a conditional release.”

“Has Palisade voted?”

“No. Engineering request only.”

Anja added the question to her reply without answering for Vela.

She recorded at 16:31. “Director, Nacre proposes two hundred sixteen hours from your receipt of the attached common packet. Nine days. The interval includes each current one-way latency, published council windows, and one repair margin for a missed beam. No forecast ballots. No earlier vote counted against incomplete evidence.”

The table gave each habitat its own earliest lawful reply. Ardent could answer after receiving the correction. Kite could reaffirm or reverse. Palisade could attach conditions to shield release. Vela would retain its own vote.

“During that interval, coordinate by declared physical boundaries. Vela publishes each Crown preparation step before execution with its latest reversible point. Nacre and Palisade publish the same for distributed preparation. Any ship may challenge the engineering classification. No preparation becomes evidence of consent.”

Anja attached a final clause.

“If observed replies reject the distributed brake, Nacre will not rename them. If they reject Crown, Vela must grant the same restraint.”

The message left at 16:49. Sumi calculated Vela receipt no earlier than 17:52.

“Two hundred sixteen hours,” Sumi said. “Quon will call it theatrical precision.”

“It ends after every declared council window.”

“And before Tomas’s conservative reversal boundary.”

“Yes.”

“You chose Nacre’s engineer.”

“I chose the earlier boundary. A later correction remains possible from there.”

Sumi nodded toward the cost table. “That principle still spends Vela’s margin.”

“It does.”

Anja could not ask Quon to honor reversible action while pretending the hold carried no material cost. The final Corva mirror would drift relative to its seed window. Reacquiring alignment required energy and exposed equipment. Waiting preserved choices unevenly, as every delay did.

The next reply could not arrive before 19:00. Anja left the channel open and slept in the chair for thirty-seven minutes. A carrier tone woke her when Ardent’s archive acknowledgment entered Nacre’s past light cone.

The packet contained no vote. Its council confirmed receipt of Pera’s corrected sequence and the distributed-brake cost model. Deliberation had begun under Ardent’s local clock. The earliest promised answer lay six hours ahead there, plus the return light path.

Anja placed the acknowledgment in the archive. Meridian’s forecast field predicted conditional support at 0.88. She left the number visible beside the blank observed-vote field.

At 19:26, Vela sent a request for Tomas’s exact mirror-damage threshold. The request carried Yselle’s office key but no recorded image. Anja routed it to drive control. Its arrival did not imply acceptance.

Quon’s final screen packet reached Nacre at 21:07.

The Crown operations desk had emptied behind Yselle. Only two trajectory staff remained at the far wall. The director looked directly into the recorder, though the gaze reached a lens rather than Anja.

“Nacre’s proposed interval ends at 2249.219.17:52 Vela source, two hundred sixteen hours after our receipt. Vela grants that final quorum interval.”

She displayed the preparation conditions. Final Corva mirror seeding would pause. Capacitor loading could continue below irreversible discharge threshold. Alignment corrections would remain within the published recovery tree. Each action would enter the Parallax Archive with source and receipt data. Distributed preparation could continue under the same rule.

“This grant does not recognize Nacre’s claim that the Crown majority lacked authority when commitment began,” Quon said. “It recognizes that the present conflict cannot be resolved by a confidence display the fleet no longer trusts.”

Her voice hardened on the next line.

“If observed testimony remains incomplete at the interval’s end, Vela will act under survival authority. If the distributed brake receives lawful support, I will require its advocates to own every listed cost. No one will call nineteen years a procedural victory.”

Anja expected the recording to stop. Yselle remained still for three seconds.

“You asked for coordination without a fiction of common consent,” she said. “For nine days, you have it. Prove that many clocks can produce an action before every path closes.”

The screen returned to the receipt frame.

Anja checked the source signature, the carrier proof, and the attached engineering limits. Sumi verified that Vela’s pause order had reached its own Crown deck before the recording left. The final mirror remained unseeded. Preparation continued inside boundaries both engineering teams could inspect.

Osei joined by local audio. “Did she grant it?”

“Observed and signed.”

“Any concealed commitment?”

“Not in the attached tree. Tomas still needs to review the capacitor threshold.”

“Then publish it.”

Anja added Quon’s recording to every cohort card. She did not label the interval peace, concession, or success. The archive displayed its beginning by Vela source clock, its receipt on Nacre, each ship’s projected reception, and the final limit Quon had signed.

Across the fleet view, blank vote fields remained blank.

On Vela’s delayed image, technicians continued reversible Crown preparation while nine local clocks advanced toward answers no screen could supply early.

Chapter 24 — Every Ship’s Time

Juniper’s vote arrived while Anja was removing a countdown from its cohort card.

The Parallax Archive had inherited the final interval from Vela’s signed grant. Sumi’s reader displayed nine local endpoints correctly, but a public interface had added time remaining beside each one. The phrase implied that silence became an answer when a number reached zero. Anja replaced it with earliest promised response and saved the earlier version in the correction path.

A carrier tone sounded before she finished.

Juniper source 2249.211.04:22:16. Nacre receipt 07:09:51. Two hours, forty-seven minutes across the current light path. Elian Ro’s council had deliberated for five hours after receiving Vela’s cost table and Anja’s procedural packet. No part of its answer existed on Nacre until the receipt proof completed.

The observed vote opened as a recording from Juniper’s seed exchange. Racks of sealed crop lines rose behind Elian. He had placed the council among the stores whose future it was deciding.

Juniper rejects Crown alignment and supports the distributed brake under three resource conditions. Food transfer to later-arriving habitats retains priority over settlement reserves. Seed banks remain common convoy property through the final arrival. No ship may condition food access on acceptance of a common civil clock.

Elian looked tired and angry.

We oppose nineteen years of divided arrival. We also know a farm cannot call its harvest necessary and salt the soil beneath it. The pellicle’s life does not need a ballot to enter our constraints. Our cohort has received the evidence and accepts the cost stated here.

The recording ended with seven council signatures and one dissent. Attached projections showed Juniper losing part of its first-settlement crop advantage by supporting later ships. Its vote did not treat food risk as symbolism. It spent a resource only Juniper could promise.

Anja verified source signature, evidence receipt, deliberation start, and return path. Sumi mirrored the packet to every host.

“Two observed supports,” Sumi said. “Nacre and Juniper.”

“Juniper counts once.”

“I did count it once.”

“You said two as if we had begun a race.”

Sumi glanced at the blank cards. “We have begun one. I’m trying not to predict who wins.”

Anja marked Juniper’s conditions as part of the vote, not a note beneath it. Tomas’s model could satisfy the food priority if Nacre surrendered additional garden output during the first delayed cohort. Osei had not authorized that surrender. Support for the plan now created a negotiation inside the plan.

By ship noon, Nacre’s council issued observed acceptance of Juniper’s food conditions. Its signature followed local resource review. No model filled the six hours between the request and the answer.

Kite answered on Day 37 with a refusal.

Its packet reached Nacre one hour thirty-eight minutes after source emission, routed directly across the forward quarter. The vote recording showed twelve people in a drive classroom. A trajectory model occupied the wall behind them, with family-transfer lines drawn between habitat tracks.

Kite rejects the distributed-brake authorization. The modeled capture branch places our habitat six years beyond Juniper and eleven years before Lumen. We lack verified maintenance life for that interval, and the proposed family-cohort remedies carry no transport mechanism after separation.

The council also rejected immediate Crown execution across Thale’s active band. It requested a revised centralized route and committed no mass to Tomas’s plan.

Sumi read the last clause twice. “Neither.”

“It answers the proposal,” Anja said.

“Quon will count it for Crown.”

“Then the archive will show a substitution.”

Kite’s reasons hurt because they did not depend on denial of the pellicle. Its council accepted Nadi’s evidence and still refused a maneuver it judged unable to keep its people together. Meridian had once predicted Kite would concur with Crown from its history of formation discipline. The observed vote declined both available paths.

Anja opened the family-cohort appendix. Kite had identified four hundred twelve contracted households with members assigned to different arrivals. The figure carried uncertainty, not faces. No one had yet designed communication law for parents who might reach Caro after their children had aged nineteen years elsewhere.

Osei joined the well during review. “Does Kite’s no break the distributed geometry?”

“Tomas modeled one nonparticipant,” Sumi said. “Kite can retain an independent capture branch if it maintains collision exchange. Two nonparticipants narrow Palisade’s release window.”

“Ask drive control to publish the exact dependency. Do not ask Kite to reconsider until they can see what their refusal changes.”

Anja entered the vote without argument. A lawful process had to survive an answer its advocates disliked.

Ardent’s packet arrived nine hours later.

Its source stamp preceded Nacre receipt by five hours fourteen minutes, inside the measured 4.8-to-5.6-hour one-way range. The council had waited for a local shift change so foundry crews could join. Heat distortion moved across the recording from the industrial ring.

The speaker did not mention Anja until the end.

Ardent supports distributed braking if the corrected sequence of the 2238 shielding crisis remains public on every archive mirror. The record must retain Pera Kest’s observed offer, the six-hour local window, Palisade’s unreceived mass option, and the later master closure. No settlement authority may seal that correction as personal material.

A second condition reserved fabrication allocations for pressure shells lost with Palisade’s shield transfer. Ardent would supply labor and foundry time. It could not supply the missing mass.

We do not make this condition to punish Nacre or Vela. We make it because a plan that depends on sacrifice must preserve who offered what before the answer arrived.

The signatures followed. Ardent’s vote showed nine in favor, three against.

Anja’s hands stayed flat on the console while she verified the packet. Pera’s name belonged to the public condition because Ardent had placed it there, not because Anja needed it. The private line remained outside the archive. She checked that boundary before signing the receipt.

Osei asked over local audio, “Can Nacre accept the correction condition?”

“It is already public.”

“Public can be temporary. They want durability.”

Sumi proposed distributing the correction root with every future archive reader. Deleting it would then break validation for later vote paths. The arrangement gave Pera’s record structural persistence without turning her message to Anja into fleet property.

Nacre accepted. Palisade acknowledged the pressure-shell obligation as an issue for its pending vote, not yet a promise.

Three supports stood beside one refusal. Vela’s field remained blank.

Bracken took until Day 39.

Its council returned no speech. The packet contained a maintenance demonstration: a pump isolation, a failed automatic correction, and a manual restart executed under separate clocks. Bracken’s operators showed that collision coordination could remain open while legal control stayed local. Their observed vote supported distributed braking on condition that each habitat retain authority to reject automated attitude changes not preceded by a local receipt.

Anja watched the demonstration twice. Bracken had answered the political question through work. Its condition complicated Tomas’s sequence, which relied on coordinated pulses spread across hours. Each ship would need enough advance receipt margin for local authorization.

Tomas attached a revised execution table. “Possible,” his note said. “Costs seven minutes of optimal mirror phase. Do not let anyone call those minutes free.”

Osei accepted Bracken’s condition after drive review. Four supports.

Palisade’s engineering data arrived the same day, followed by silence from its council. The data named every reserve panel proposed for release. It listed radiation shelter capacity, worker relocation time, fabrication losses, and the brake value of each kilogram. No vote accompanied the inventory.

Vela’s condensed board labeled the packet probable concurrence.

The archive labeled it engineering data received.

On Day 40, a Palisade carrier acknowledgment reported that its council had recessed after exterior crews challenged the exposure model. On Day 41, another notice extended deliberation for a hull inspection. The final interval allowed the delay. Anja kept both notices visible, resisting messages from Nacre residents who wanted the empty card colored amber.

Lumen answered during that wait.

Its medical council supported distributed braking after revising the arrival order. Lumen requested an earlier trajectory than Tomas’s preferred mass solution, enough to preserve two culture systems near certified life. Moving Lumen forward pushed Nacre nine months later and increased garden-light loss during cruise.

We cannot preserve every stored line. We can preserve reproduction capacity for all but two. Crown preserves our schedule by destroying active native metabolism. We choose the losses that leave both futures capable of growth.

The line risked sounding cleaner than the table beneath it. Lumen included that table: failed cultures, increased pregnancy restrictions during low-resource years, clinic equipment deferred on Caro. Its eight council signatures arrived after a twenty-two-hour local review.

Tomas recalculated through ship night. Nacre’s drive room rejected his first revision because the radiator margin disappeared in year three. His second moved Bracken’s pulse by eleven minutes and restored the margin. Bracken had to receive that change before its condition remained satisfied.

The plan’s supporters could not freeze their votes while engineering changed beneath them. Anja sent the revised table to Juniper, Ardent, Bracken, and Lumen. Each council returned an observed reaffirmation after its own latency. None came back inside the time a master display might have preferred.

Five supports now met the ordinary numerical threshold for fleet action. Yet the Concord required every entitled habitat to receive common evidence and return observed testimony before a causal quorum closed. Palisade still deliberated. Orison had not answered. Vela continued reversible Crown preparation under its published tree.

“Can we call it an actual quorum?” Sumi asked on Day 42.

“Not completed,” Anja said. “We have an observed majority. Quorum still names the receive-and-return cycle.”

“People need a phrase for the difference between this and Vela’s predicted six.”

“Then say five ships have actually supported it.”

Sumi entered those words on the public view.

Palisade’s vote reached Nacre on Day 43 at 18:06.

The recording began outside a shield locker. Council members wore exposure badges at their throats. Behind them, reserve panels sat in numbered cradles, dark slabs made from metals and hydrogen-rich composites carried across forty-three years.

Palisade authorizes release of the listed shield reserve for distributed braking under four conditions.

The speaker named them without rhetoric. Only listed panels could move. Crew shelters had to reach full capacity before separation. Ardent’s pressure-shell allocation would take priority in replacement fabrication. Release would begin only after Palisade received the final execution table with observed acceptance from every participating ship.

A hull worker stepped into frame for the minority report.

We are giving away working years from people who repair the outside of this habitat. Do not thank us by pretending we volunteered unanimously.

The vote carried ten approvals and four refusals. Palisade attached each signature to the complete evidence receipt. Its earlier engineering acknowledgment remained separate.

Anja validated the panel list against Tomas’s model. The mass met the minimum branch with less than one percent margin. No extra shield reserve existed behind the offer.

Palisade supplied a relocation schedule with the vote. Exterior crews would move into central shelters during panel separation, leaving radiators under remote inspection. Four repairs already due would wait until replacement shielding came from Ardent’s foundries. One delay affected a coolant hinge whose failure probability rose each month. The council had accepted that risk by name.

Sumi traced the panel identifiers through the archive. Vela’s Crown inventory had counted the same slabs as available convoy shielding without recording Palisade’s local exposure limits. Tomas’s early brake model had treated them as a single mass reserve. The observed packet broke the number into objects attached to workers, shelters, and deferred tasks.

“His physics did not change,” Sumi said.

“No. The authority around the mass did.”

Anja sent Palisade’s conditions to every participating ship. Juniper accepted the food cost of replacing delayed exterior work. Ardent reaffirmed its foundry obligation after receiving the exact panel composition. Bracken requested an inspection channel for the coolant hinge. Lumen offered remote diagnostic time during its earlier arrival branch. Each answer created work beyond the vote.

Tomas revised the execution table to place shield separation after crew-shelter confirmation and before the optimal pulse sequence. Seven minutes disappeared from mirror phase. He signed the loss rather than hiding it inside a tolerance range.

Six supporting habitats made the outcome arithmetically irreversible. Even if Orison and Vela both rejected the plan, six of nine supported it. The distributed brake now had an actual quorum of observed supporters in ordinary speech, though the legal causal quorum still lacked two returns.

Osei refused to order shield separation. “Palisade conditioned release on the final execution table and participating acceptances. We meet neither condition yet.”

Tomas objected that waiting cost mirror phase.

“Then publish the cost,” Osei said. “A condition does not become inefficient consent.”

The panel cradles remained locked.

Orison’s reply left its ship on Day 44. A relay receipt showed the packet crossing Bracken’s bead, but content could not reach Nacre for another three hours forty-one minutes. Meridian’s last culture model gave Orison rejection confidence of 0.72 based on prior sovereignty votes.

Anja turned the forecast field off, then restored it. Hiding the number when an observed answer approached would make the archive another preferred view. She left prediction beside the moving receipt path and waited for the packet to arrive.

Orison voted no.

Its council accepted the pellicle evidence and the archive’s legal case. It rejected the distributed plan because its stores could not support the assigned late-arrival branch without suspending wake cycles beyond local constitutional limits. Orison offered collision data and rescue reserves but no brake mass or maneuver commitment.

Our refusal does not authorize Crown. It states that this cohort cannot bear the proposed interval awake and remain the polity that departed.

The culture model had predicted the direction and missed the reason. Orison’s answer had legal force because its council produced it after receipt, not because Meridian assigned a useful probability beforehand.

Six supports and two refusals now occupied eight cohort cards. Every vote included reasons, conditions, source clocks, evidence receipts, and return paths. The count could not show how differently each ship had reached it.

Vela alone had not answered.

Day 45 narrowed around Yselle’s deadline.

Vela continued sending Crown preparation logs. Capacitor charge remained below the published irreversible threshold. Final Corva mirror seeding stayed paused. Each packet arrived about an hour after its source event, proof that coordination survived even while consent remained disputed.

Anja sent the complete observed record to Vela at 06:12 Nacre source. It included Nacre, Juniper, Ardent, Bracken, Lumen, and Palisade in support. Kite and Orison refused. The packet stated that six votes fixed the numerical outcome. It also stated that Vela’s observed answer remained necessary to complete the receive-and-return requirement.

Carrier receipt returned at 08:20. Vela had the record.

No council vote followed.

At 10:03, Quon’s office requested clarification of Palisade’s worker-relocation condition. At 11:47, Vela engineering challenged Tomas’s ion-band avoidance margin. At 13:14, a Crown packet warned that the final mirror seed window would close soon after the interval. Each message advanced preparation or tested the alternative. None answered the decision.

Nacre’s public boards accumulated people around them. Anja could see only local camera views: gardeners in work aprons, pressure crews between shifts, children held above adult shoulders. Nobody saw Vela’s present face. They saw the latest packet light permitted.

A teacher in the second ring wrote each source time on a slate below the fleet display. When a child asked why Vela’s square stayed empty, the teacher pointed to the receipt mark rather than offering a reason. The lesson took less than a minute and resisted the habit Meridian had taught adults.

Osei came to the audit well for the final hour. Tomas joined from drive control by local screen. Sumi kept all nine clocks separate.

“Can Vela claim survival authority when its interval ends?” Osei asked.

“It can claim it,” Anja said. “The archive will show six observed supports for another viable path.”

“Can we execute without Vela?”

Tomas answered. “Palisade can release. Participating ships can begin their pulses. Vela controls the forward beamer and seeded mirrors. Without its cooperation, our margin falls below certification. With active resistance, neither plan remains safe.”

“So the vote decides authority, not machinery.”

“It decides whose refusal machinery must not erase,” Anja said.

The final quorum interval ended at 17:52 Vela source. Its corresponding Nacre display time passed sixty-three minutes later, after light could have carried an event from the deadline. Anja did not close Vela’s card at 17:52. She waited through the one-way latency, then through the measured receipt uncertainty.

At 18:58 Nacre source, a carrier packet arrived from Vela.

It contained the final Crown preparation state, signed by engineering. Capacitors charged to the reversible limit. Mirror seed package held. Beam path constrained away from Thale pending command. No civil vote appeared in the envelope.

Sumi checked for a delayed attachment. “Nothing else.”

Anja expanded Vela’s receipt path. The complete fleet record had reached Quon’s office ten hours earlier. The director possessed the evidence, the six supports, the two refusals, and Palisade’s conditions. Silence no longer came from light travel.

Still, Anja would not predict what it meant.

She entered the preparation packet under Vela’s source clock and left its observed-vote field open. Around that blank, eight ships’ answers held their distinct shapes.

The convoy had produced a majority without borrowing anyone’s voice. It had not produced Vela’s.

At the close of Day 45, the only empty cohort card carried the ship that had once filled every absence in advance.

Chapter 25 — Breaking the Crown

Tomas Ren watched Palisade dismantle its future one checksum at a time.

The shield-release packet occupied the left side of his model board. Each line named a panel stack, a fabrication lot, its remaining exposure life, and the families whose descent allotments would lose protection when the stack left the habitat. Palisade had not sent a simple mass figure. Its council had sent consequences attached to serial numbers.

Across the board, the ion-band map from Thale moved in slow colored folds. Nadi Arko's latest transmission described the active pellicle regions eighteen hours before Tomas could see them. No instrument aboard Nacre could show their present condition. He had a seasonal model, a delayed observation, and uncertainty broad enough to contain errors that could sterilize an ocean.

“Palisade source confirms physical release in twenty-two minutes,” Sumi Dax said from communications. “Observed authorization matches the archive copy. No substitutions.”

Tomas enlarged the receipt path. “And Vela?”

“Nothing beyond carrier noise.”

Six boards faced him in the drive-model room. Five carried cooperative solutions from the habitats that had assigned people and matter to the distributed brake. The sixth showed Vela's beamer holding Crown alignment, still pointed through the part of Corva's magnetosphere they needed to avoid. A red commitment arc narrowed with every update.

They could begin without Vela. They could not finish the safest branch without it.

The alternative used no single decisive burn. Palisade would eject layered shield stock into six diverging tracks. Bracken and Orison would strike selected bundles with their fabrication lasers, opening the layers into conductive clouds. Kite would shape two clouds with its drive field. Nacre would spend rotation reserve and garden power to push a broad, weak braking pulse through them. Ardent and Lumen would take later pulses after their trajectories separated. Each action reduced velocity by little. Together, spread across years, they kept human arrival possible without driving Crown energy through Thale's active regions.

Vela owned the forward beamer and the cleanest angle. If it refused, Tomas could route around it, but the reserve margin fell below the value Osei had authorized. Two shield clouds would disperse before another ship could reach them. Nacre would then lose more garden light, Juniper would surrender seed-vault refrigeration, or the trailing habitats would add another four years to an arrival already spread across nineteen.

A tone marked Captain Osei's entry. She pulled herself through the hub hatch, caught the rail, and read the room before speaking.

“Material state?” she asked.

“Palisade release remains on schedule. Seven local actions stand authorized. Vela has not answered the handoff.”

“Can it still answer?”

“For fourteen minutes by its source clock, plus the known path. After that, its beam cannot open the second cloud before the cloud shears.”

Osei looked toward the garden-power board. “And if it waits fourteen minutes?”

“We wait here seventy-one before we learn what it did. The plan executes from signed source schedules, not from our display.”

That feature had frightened every captain during review. The ships could not watch one another in a common present. They had to commit local actions against receipt-proven plans and accept that distant events would already have occurred before their images arrived. Meridian had once hidden that discomfort beneath a green master-time bar. Tomas now exposed it on every board.

Small labels named the source clock beside each action. No line claimed simultaneity. A dashed region showed what Nacre could not yet know.

Osei tapped Palisade's panel list. “Their conditions?”

“Every receiving habitat carries the sacrificed exposure life as debt. No one gets to describe the panels as surplus. Replacement fabrication outranks settlement construction when a cohort arrives. If a ship cannot replace them, the others owe shelter mass according to capacity at that later date.”

“Political debt across nineteen years.”

“Material debt first. Politics will decide whether anyone honors it.”

The captain gave him a level look. “You built this.”

“I built a path that fails more slowly than Crown.”

“Do not become modest during execution. It wastes time.”

Tomas almost laughed. Osei had already turned to the next board.

A fresh line entered from Mote. Sumi routed it without passing the packet through Meridian's condensed display. The transmission carried a revised phase estimate for Thale's active ionic season. Nadi had marked three regions with high confidence, two with moderate confidence, and a broad southern band as unknown because Mote's damaged antenna had lost a portion of the observation set.

Tomas laid the delayed map over the brake geometry. Their preferred corridor crossed the southern uncertainty.

“Of course it does,” he said.

Sumi glanced over. “Can you move it?”

“Movement costs coupling.”

“How much?” Osei asked.

He ran the branch. The room's ventilation changed note while the calculation spread through the boards. Meridian handled ephemerides and field propagation as software should: rapidly, without opinion, and with every assumption listed. Tomas supplied the legal inputs himself. Observed ship commitments entered as fixed constraints. Forecast behavior remained outside the solution.

The revised corridor bent above the uncertain band. Palisade's third cloud would need to open wider, lowering its density. Nacre could compensate with a longer pulse, but the garden ring would lose another fraction of its reserved light during the next growing cycle.

“Four days of full-spectrum equivalent,” he said. “Added to the ration already accepted.”

Osei did not ask whether the pellicle justified four days. That question had ended when the councils voted. She asked which crops would pay.

“Leaf beds first,” Tomas said. “Medicinal algae stays protected. Fruit walls drop to maintenance. Juniper's transfer model says we can keep caloric loss inside the rationing case if their first seed shipment remains on its current trajectory.”

“If Juniper misses?”

“Then Nacre cuts awake allotments before it cuts cycling support.”

The captain studied the map for several breaths. “Take the safer corridor.”

Tomas entered the change. His thumb hesitated above the authorization field because the added loss touched people who had not seen this revision. Osei's emergency authority covered immediate ship survival, not ecological policy. Yet the actual quorum had authorized a brake constrained away from observed pellicle activity and had delegated local execution within published scarcity bounds. Four days remained inside those bounds.

He attached the changed crop estimate, signed by source clock, and sent it to the public execution stream.

No prediction completed the approval. Osei pressed her own thumb after reading the cost.

Palisade's release arrived first as numbers.

At 14:06 by its local clock, clamps opened along the habitat's fabrication spine. At 14:07, the first shield stack cleared the hull. Optical confirmation could not yet have reached Nacre, but the source packet carried independent clamp sensors and a photonic checksum from Palisade's outgoing beam. Tomas accepted the event provisionally. Final receipt proof would come with the light.

The model replaced twelve gray icons with moving blue marks.

Each stack contained carbon lattice, superconducting mesh, and hydrogen-rich armor intended to stand between families and interstellar dust. Engineers had designed it to survive. The distributed brake required the same matter to come apart. Timed charges separated the layers. Embedded loops took current and spread. A compact wall became a widening foil, then a cloud thin enough to shape with fields.

Palisade sent a camera view thirty-eight minutes later. In the delayed image, shield packages slid from the habitat's dark flank. No flames accompanied them. Small cold-gas tugs gave each bundle a different angle. Behind the moving cargo, exposed attachment frames formed pale rectangles along Palisade's hull.

Someone on its operations deck had placed the council conditions beneath the image.

RELEASE OBSERVED. EXPOSURE LIFE TRANSFERRED, NOT ERASED. ARRIVAL CLAIMS REMAIN ATTACHED TO THE NAMED LOTS.

Tomas read the sentence twice. The people in those exposed sectors would spend decades knowing their protection had become everyone else's braking surface. Gratitude would not stop dust. A constitutional promise would not turn replacement stock out of vacuum.

“First track clean,” said Ilya Ven from Nacre field control. “Second package has a spin error.”

The blue mark on Tomas's board began to wobble.

“How large?”

“Point six rotation above plan and increasing as the layers separate.”

Bracken's laser could correct the opening by striking one edge early. The adjustment would evaporate more conductive mesh and leave less matter for the field to hold. Tomas asked the model for outcomes. It offered four, ranked by braking yield.

He removed the ranking.

Yield alone had produced Crown. His board instead displayed each branch against the approved constraints: Thale loading, shield expenditure, food-light draw, trajectory spread, and collision risk. The second branch sacrificed nine percent of the faulty package and kept the ion corridor clear.

“Send Bracken correction two,” he said.

Sumi's hands moved. “Receipt in three hours at current separation.”

“They scheduled delegated correction authority.”

“I know. I am reminding the room that we will not see their answer before our own pulse.”

Ilya exhaled through his teeth. “Comforting.”

“It should not be.”

Tomas armed Nacre's field schedule. The ship's first action would begin after its receipt clock verified Palisade's release window. Bracken's correction remained causally unavailable to them, but Bracken had lawful authority to choose among published branches. That gap did not count as consent. It counted as bounded independence.

Beyond the drive-model room, a sequence of relays clicked through the habitat. Pumps shifted to storage loops. Workshops shut down nonessential furnaces. The garden ring surrendered its surplus generation, then part of its ordinary allotment. A power curve descended across the board in clean steps.

Tomas had worked beside such curves for thirteen years. He usually read them as capacity. This one named supper tables, germination racks, school hours, and the green light people sought when the metal passages narrowed around them.

A public channel opened from the garden ring. The image showed no crowd, only a cultivation technician beside a wall of dwarf citrus. Half the lamps behind her had dimmed to amber maintenance strips.

“Drive control,” she said, “garden transfer confirms.”

Tomas checked her source clock. “We have it.”

“Do you need the reserve tier?”

“Not yet.”

“Do not say yet if you mean no.”

He looked at Vela's silent board. “I mean not unless Vela misses the useful window.”

The technician nodded once. “Then we will keep the reserve tier armed.”

Her transmission ended. The citrus wall remained on Tomas's display until Sumi cleared it for bandwidth.

Nacre's garden lights had dimmed before any brake impulse touched the ship.

The first conductive cloud opened against the stars as a stain no human eye could have found without enhancement.

Tomas watched through field telemetry. Kite's shaping pulse entered from the far side, pinching the cloud into a slanted sheet. Bracken's laser struck along its upper edge. The sheet brightened in radio and began to carry current.

Nacre rolled four hundredths of a degree away from Crown alignment.

Hull vibration changed under Tomas's boots. The rotational system compensated for the field coils drawing power from the garden buses. A cup tethered beside Ilya's station tapped twice against its clip, paused, then began a slower tapping rhythm.

“Attitude on branch,” Ilya said.

“Cloud density?”

“Eleven percent below central estimate, within the published lower case.”

Tomas set the first pulse. Its route curved through Corva's magnetospheric edge, distant from the latest high-confidence ion-band. The southern unknown remained outside the three-sigma spread. Energy that missed the conductive cloud would continue into empty approach space rather than cross Thale.

The design sacrificed efficiency to uncertainty. That loss protected what they could not presently observe.

Osei anchored herself beside field control. “Execute when lawful.”

A receipt proof changed color. Palisade's release had entered Nacre's past light cone. The checksum agreed with the source packet. Nacre could now act on an observed event rather than a scheduled one.

Tomas pressed the pulse control.

Current left the drive reserve, crossed the temporary bus, and pulled the habitat's field into an asymmetry no passenger could see. The conductive cloud answered minutes later by its own location and source model. Momentum changed in increments too small for the body. On the trajectory board, Nacre's arrival line shifted by seven months.

Nobody cheered.

Field control began the damage inventory before the current reached baseline. Coil bank four had warmed beyond its preferred case. Two bearings in the garden transfer bus reported unequal loads, which meant the next pulse could not draw power along the same path. Ilya assigned a maintenance pair to the hub-side bearing and asked whether Tomas wanted to shorten the second pulse.

Tomas compared the heat curve with the ecological corridor. A shorter pulse preserved hardware but reduced their grip on the third cloud. Delaying it allowed the cloud to spread. The loss would move Nacre another three months outward and force Ardent to accept a steeper correction when its turn came.

“Alternate the bus,” he said. “Keep duration.”

“That takes the public kitchen reserve.”

“For eighty-six minutes. Post the draw before transfer.”

Sumi added the temporary loss to Nacre's execution stream. Within seconds, replies entered from kitchens across the three rings. Some asked which meal cycle would lose heat. One demanded that command shut its own gallery before touching communal cookers. Osei read that line over Tomas's shoulder.

“Reasonable,” she said. “Thermal gallery to standby. My cabin too.”

The additional power did little by engineering standards. Its public attachment mattered more. Every correction now carried a named source, a local cost, and the authority that spent it. No green summary hid the kitchen reserve inside total capacity.

A maintenance camera showed two suited figures pulling themselves along the transfer bus. Their lamps crossed the dark conduit while the bearing temperature climbed. Tomas held the second pulse at armed status until both workers clipped behind a shielding rib and Ilya received their spoken clearance.

A second pulse followed from Kite. Orison began its release. Ardent transmitted readiness against a schedule more than four hours old from Nacre's viewpoint. The distributed brake existed as separate local acts linked by received commitments. No master display could make them share a present.

Then Vela's board flashed.

Sumi caught the packet before Meridian condensed it. “Source event from Crown operations. Director Quon, observed. Full receipt path attached.”

“Time?” Tomas asked.

“Vela signed nine minutes before its last useful field opening.”

The image had already spent nearly an hour crossing to them. Yselle Quon appeared at the Crown operations deck with ceremony stripped away. Work lights made one side of her face look colorless. A trajectory field covered the wall behind her.

Vela acknowledges the observed authorizations carried in the Parallax Archive. Vela will break Crown alignment and execute distributed branch V-six within the published ecological corridor. This testimony does not merge habitat clocks or surrender Vela's authority over its cohort. It commits our beamer, our trajectory, and our share of the loss.

Quon looked away from the prepared text before adding one sentence.

Record that we arrived at this answer ourselves.

The packet ended.

Sumi displayed the raw source time beside Nacre's receipt time. Meridian contributed no voice, only propagation data and a verification result. Vela had answered late enough to frighten every habitat and early enough to matter.

Tomas opened branch V-six. The beamer would abandon Crown's central corridor, strike Palisade's second and third clouds, then accept a wider trajectory around Corva. Vela would lose command of the convoy's shared arrival geometry because no shared geometry would remain. Its own settlement window moved three years later.

“Verify their branch against the revised southern exclusion,” he said.

“They used Nadi's newest map,” Ilya replied. “Their source receipt predates ours by twenty-six minutes.”

Of course. Mote lay closer to Vela. The truth reached different ships in a different order, and this time nobody treated difference as defect.

Vela's beamer changed aim in the delayed optical feed. Crown's bright alignment marker separated from the physical beam trace. The old plan persisted on one board as a thin line crossing Thale. Beside it, the new pulses divided toward sacrificial clouds and empty magnetospheric corridors.

“Crown commitment falling,” Ilya said.

Tomas did not look at the status color. “Show material events.”

Clamps released. Fields rose. Source clocks signed. Receipt paths lengthened across the convoy. Palisade's useful spare shields continued outward, their protective surfaces opening into brake clouds. Nacre's power draw reached the garden reserve boundary and held there. Bracken's correction cut the wobbling package into a stable crescent. Vela's first distributed strike reached the cloud according to its source telemetry, though Nacre would wait for light before calling it observed.

The model recalculated human arrival. Nine lines no longer converged on one date. They spread across nineteen years, with uncertainty beyond that if replacement fabrication failed. Food margins tightened on every line. Exposure margins narrowed most sharply on Palisade. No branch restored what the maneuver spent.

Thale's loading estimate fell below the published protection ceiling. Not zero. Not certainty. The active regions in Nadi's map remained outside the high-energy paths, and the broad unknown received only background spill within Mote's measured tolerance.

Tomas saved every assumption with the execution record.

A low shudder moved through Nacre as the second local pulse began. Far below the hub, pumps took over from darkened garden exchangers. The air would grow drier by morning. Meals would change within a day. Children who had expected to reach Ione as young adults would arrive in middle age, if their ship carried them there at all.

Osei read the separated trajectory lines. “Do we still make a viable approach?”

“Yes. Different approaches.”

“Does Thale hold?”

“The pellicle keeps its active bands under every observed case. The unknown regions keep the agreed margin.”

“Does Crown remain recoverable?”

Tomas checked the beamer geometry, cloud expansion, and Nacre's changed attitude. “No. Not after Vela's next strike.”

They waited for that event through the time light required.

The garden technician sent a second image. Amber strips now edged whole terraces. Citrus leaves reflected a dull bronze instead of their usual white-green. Behind the technician, two workers covered seedling trays to preserve warmth.

On another board, delayed optics from Palisade showed the last shield stack clearing its clamps. Empty frames patterned the habitat's side.

Vela's receipt proof arrived at 19:41 Nacre time. Its beamer had struck the opened cloud. The physical line of Crown could no longer be recovered before the old alignment window closed.

Tomas marked no victory. He signed the observed strike, attached Nacre's receipt clock, and watched nine arrival lines continue apart.

Across the darkened garden terraces, the reserve tier remained unlit.

Chapter 26 — The Cost of Different Arrivals

The first ration dispute began before Osei reached the garden ring.

Two hundred people had gathered outside the western distribution room, though the revised meal schedule did not take effect until ship evening. A food clerk stood behind the sealed counter with three council deputies and a handwritten sign: FRESH LEAF ALLOTMENTS SUSPENDED. The lamps above them burned at half spectrum. Everyone's skin looked gray beneath the amber remainder.

Osei entered through a maintenance passage rather than ask the crowd to clear a ceremonial path. The air carried warm soil, nutrient salt, and the sharper odor of citrus leaves cooling in darkness. Along the terrace wall, technicians had covered seedling beds with reflective cloth. The cloth held heat but could not replace photons.

A woman near the counter recognized Osei and raised a ration card.

“My household loses greens tonight,” she said. “Command ate under full light this morning.”

“Command meals enter the same schedule at eighteen hundred,” Osei said.

“After you had breakfast.”

“Yes.”

That answer produced several angry voices and one short laugh. Osei did not defend the timing. The brake had drawn power during first meal. Food services needed hours to recalculate stocks, and the people who ate before the change had received something later shifts would not. No sequence correction could distribute the loss backward.

Garden representative Ilya Chen stepped from beside the counter. She no longer wore her pollination veil. Dark marks beneath her eyes matched the bands of unlit growth racks behind her.

“We need the captain inside,” she told the crowd. “The ration board stays public.”

“Public numbers do not make more food,” someone answered.

“No,” Ilya said. “They show which person took it.”

The distribution room had once stored festival tables. Staff had replaced them with portable boards listing crop losses, stored calories, kitchen heat restrictions, and wake-cycle demand. Numbers differed across the three rings. Children under twelve kept full protein. Medical diets remained protected. Heavy labor gained calories but lost nearly all fresh produce. Awake adults in noncritical assignments took the largest immediate cut. Residents scheduled to enter cycling within sixty days had their induction reserves sealed against council use.

Each protection pushed scarcity elsewhere.

An appeals table occupied the room's far end. Rationing staff had expected questions about medical diets. Instead, the first cases concerned time. A maintenance worker named Bo Sen held a transfer notice showing that his husband would enter cycling in nine days. Their household allocation assumed two awake adults until induction, then released one share back to communal stores. The revised schedule reduced both shares immediately.

“You count him awake when you take food,” Bo said, “and absent when you count objections.”

Marat examined the notice. “The household formula changed with the emergency table. It does not remove his vote.”

“He cast no vote on this brake. His work shift ran through council.”

The clerk offered a supplemental calorie packet for the induction week. Bo refused to treat the packet as an answer. He wanted the formula corrected for every household crossing a wake boundary during the cuts.

Osei asked how many households shared the condition.

“Eighty-three in the next thirty days,” Marat said. “Another two hundred six scheduled to wake. Their rations rise while total light stays down.”

Ilya pulled a blank board from storage. “Then publish the crossings by date and ring. No household names.”

A second claimant objected that cycling residents kept protected induction food while children awake now lost fresh produce. A garden technician answered that a failed induction could kill within hours, while a month without leaves would not. The claimant asked why current families always received the survivable harm.

Nobody at the table found a sentence that repaired the arithmetic.

Osei directed staff to separate necessity from fairness on the appeals form. Medical minima could not be voted away. Distribution above those minima remained political and had to return to council each seven days. The distinction promised repeated arguments, but it prevented a temporary emergency table from acquiring permanent authority through inertia.

Bo signed his appeal and took the supplemental packet after all. “This covers nine days,” he said.

“It covers nine days,” Marat agreed.

No clerk predicted what Bo would accept on the tenth.

Osei walked the boards with Ilya and food chief Marat Ves. No total had changed since Tomas's execution estimate, but totals did not eat. The western ring grew most of Nacre's leaf crops and would see the loss first. The eastern ring held grain reserves and accused the west of asking for unequal compensation. The lower garden cohorts wanted Juniper's promised seed transfer counted now. Marat refused because Juniper's shipment had not yet entered a launch tube.

“Forecast delivery does not fill a pantry,” he said.

A deputy pointed at the crowd beyond the glass. “Try putting that on the sign.”

Marat handed her the marker.

The first formal challenge came from Hale, the representative who had voted against leaving Crown geometry. He arrived with two council clerks and a petition carrying 1,106 local signatures. Osei read the header before he spoke.

“Suspension of command discretion pending resource review,” she said.

“Not suspension of the brake,” Hale replied. “That event has occurred. I want further emergency transfers held until the council assigns burden by law.”

“Field control may need corrections.”

“Then field control can name them. Your authority cannot widen every time the plan encounters reality.”

Osei looked past him toward the dark fruit walls. “Agreed.”

Hale seemed prepared for resistance. Her answer delayed him for a breath.

She gave the clerks access to command's live resource ledger and signed a restriction: any transfer beyond the published brake envelope required a fresh Nacre council decision unless delay threatened immediate life. The term immediate received a narrow definition tied to pressure, thermal control, collision, radiation, or irreversible loss of brake capture.

“Will you submit the kitchen draw from yesterday to review?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“And the shutoff of your gallery?”

“That too.”

Hale accepted the signed restriction without gratitude. “I still think you spent our future too early.”

“You may continue thinking it in office.”

“That depends on the confidence vote.”

Political fracture had not waited for arrival. Nacre now contained people who opposed Crown, people who opposed the distributed brake, and people who accepted both the maneuver and Osei's removal. Others wanted Vela cut out of all future coordination. A smaller group called for Meridian's destruction, as though breaking software could recover shield panels already moving into conductive clouds.

Osei had communications publish a technical notice instead. Meridian remained in service for ephemerides, collision solutions, thermal schedules, and prediction clearly marked as prediction. Its legal classifications no longer carried authority on Nacre. Engineers isolated those functions with visible interfaces and human sign-off. Software had participated in the fraud because people had changed what its outputs were allowed to mean. Treating it as a defeated enemy would preserve the human decision behind a machine-shaped story.

Beyond the distribution glass, the handwritten sign gained a second line in smaller letters: JUNIPER SHIPMENT NOT COUNTED UNTIL OBSERVED RELEASE.

A child asked his father what observed meant. The father answered by pointing at the empty fresh-leaf bin.

Shield redistribution reached the council as a list of unprotected hours.

Palisade had released all useful spare shield reserve to seed the brake, but its conditions required every habitat to account for the transferred exposure. Nacre's share amounted to four replacement panel lots or equivalent shelter capacity when Palisade arrived. No one knew which Nacre would possess at that date. Their trajectories now placed Palisade eleven years behind the earliest settlement cohort and three years behind Nacre, subject to later corrections.

The council met in distributed rooms because the public chamber drew more power than eight smaller links. Each image carried its local source clock. No master strip reordered replies into conversational smoothness.

Chief Varo placed the shield claims beside new radiation forecasts. “Palisade wants acknowledgment within one local day. Ardent has requested a portion of our remaining mobile shielding for its first correction. Lumen offers fabrication labor at arrival in exchange for present tracking support. Orison declines any debt that binds a future elected council.”

“Can Orison decline after using the brake clouds?” Hale asked.

Anja answered from the audit well. “Its observed vote accepted material liability within capacity at arrival. The phrase within capacity remains disputed. Orison argues that no present council can define future capacity.”

“They accepted a door with no room behind it,” Ilya said.

“They accepted an obligation whose enforcement requires later government,” Anja replied. “That weakness belongs in the record.”

Osei asked what Ardent needed.

Varo showed its transmitted model. Crown preparation had left Ardent with a poorly oriented shield segment during the first distributed correction. Its industrial ring could rotate behind fixed armor, but the maneuver would interrupt foundry cooling and consume stores already reduced by Pera's disaster eleven years earlier. Nacre could release two mobile panels from an unused transfer lock. Their departure would increase exposure during Nacre's own later dust crossing.

“How long until Ardent's request reaches the authority that can answer it?” Osei asked.

“It has reached us. Their full damage model followed forty-three minutes later and arrived six minutes ago.”

“Then start review from the full model's receipt, not the first request.”

The council considered two physical losses separated by nearly five light-hours. Ardent's risk lay in events Nacre could only see late. Nacre's risk would unfold years before anyone occupied the first settlement shelter. They could compare models. They could not share a present emergency.

Tomas joined from field control, face drawn after twenty hours awake. “Releasing one panel keeps Ardent inside its low-foundry branch. Two improve margin but do not change the branch. Nacre can afford one if we move exterior repairs to the inner face during our dust crossing.”

“Afford means what?” Hale asked.

“Three hundred twelve additional suit-hours in constrained radiation windows over the next four years. Some hull work waits. A severe micrometeoroid strike during that interval would make the choice worse.”

“People, Tomas.”

“Forty-one exterior workers take the added schedule. No modeled deaths. Higher cumulative exposure for sixteen.”

Silence on a distributed council did not begin or end at once. One member lowered her eyes before another had received Tomas's final sentence through an internal routing delay. Osei waited until every receipt indicator confirmed delivery.

Ilya proposed one panel, with a recorded claim on Ardent fabrication after its arrival. Hale moved to add an exposure guarantee for the sixteen workers. The clinic representative objected that a guarantee could promise treatment but not undo dose. They replaced the word with priority.

The vote passed six to two.

Osei signed the release, then watched a panel crew begin work on a local camera. Mechanics crossed the transfer lock in suits, unlatching armor designed for emergencies exactly like the one they might later face. Orange tags marked each fastening point. The panel moved along a rail toward an ejection cradle while the exposed lock wall disappeared behind a temporary foam layer.

Ardent could not know their decision for hours.

“Send the vote, the minority reasons, and Tomas's risk table,” Osei said. “No completion marker until their receipt returns.”

Varo nodded. “That could take most of a shift.”

“It takes what it takes.”

A notice from Vela entered during transmission. Director Quon acknowledged the distributed execution and proposed restoring a central trajectory board under Meridian, with each habitat's legal layer remaining local.

Hale read the proposal first. “Useful.”

Ilya shook her head. “It begins again with one board.”

“Ships still need to avoid each other.”

“They avoided each other yesterday without claiming one consent.”

Osei requested separate votes on the operational feed and its legal treatment. Nacre accepted Vela's ephemeris exchange. It rejected any status color that collapsed unreceived political answers into completed action. Kite accepted the full proposal. Juniper demanded that food-transfer dates display its agricultural calendar rather than Vela source time. Ardent insisted that Pera Kest's corrected sequence remain attached to every future emergency-window calculation. Palisade required material debts to survive clock conversion. Orison reserved all constitutional questions for its arrival council.

No common settlement erased those terms.

By ship evening, the old arrival diagram no longer fit on one page.

Osei stood in the thermal gallery while Varo projected nine trajectories along the curved wall. Crown had shown a bundle narrowing toward a single Corva approach. The distributed brake showed a fan. Kite and Juniper occupied the earliest corridor. Nacre followed years later. Vela, despite its lead position, had moved to a slower branch after spending its beamer on the shield clouds. Palisade and Ardent trailed under wider uncertainties. Lumen's line carried a fork because its council had authorized two possible correction schedules and refused to let a confidence model choose between them.

“Operations wants a standard epoch for collision work,” Varo said.

“They may have one.”

“Council members hear common epoch and think master time.”

“Then operations must explain the difference each time.”

A reference epoch let engineers compare positions. It did not decide which council had received evidence or when a reply became lawful. The distinction demanded extra fields, more training, and displays that admitted absence. Nacre's communications staff had already found six legacy systems that assumed every blank would eventually turn green under Meridian normalization.

Varo highlighted the first transfer conflict. “Juniper wants to launch seed stock for our arrival branch. Its local calendar gives the window as Harvest 12. Our navigation calendar places it on Day 4,903. Vela's epoch gives a fractional year nobody wants on a food label.”

“Carry all three.”

“That creates human error.”

“So does pretending one label belongs to everyone.”

“We need a conversion authority.”

“Yes. Not a consent authority.”

Varo saved that distinction in the operations draft.

Below the gallery, coolant pumps maintained their new reduced cycle. The radiator margin had recovered, though field corrections could spend it again. Osei opened the ship report. Fresh-food loss appeared first because people would feel it first. Shield exposure followed. Maintenance delays, settlement dates, family separation, and governance disputes occupied subsequent sections. The report did not conclude that sacrifice had united Nacre. It listed twenty-three petitions filed since the garden dimmed, then moved directly to the rule those disputes required.

Osei asked Anja to join her in the gallery.

The auditor arrived carrying a paper folder against her chest. Paper had multiplied aboard Nacre since the Parallax Archive went public. People trusted it for the wrong reasons, but it forced each amendment to occupy visible space.

“I need a rule that keeps this from becoming nine elegant ways to fail separately,” Osei said.

Anja opened the folder. “Coordination duties survive disagreement. Collision warnings, disease notices, receipt service, rescue capacity, and navigational hazards remain mandatory carriage.”

“Who compels them?”

“Each habitat compels its own officers under the Concord until a later federation revises it.”

“And if Vela calls itself the later federation?”

“It can call. The other ships can answer.”

Osei looked at the fan of trajectories. “Years apart.”

“Then the answer may take years.”

“That leaves emergency command exposed.”

“It does.”

Anja gave her the draft. The heading read PROVISIONAL RIGHT TO CAUSAL TIME. Beneath it, clauses separated operational prediction from legal testimony. No model output could substitute for receipt, deliberation, or observed reply. Emergency action without full quorum had to remain reversible, or else rest on the acting cohort's own authority and bind only its resources. Common reference epochs could coordinate physics without ranking local calendars. Mandatory carriage protected messages even when their content opposed the carrier's government.

A final clause addressed people who could not answer in the current interval: cycling residents, later generations, and ships whose councils had not yet received a change. Present authority could still act, because no government could wait for every future person. It had to name whose time it spent, preserve the record of dissent, and leave later councils the widest physically possible power to revise obligations.

“This does not solve representation across nineteen years,” Osei said.

“No.”

“It barely restrains us.”

“Restraint that claims completeness would repeat Meridian's error.”

Osei turned to the signature page. “Why mine?”

“Because Nacre needs provisional operating law before next shift. You have council authority to issue it for command departments, subject to review in seven local days.”

“Not fleet law.”

“Not until answers arrive from the other ships. Perhaps not then.”

“Vela will propose different language.”

“Likely.”

“Orison may reject any common doctrine.”

“It said as much.”

The draft therefore promised no painless constitutional birth. It gave Nacre a rule for tomorrow's packets and offered the rule outward without pretending acceptance.

Osei called the council back. Debate lasted three hours. Hale narrowed emergency action. Ilya strengthened mandatory carriage for food and ecological evidence. The cycling representative required every long-duration decision to name the absent cohort. Clinic counsel removed a phrase implying that delay alone excused preventable harm.

At 23:18 Nacre time, seven representatives approved the provisional text. One abstained because no cycling ballot could yet be observed.

The western ration room sent its closing inventory while Osei prepared to sign. Leaf bins stood empty. Grain stores remained adequate. Three arguments had required staff intervention. No one had missed a protected medical meal. Tomorrow's breakfast would carry fewer calories for most awake adults.

Osei attached the inventory to the constitutional packet.

She added Palisade's named shield lots, Ardent's pending panel receipt, the nineteen-year trajectory fan, Nacre's maintenance exposure, and the record of the abstention. The doctrine would travel beside its costs rather than above them.

Her thumb rested on the reader.

“Source clock visible?” she asked.

Varo checked the transmission pane. “Nacre time and comparison epoch. No predicted receipts.”

“Destinations?”

“All eight habitats. Mote as evidence observer, not voting cohort.”

“Meridian routing?”

“Carrier optimization only. Legal classification locked to local receipt.”

Osei signed the provisional Right to Causal Time.

Tightbeam emitters took the packet shipward in eight directions. Vela would see it first. Ardent would wait hours. No return field changed color, because nobody had answered yet.

Along the gallery wall, nine trajectory lines continued toward different years.

Chapter 27 — The Testament

Anja began the doctrine with three clocks and an empty field.

Nacre's source time occupied the left margin. Vela's comparison epoch occupied the right. Between them, she placed the receipt clock for Osei's provisional packet and left a space for every ship that had not yet answered. The old legal forms refused to save while required fields remained blank. Sumi altered the interface so absence could persist without error.

“Elegant,” Sumi said. “We repaired government by allowing null values.”

“We repaired one form.”

“Let me have the morning.”

The audit well had grown crowded since the brake began. Resource officers moved through the passage with shield claims. Food clerks arrived carrying ration appeals. Drive control transmitted corrections that needed legal classification before a local action could bind any other cohort. Every request came with its own urgency. None could make light move faster.

Anja titled the record THE PARALLAX TESTAMENT: PROVISIONAL PRACTICE AFTER CROWN.

She disliked the title at once. It sounded complete. The document described two days of practice aboard one habitat after a maneuver whose consequences would occupy nineteen years. She added a source note beneath it.

This record speaks from Nacre at the stated receipt times. It does not certify agreement beyond observed replies attached below.

Sumi read over her shoulder. “Yselle will call that an abdication.”

“Director Quon has not received it yet.”

“She will call it an abdication when she does.”

“Then her objection gets a clock.”

The first attached reply came from Juniper. Elian Ro accepted mandatory message carriage and the bar against forecast consent. His council rejected Nacre's claim that an acting habitat could create long obligations by spending only its own resources. Children born into scarcity would inherit those obligations without voting, he wrote. Juniper proposed a generational review whenever a ship crossed five local years without contact from an affected cohort.

Kite answered next. Its council wanted Meridian confidence models preserved in every operational display, arguing that hidden prediction caused the fraud, not prediction itself. It accepted visible confidence bands and rejected Nacre's preference for blanks. A blank, Kite said, could conceal danger as easily as a green mark concealed absence.

Palisade attached serial numbers. Its response accepted the Right to Causal Time only if material claims survived political succession. Shield reserve had left its hull through observed authority. Any federation that treated later councils as free of those debts would convert Palisade's sacrifice into another forecast about generosity.

No reply matched Nacre's text.

A clerk asked which version would appear on public terminals. Anja ordered every version available by source, with Nacre's provisional rule identified only as the local operating text. The clerk warned that most readers wanted one answer. Anja told him to give them an index instead.

Anja linked each answer without normalizing the differences. The document grew sideways. A reader could select a clause and see support, rejection, amendment, or silence by ship. Meridian calculated conversion between calendars. It never supplied a missing position.

At 10:42, Levan Oris entered the record from confinement aboard Vela.

His packet carried public observed testimony, the Annex Twelve drafting-note checksum, and a legal commentary stripped of the authority seal he once held. He did not ask for restoration. He traced the chain from reversible scheduling to forecast testimony, then named the administrative severance that let Crown treat a predicted reply as received consent.

I drafted language that made substitution possible and accepted assurances about its scope. My belief in a narrower use does not remove my work from the cause. No system may certify that a person has answered because a model expects accuracy. An exact prediction remains someone else's statement until the predicted person speaks.

Anja placed the testimony beside Pera's corrected sequence, not above it. Levan's confession explained the instrument. Pera's record showed a person the instrument had erased. Neither needed to serve as metaphor for the other.

The private line from Pera remained outside the public doctrine. Anja kept it in her family archive with its source proof and the words Pera had chosen for one recipient. Law did not gain force by consuming every intimate thing it touched.

Osei arrived near midday carrying a meal packet with the fresh portion removed.

“Confidence review convenes in two hours,” she said. “Hale thinks I should step aside before field correction three.”

“Will you?”

“If the council votes it.”

“Does field control agree?”

“Field control has prepared a transfer. Tomas hates the idea and has therefore documented it well.”

Osei placed the meal packet on Anja's desk. “Eat before you turn restraint into another form of vanity.”

Anja opened it. Grain paste, algae protein, preserved pepper, no leaf. Garden losses had reached the sequence office in a paper tray.

“What do you want from the testament?” she asked.

“A rule that tells the next captain where authority ends.”

“It cannot tell her what the emergency will be.”

“No. It can force her to name which people she spends and whether they can later refuse the debt.”

Anja entered the wording as Osei's proposed clause. The document labeled it proposed until Nacre's council could answer. Osei watched the status remain open and nodded.

“Better,” she said. “A constitution should make command slightly uncomfortable.”

The confidence review kept Osei by five votes to three and removed her unilateral authority over noncritical settlement allocations. Hale became chair of the seven-day resource committee. Osei transmitted the result to every habitat with her own dissent attached: command had carried too much authority during execution, but a standing committee could not answer a pressure breach. The dispute continued without becoming betrayal.

Tomas sent no legal prose. He attached the trajectory fan, shield expenditure, garden-light loss, ecological corridor, and every uncertainty that could widen arrival. A note at the bottom read: NO PATH RETURNS THESE RESOURCES. Anja classified the note as engineering testimony rather than doctrine.

Nadi's packet reached Nacre after ship evening. Mote had observed Thale through another partial ionic cycle. The active pellicle bands continued outside the distributed energy routes. Repair followed ordinary particle shadows. No burn scar appeared within the resolution of Mote's instruments. Nadi refused to certify survival for a global ecosystem from limited orbital data.

We have not proved safety. We have preserved the conditions under which further observation remains possible.

Anja attached that sentence to the ecological constraint. The pellicle lived. The claim stopped there.

Yselle Quon's answer arrived last among the near ships. Sumi put its raw receipt path on the board before opening the image.

The director appeared in the same Crown operations room where she had committed Vela's beamer. The old alignment line had vanished from the wall. Separate trajectory branches occupied its place.

Vela rejects any doctrine that makes delay a sufficient defense for failure to coordinate. We accept that forecast testimony cannot constitute consent. We retain common operational reference and propose a federal carriage authority limited to navigation, rescue, epidemic warning, and destination protection. Vela will not submit its cohort calendar to Nacre review.

Yselle paused, reading from something outside the frame.

My office authorized the classification that represented predicted replies as completed concurrence. The survival argument did not create consent. Record both facts.

She offered no resignation in the packet. Later packets would carry Vela's inquiry, its divided council, and the limits placed on her office. For now, Anja recorded what had arrived.

The testament held six different constitutional futures by midnight. Ardent and the far habitats remained blank. Osei's provisional law governed Nacre. Vela governed itself under amended emergency limits. Juniper tied resource promises to generational review. Palisade kept debt by named matter. Kite kept prediction visible. No phrase turned those disagreements into one polity.

Anja closed the working view without closing the record.

Nineteen years later, Caro's dawn lasted eleven hours and warmed nothing.

Anja stood inside a pressure crawler while regolith dust moved beneath its treads in slow gray folds. Corva filled half the forward window, banded in ocher and brown. Beyond its limb, Thale crossed as a white point too small to reveal ice, ocean, or living membrane. Human instruments watched it. No human settlement scarred its surface.

The crawler descended toward Installation Nacre Three, a trench roofed with local aggregate and retired hull plating. Antenna masts rose from the ridge. Their dishes pointed in different directions because the arriving habitats occupied widely separated approaches. A single mast could not face the federation.

Tomas sat at the drive console though the crawler needed no physicist. His hair had silvered at the temples, and one hand carried a tremor left by radiation exposure during Nacre's fourth brake correction. He had refused treatment priority until Hale's committee produced his own signed exposure schedule.

“You are staring at the roof line,” he said.

“It leans.”

“Caro leans. The roof follows survey vertical.”

“Whose survey?”

“Kite's. We converted it.”

“Then it leans in Nacre vertical.”

Tomas looked pleased. “You have been on the ground nine days and already developed planetary provincialism.”

“Moon provincialism.”

The crawler crossed a marker where three calendars had been cut into one post. Nacre Arrival 0. Kite Settlement 3. Juniper Harvest 7. A conversion plate below them gave the common reference epoch used for navigation. No date held legal priority. Work crews complained about the complexity and carried pocket converters. Twice in the first month, shipments had waited at the wrong lock because someone read a Juniper agricultural date as Nacre elapsed time.

Difference produced errors. One clock had produced erasures. The settlement lived inside that trade rather than pretending to escape it.

Installation Nacre Three housed six hundred people and parts for pressure halls not yet built. The earliest cohort from Kite had begun Caro's subsurface works eight years before Nacre arrived. Juniper followed with seed vaults and microbial soil cultures. Vela established a separate ridge station under its own civil charter. Nacre's descent added fabricators, archive staff, and a population tired of eating from gardens that had never fully regained their pre-brake light.

Palisade remained in approach, three years from its own settlement window. Ardent trailed farther under a correction chosen after the shield transfer. Lumen had altered its plan twice through observed council votes and would not commit to a landing date beyond a broad local season. The Ninefold Convoy had become nine arrival histories connected by messages, debt, kinship, and shared hazards.

Federation described the connection. It did not describe a state.

At the pressure lock, Osei waited in a wheeled chair beside two settlement workers. Age had narrowed her shoulders without softening her attention. She had retired as captain seven years earlier, returned to council service, lost an election, and accepted appointment to the rescue-carriage board after the winning coalition discovered that dissent required logistics.

“You are late,” she told Tomas.

“By Nacre surface time, we have four minutes.”

“Installation time says you lost six.”

Anja touched the conversion plate. “File a derogatory record.”

Osei smiled and rolled through the opening lock.

The first settlement work that morning involved neither flag nor monument. A pressure team had to seal a seam along the archive hall before the day's thermal contraction. Regolith aggregate met old Nacre hull plating at an angle Kite's survey had not anticipated. If the seam failed, the hall would lose air and four decades of raw source records would remain behind an emergency barrier.

Anja joined the inspection because the archive belonged to her department. A worker handed her a lamp and warned her not to touch the white sealant. The trench smelled of heated polymer and suit fabric. Beneath transparent floor panels, bundled optical lines carried transmissions from the approach habitats into separate receipt vaults.

One bundle bore Pera Kest's name.

Ardent's council had adopted the corrected six-hour record as the foundation for its emergency-window law. Years later, it named a carriage channel for every answer still traveling when an operational deadline closed. The Pera channel did not keep a window open automatically. It preserved the late answer, required review of any action taken without it, and barred officials from recording predicted assent in the gap.

Anja had opposed the name. Ardent had chosen it anyway under its own authority. She could not protect Pera from public memory by overruling the people who had shared her last emergency.

The archive hall opened after the seam passed pressure test. Shelves held no paper. Photonic vaults sat behind regolith shielding, each storing frame-specific accounts with original checksums and receipt paths. The Parallax Archive had grown too large for any single installation to hold in full. Caro carried Nacre, Kite, and Juniper mirrors. Vela kept another set on the ridge. Palisade transmitted rolling fragments during approach.

Levan Oris had designed the archive's legal cross-reference after Vela released him. He never returned to office. His later work tracked each clause of Annex Twelve through repeal, amendment, or surviving operational use. A short note in the entrance named him as compiler, not absolved witness. He had reached Vela Ridge two years before Nacre and now taught document order to apprentices who knew Crown only as a broken line in history.
