Yselle had not traveled down. At eighty, she remained aboard Vela in low gravity and served as an elected opposition delegate after losing executive authority. Her federal carriage proposal, revised twelve times by six habitats, governed hazard messages between the settlements and ships. She still argued that local councils romanticized delay. Anja still answered that Vela romanticized control. Decades of divergent braking had drawn Nacre and Vela closer than their old convoy separation. Their exchanges took twenty-eight minutes across the current geometry and entered the archive under separate clocks.

Nadi directed Thale observation from Mote's successor station. No settlement existed on the ice moon. The federation maintained an exclusion corridor around its magnetosphere and reviewed every orbital instrument for particle loading. Nadi's latest map showed active ion-band migration beneath the northern shell, metabolism continuing through patterns no early model had predicted. She still declined the word intelligence.

Tomas left the archive workers to their seam and led Anja toward the surface council chamber.

They crossed a tunnel lined with shield panels whose serial marks had been sanded but not erased. Two came from Nacre. Four had reached Caro from Lumen. None matched the lots Palisade had sacrificed. Replacement for those remained an active claim against all arrived settlements.

“Fabricator allocation again?” Anja asked.

“Palisade needs confirmation before it enters the high-dust approach. The ridge councils want to hold two printers for habitat expansion.”

“Has Palisade received the revised allocation?”

“Not yet.”

“Then there can be no answer.”

“That has not stopped projections.”

The council chamber contained sixteen seats assigned by installation rather than ship population. Vela objected to equal installation votes. Juniper objected to population weighting that rewarded longer wake schedules. Nacre favored mixed representation. Orison participated only in hazard and resource sessions. No constitutional convention had settled the dispute.

Today's council held authority to allocate printers owned jointly by the three Caro installations. It could not bind Palisade to an approach maneuver. The practical temptation remained obvious: reserve the machines now based on what Palisade would probably request, then call the reservation coordination.

A young councilor named Tava Meren displayed Meridian's confidence model. Software predicted Palisade would accept one printer slot, provided the settlements guaranteed outer-hull shelter until replacement shield lots came online. Confidence stood at 0.982.

Anja sat in the witness chair. She no longer served as chief auditor, but the council had asked her to verify the receipt order.

“Has Palisade received our shelter guarantee?” the chair asked.

“Transmission entered its past light cone eleven minutes ago by estimated path,” Anja said. “Verified receipt has not come back.”

“When can an observed reply arrive?”

“Forty-six minutes at minimum. Council deliberation will add whatever time Palisade uses.”

Tava gestured toward the construction schedule. “Holding both printers costs a work shift. The model has missed none of Palisade's material votes in nine years.”

“Use it to prepare branches,” Anja said.

“We have prepared them. One branch releases a printer now.”

“Then releasing it chooses before Palisade answers.”

“They are almost certain to choose the other branch.”

“Almost describes our knowledge. It does not describe their act.”

Nobody in the chamber needed the history explained. The old Crown record hung in the archive below their feet. Yet current scarcity made old temptations reasonable again. A pressure-hall crew waited for equipment. Families remained in temporary cells. Palisade approached with thin shielding and a debt the settlements had promised to honor.

The chair asked operations what delay spent.

“One hundred eighty worker-hours,” Tomas said from the technical table. “Possible loss of the southern trench window if the thermal forecast worsens. No immediate life threat. Palisade's approach margin remains unchanged until it sends its maneuver choice.”

Vela's delegate proposed conditional release with automatic recall. Juniper's delegate noted that hauling a printer back would consume the same shift they claimed to save. Nacre moved to hold both machines until receipt. Kite requested that Meridian's projection remain visible beside the empty reply field.

The motion passed by installation authority. Vela entered dissent. Orison abstained outside its limited compact. The chair signed the hold and displayed Palisade's field without color.

No one called the blank safe. Workers would lose hours. The southern trench might wait through another long Caro night. If Palisade rejected the shelter terms, every prepared branch would change.

Above the chamber, tracking motors turned an antenna toward the approaching habitat. Its last image showed a bright speck wrapped in incomplete armor, three years from ground and twenty-three light-minutes from the council that discussed its debt.

The predicted answer remained beside the field, useful and unpromoted.

Anja watched the receipt clock advance. Around her, delegates reopened the construction schedule and assigned work that could proceed without the printer. Tomas shifted crews to sealant production. Osei asked for a rescue-board review of Palisade's dust corridor. The federation continued through incomplete knowledge.

Forty-six minutes passed. Then fifty.

The chair refused a motion to query the model again. They waited for the answer.

On the council wall, Palisade's field remained open while Caro turned beneath the stars.
